# Compiler behavior specification

**Frozen on 19 October 2026.** Guardian's `spec` and `spec-quality` checks were turned off
then, and Guardian itself on 18 November 2026. A change no longer needs a bullet here, and a
test no longer needs a `// spec:` tag. A behavioural change under `src/` still needs a test
change in the same diff, as a convention that nothing checks. The bullets and tags below stay as a record of
the behaviour they described on that date. They are not kept current.

This document records implemented behavior with executable Zig regression evidence.
Each stable ID has one primary `// spec:` test. Additional tests and Python
acceptance drivers exercise further cases; these links are not a claim of exhaustive
coverage or proof of memory safety. The language remains experimental. Native
code is the only runtime: the compiler core (`src/core/`) lowers every checked program to LLVM, so every
behavior below is native behavior.
The roadmap in `docs/experiments/roadmap.md` tracks unsupported and planned features.

History, 2026-10-17: reference counting, copy-on-write, the hybrid ownership selector and list `.snapshot()` were retired. Their implementation-only contracts were removed: `NEW-CORE-SINGLE-OWNER-3`, `NEW-CORE-SINGLE-OWNER-4`, `NEW-CORE-RC-1`, `NEW-CORE-RC-4`, `NEW-CORE-RC-5`, `NEW-CORE-RC-6`, `NEW-CORE-RC-8`, `NEW-CORE-RC-9`, `NEW-CORE-RC-10`, `NEW-CORE-RC-13`, `SHARED-COUNTS-1`, `SHARED-COUNTS-2`, `NEW-CORE-TEXT-BORROW-1`, `NEW-CORE-ROW-COW-1`, `NEW-CORE-ROW-COW-2`. Stable IDs on surviving destruction, lifetime and writeback rules retain their historical names; their text specifies checked ownership.

## Core semantics

- [OWNERSHIP-RETIREMENT-CLI-1] Plain check, run, build and test use checked ownership. The retired `--experimental-zero-rc` and `--experimental-single-owner` selectors fail with exit status 2 and a diagnostic saying reference counting was removed. Command arguments and guest arguments after `--` retain their meaning, and JSON diagnostics remain valid JSON lines.
- [OWNERSHIP-DEFAULT-NOALLOC-1] A violated `@noalloc` promise reports E_NOALLOC_ALLOCATES as an error at its ownership-expanded Core allocation witness, with the source promise as a related location. Build, run and test fail before execution; plain check does not compute this Core allocation contract.
- [OWNERSHIP-RETIREMENT-1] Checked ownership is the sole memory model. Core contains direct destruction only, runtime blocks have no reference count, and no RC, hybrid, list `.snapshot()` or RC instrumentation path remains.

- [NEW-CORE-SINGLE-OWNER-1] Checked ownership validates typed bodies before optimization, with successful per-function proof reuse. Owned values move through assignments, construction and returns; copyable managed ordinary arguments use nonescaping readonly call-scoped borrows; `mut` arguments lend writable caller storage, while affine arguments follow ownership-transfer rules. Use after move, borrowed escape, overlapping mutation and moves unbalanced across loop iterations are located errors. Unsupported shapes are refused conservatively.
- [SINGLE-OWNER-DIAG-1] An ownership refusal names the source of a borrow when the moved value is not that source: a `match` payload moved while its scrutinee borrows it reports "`detail` borrows from `failure`; copy it with .copy() or finish the borrow first", and an element read bound to an owning local names the destination, as in "bind `row` with .copy() or use pop()". Other overlaps retain the general argument-or-iteration wording, and every refusal still carries the same code, span and copy repair.
- [SINGLE-OWNER-DIAG-2] A loop refusal names the binding that became unavailable and adds a related location for the move that moved it, as in "`xs` is moved inside a loop and is unavailable at the next iteration or loop exit" with "`xs` is moved here".
- [SINGLE-OWNER-DIAG-3] A use-after-move refusal links the earlier move that consumed the binding: the primary message says the value "was moved or is unavailable on this path", and a related location says it "is moved here", so a `.copy()` at the later use can be placed at the consuming expression.
- [SINGLE-OWNER-DIAG-4] An overlap refusal for a value moved or mutated inside the arm of a `match` on that named value says so: the primary message names the value as the one a running `match` borrows and gives both repairs (`.copy()`, or match on the producing call so the payload transfers), and a related location marks the matched value, instead of the argument-and-iteration wording; an overlap between two arguments that name fields of one record local says that the fields of one record count as one value and names the way out (pass the record `mut` and use both fields in the callee).
- [NEW-CORE-SINGLE-OWNER-2] List `.copy()` explicitly duplicates nested owned storage. The sole owner may update its storage directly, and cleanup follows the element type through lists, records, fixed arrays and immutable leaves. Checked lifetime and lowering guards reject invalid ownership; there is no runtime uniqueness test or shared-value fallback.
- [OWNED-MATCH-IO-1] A `match` on a fresh file-builtin result (`fs.read_file`, `fs.read_text`, `fs.listdir`, a file method) transfers the matched payload exactly as a match on a fresh call result does: the arm may return it, wrap it as `Some(...)` or `T?`, return it from a fallible function or `fail` with an `Err` payload, with no `.copy()` and no leak or double release on either arm.
- [NEW-CORE-FRAME-FLOOR-1] A built binary carries no frame budget; recursion that fills its native stack (the main thread's `ulimit -s`, 8 MiB by default) ends with `R_FRAME_LIMIT`, a trace and status 1, and the message names the native stack and the remedy a binary can take, `ulimit -s KBYTES` before starting it, or running under `selka run --max-frames=N`, instead of advising `--max-frames` alone; the budgeted traps of `selka run` and `selka test` keep their `raise it with --max-frames=N` advice. A larger stack lets the same binary finish.
- [BUILD-OUTPUT-DIR-1] `selka build -o PATH` creates PATH's missing parent directories, at any depth, before it publishes the program, as `go build -o` does; a parent that cannot be created (a regular file in the way, no permission) fails with `error: cannot write PATH: <cause>`, exit 2.
- [FILE-ERROR-REASON-1] A refused file operation names what refused it: when the operating system or the restricted root refused (a directory or FIFO read as a file, `is a directory` and `is not a regular file`, a file used as a parent directory, a permission or symbolic-link refusal) the error text carries that reason, as in `read 'adir': is a directory` and `read 'f': permission denied`, and a grant-policy refusal says which of three it is (`denied: 'RESOLVED' is outside the granted directories`, `denied: writing needs a write grant (--allow-write); ...`, or the narrowed handle's own text); a missing path says `the path does not exist` (`a folder on the way does not exist` for a call that creates below a folder that is not there). The system's reason is never replaced by a generic sentence: one table from `errno` to a plain sentence (`no space left on the device`, `disk quota exceeded`, `the file is too large for the size limit or for the filesystem`, `the filesystem is read-only`, `permission denied`, `too many files are open`, `the path, or a name in it, is too long`, `input/output error`, `is a directory`; an error outside the table gives its number and the system's words) serves the durable calls, the whole-file calls, the methods of an open file and `filesystem.memory()` alike, and the `R_FILE` trap of a failed method of an open file reads `error[R_FILE]: VERB 'PATH': REASON`.
- [COLLECTION-METHOD-HELP-1] `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` for a method the receiver lacks says which receiver lacks it and what it accepts: a method that exists for another collection family but not this one (`b"AbC".lower()`) names the receiver, the method and that family's accepted methods, and any method on a checked `Result` says a `Result` has no methods and names `match` and `try`, instead of listing every collection's methods.
- [STDLIB-COUNT-1] The list of standard modules is one table (`standard_library.paths`, `standard_library.count`): the reference engine's analysis, its project module limit and the tests read it rather than repeating a list or a number, every module has a heading in the standard library guide, and every prose count of the modules ("twenty-three modules ship", "twenty-three embedded modules") in the guides and in `src/features.json` equals the table's count, so adding a module to the table cannot leave a stale number behind.
- [STRINGS-DECIMAL-1] `strings.format_decimal(value, decimals)` renders a float with `decimals` (0 to 17) fractional digits by rounding the shortest round-trip digits that `str(value)` prints half up at the Nth decimal, as Zig's `{d:.N}` does: `format_decimal(6.475, 2)` is `"6.48"` where `format_float` (the exact binary value) answers `"6.47"`; a carry grows the integer part (`9.995` is `"10.00"`), missing decimals pad with zeros, the sign is kept on a result that rounds to zero (`"-0.00"`), `inf`, `-inf` and `nan` print as such, and a decimal count outside 0..17 is `Err(Invalid)`. It matches the kicad-convert `floatfmt` oracle on 320,000 generated values.
- [CORE-ARRAY-FROM-BYTES-1] A byte-result read (`read_resource[network.Read[N]]`, an `Environment` value) fills the inline `u8` array of its result from the runtime's byte list with one bulk copy (`array_from_bytes`) once that array is held in memory, so the emitted code and the build time do not depend on N: a 65536-byte chunk emits under 256 KiB of LLVM (it was about 20 MB and over a minute to build) and every octet read is the runtime's. A small array stays in registers and is filled element by element.
- [BYTE-BUFFER-APPEND-ARRAY-1] `byte_buffer.append_array(array, start, end)` appends the octets `array[start:end]` of a `FixedArray[u8, N]` of any length with the checks of `append_range`: a negative endpoint traps (`R_LIST_BOUNDS`), a range that is not `start <= end <= N` appends nothing and answers `False`, an empty range succeeds, and an array that is not a fixed `u8` array or a receiver that is not a `byte_buffer` is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. An array held in memory crosses to the buffer's capacity in one copy from its own address (a 64 KiB `network.Read` chunk adds one runtime call and no per-octet code); a register-sized array is spilled into a short `bytes` first. The method works in a generic body over a symbolic length, which `byte_builder.append_array(mut builder, array, count)` (the first `count` octets) and `byte_builder.append_array_range(mut builder, array, start, end)` use, and `network.recv` uses it for the octets it receives.

- [BYTE-LITERAL-1] Quoted byte literals produce ordinary fixed `[N]u8` arrays without terminators. UTF-8 source contributes its encoded bytes; escapes are `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\0`, `\\`, `\"` and exactly two hexadecimal digits after `\x`. Empty literals have length zero; each literal is limited to 65,536 decoded bytes (`E_BYTE_LITERAL_LIMIT`). Checking, generic bodies, module relocation, cached syntax and formatting preserve ordinary array identity and behavior. Invalid escapes and unterminated literals are located source errors.

- [CORE-1] Evaluates multiplication before addition, honors parentheses, and associates subtraction from the left.
- [CORE-2] Reports checked arithmetic overflow at the responsible operator without evaluating arithmetic during checking.
- [CORE-3] Returns located source diagnostics for empty input, malformed syntax, and out-of-range integer literals.
- [CORE-4] Accepts the signed and unsigned integer width limits and rejects literals above each maximum.
- [CORE-5] Constructs records by field name and preserves original values when producing updated record copies.
- [RECORD-DEFAULT-1] A record field may declare an immutable scalar default after `=`; a constructor call may omit that field and the default is materialized, positional and named construction stay in declaration order, and a required field after a defaulted one rejects like a function parameter. Enum-keyed tables keep requiring every key.
- [RECORD-UPDATE-1] `replace(record, field=value, …)` returns a copy of the record with only the named fields changed and every other field preserved; the base evaluates before the values, the result is a fresh record value, and the positional `replace(list, index, value)` intrinsic is unchanged.
- [RECURSIVE-VALUES-1] A `record` or `union` may hold itself by value, directly, mutually, or through `Option`, fixed arrays, generic instances and other declarations, when the recursive type has a finite value: a union or `Option` case, or an empty fixed array, that ends every cycle. A cycle every value of which holds another value of it (`record Loop: next: Loop`) still rejects with `E_RECURSIVE_RECORD` at the field that closes it. A recursive value type cannot hold an owned resource, an unhandled result or a capability; each is `E_RECURSIVE_RECORD`. Recursion through `list`, `dict`, `set` and `class` needs no box and is unchanged.
- [RECURSIVE-VALUES-3] Equality, dictionary and set hashing and key comparison walk a recursive value with growable work lists, so a value of any depth (a 1,000,000-node chain) compares and hashes without host recursion; equality compares each pair of boxes once, and list equality has no element-count limit. Printing a recursive value stops at the renderer's depth cap with `...`. The test boundary carries a recursive value as its typed JSON, and property tests generate recursive unions whose nesting grows with the run's size and shrink them toward their leaves.
- [EQUALITY-SCRATCH-1] Native equality compares scalar values, text, bytes and small structural values without scratch allocations. Larger worklists and recursive visited-pair tables grow without a fixed depth or size limit; exhausted scratch allocation returns failure after releasing earlier buffers. Float equality keeps IEEE NaN and signed-zero behavior even inside a nested box or collection.
- [CORE-6] Preserves fixed-array copies and accepts each integer index width, including zero-length arrays.
- [CORE-7] Historical scalar-domain construction contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1: distinct nominal domains were not interchangeable and required their declared construction operations. Current replacements use nominal records and ordinary free construction functions; record declaration identity remains governed by NOMINAL-INSTANCE-1.
- [DOMAIN-EQUALITY-1] Historical scalar-domain equality contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1: equality required the same declaration identity across aliases, compared the integer or Boolean backing values without exposing them, and permitted no implicit arithmetic or ordering. Current replacement records use ordinary field equality and retain nominal declaration identity; equality evaluates operands left to right and preserves cleanup and execution limits.
- [CORE-8] Rejects nonexhaustive matches after a variant is added, including matches on statically known values.
- [CORE-9] Uses enum case names for table construction and indexing regardless of declaration or initializer order.
- [CORE-10] Rejects silent result abandonment and permits `_ = value` to release the result obligation explicitly. A diagnostic about a binding that cannot be assigned or modified names that rule rather than a declaration keyword.
- [CORE-11] Unwraps successful results and propagates compatible error payloads by case name across nominal result types. Native propagation constructs the outgoing result before releasing unfinished operands; failed construction releases the propagated payload before those operands.
- [TRY-CONVERT-1] `?` propagating a result whose error type is the payload of a case of the enclosing function's error union converts implicitly: the error value is wrapped in that case, the outgoing tag is the case's tag, and a payload type that is not a case of the enclosing union still rejects with a located type error.
- [ERROR-TRACE-1] Every build (a debug build, a `--release` build, `selka run`, `selka test`) keeps one process-wide error return trace. Constructing an `Err(...)` value restarts it with that construction as its origin, and a runtime operation (a file, resource or boundary call) that answers `Err` restarts it at the call. A `?` that propagates an `Err` appends its site, marked as converted to `Union.case` when it wraps the error in a case of the caller's error union; a `return` in a function returning a `Result` appends its site when the returned value is an `Err` the statement did not itself construct (a variable or a call) — a `return Err(...)` is already the origin and a `return Ok(...)` is never checked. Handling an `Err` (`match`, `unwrap_or`, discarding it) appends nothing. Each site is the recording function's metadata and the instruction's position, from which the report reads the module path, line, column, function name and case. Recording sits on paths only an `Err` takes, so a successful `?` and a `return Ok(...)` record nothing.
- [ERROR-TRACE-2] When `main` returns an `Err`, the entry wrapper writes `error: VALUE` to standard error, `VALUE` rendered as `print` renders it, followed by one `  path:line:column  function  action` line per trace site, the origin first and the outermost last, with the columns aligned; the actions read `Err(...) created`, `Err produced by the runtime`, `returned`, `? propagated` and `? converted to Union.case`. A `returned` site on the same line of the same function as the site listed before it is folded into that line, so `return Err(...)` in a function with `@ensures` (which holds the value before returning it) or `return Ok(v) if c else Err(e)` reads as one line. The exit status is unchanged: an `int` error is the status and a `str` error exits 1. A trap is reported as before, without a trace.
- [ERROR-TRACE-3] `selka test` reports a test whose value is an `Err` with that test's error return trace after the expected/actual line of its failure, in the text summary and in the `--json` report's `reason`, with the snapshot directory rewritten to the project root like any other failure text. A parameter-free test returning `Result[bool, E]` expects `Ok(True)` without a cases file, so a failure it propagates with `?` fails the test with its trace; any other `Result` still needs an explicit `expected`.
- [ERROR-TRACE-4] The trace keeps the origin and the last 32 appended sites. When more were appended, the report lists the origin, then one `... N more entries dropped ...` line naming how many were overwritten, then the 32 survivors in order.
## Ownership and capabilities

- [OWN-9] Releases previously acquired resources exactly once when a later acquisition propagates an error.
## Projects and native output




- [LIST-GROWTH-1] List growth preserves the live elements and their order while providing capacity for another element. A static empty list receives fresh storage for at least eight elements with the same element layout; its static header is neither modified nor freed. Small blocks grow by allocating storage, moving the live element bits with their length and layout, and releasing the old backing without destroying its children. Large blocks use realloc and repair alignment by copying the header and live element bits when necessary. The caller adopts the returned owner because growth may move storage. Checked ownership prevents addresses into the old backing from remaining usable across consuming growth; transferring the backing does not duplicate or destroy its child owners. Capacity growth and byte-size arithmetic are checked for overflow, and allocation failure follows the runtime out-of-memory path.
- [DICT-GETORPUT-1] A string-keyed read-modify-write can reuse a dominating compiler probe when the dictionary and key SSA values are unchanged, as specified by PY-DICT-4. Runtime reads keep no mutable pointer or last-probe hint. Consuming updates receive their probe explicitly, and a rebuilt index is probed again before insertion. Equal strings find the same equal key, defaults retain their ordinary meaning, and insertion, removal or clear cannot make a stale absence hide a current entry.
- [DICT-TOMBSTONES-1] A dictionary that holds eight entries or fewer has no index, so every lookup scans its dense entries; it reclaims the entries it has deleted when an insert finds them filling the table or numbering sixteen dense entries in all. A pop and put-back cycle (a connection table, a session table, a cache with eviction) therefore costs the same on the millionth round as on the first, the capacity does not double with the number of deletions, and every live entry keeps its value and its place in insertion order. A dictionary with an index already counts its tombstones against its half occupancy.
- [BUFFER-GROWTH-1] Growing a byte buffer preserves its existing length and octets and provides capacity for the requested length. Growth starts at at least eight octets and doubles until sufficient, with checked arithmetic near the size limit. Small blocks grow by allocating a new block, copying the used octets and releasing the old block. Large blocks use realloc; if its result is not 32-byte aligned, the header and used octets are copied to a fresh aligned block. The caller adopts the returned owner because growth may move storage. Neither an in-place result nor copy-free remapping is guaranteed. Unused capacity is not part of the buffer's value.
- [UTF8-TEXT-1] A `str` stores validated UTF-8 octets with optional Unicode scalar-count metadata and an "every scalar is below 0x80" flag. Length uses a cached count when available, while equality checks length and octets. Every boundary that already holds UTF-8 — `from_utf8`, `utf8`, `bytes.encode`, a file path, a print sink — copies octets instead of decoding and re-encoding scalars. Scalar indexing, slicing, a stepped slice and `ord` keep Python's scalar semantics by computing byte offsets on demand: a scalar index is a byte index on ASCII text and one payload walk otherwise, and the same walk turns a byte offset back into a scalar index for `find`. Where scalars were compared, octets are: equality is a byte compare, order is UTF-8 byte order (which is code point order), `find`/`count`/`split`/`replace`/`splitlines`/`split()` scan octets because an ASCII scalar is its own octet and no octet of a non-ASCII scalar is below 0x80, case mapping and the three classifications stay ASCII-only, and rendering a nested text escapes per octet. A stepped slice gathers scalars, so it walks the payload once and copies the selected scalars' own octets rather than reading a stride of it; an explicit text copy creates independent owned octets. A whole-path file helper borrows a payload only for the write helpers that consume one, because `open`'s third operand is its mode spelling and the request's text bit describes the handle that mode names, not that operand. Immutability, UTF-8 validity, the `R_TEXT_SCALAR`, `R_TEXT_BOUNDS` and `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR` traps, `dict[str, ...]` keys and every produced value are unchanged, and a text built from scalars outside `0..0x10FFFF` or inside the surrogate range is refused rather than stored. Equal UTF-8 octets have equal deterministic hashes. A text hash query reuses an existing cached hash; before worker-pool startup it may fill the cache of dynamic owned text. After startup it computes a missing hash without writing into the text block.
- [RELEASE-CHECKED-1] No undefined behaviour in safe code, and optimization never changes a program's result: every build mode (Debug and ReleaseFast, `--release`), `selka run`, `run-project`, `build --run` and `selka test` emit the integer-overflow guards on add, subtract, multiply and unary negate, the narrowing integer-cast guards, the index-bounds guards on lists, fixed arrays, `bytes`, `str`, dictionary position reads and `pop`, and the frame protocol that reports a trap's trace, so a would-be trap is always reported, never undefined behaviour. A guard is omitted only where the compiler has proved it cannot fire (a range-fitting cast, a versioned loop's proved accesses and arithmetic). `--unchecked`, which once omitted them, is refused as removed: on `build` (with or without `--run`), `run`, `run-project` and `test` it is a usage error (exit 2) whose message says `--unchecked was removed: every build keeps overflow, cast and bounds checks so optimization never changes a program's result`, while the same spelling after `--` still reaches the guest.
- [FLOAT-NAN-OBSERVED-2] Text observes no NaN payload: `str`, `stream.print`, f-string interpolation, container rendering and `byte_buffer.append_float` spell every NaN `nan`, whatever its sign or payload, never `-nan`.
- [FLOAT-NAN-OBSERVED-3] A `float` argument crosses into an `extern def` as the canonical quiet NaN when it is any NaN: the generated C ABI wrapper selects the canonical word with straight-line mask arithmetic before the call, so foreign code that reads a double's sign or payload (`copysign`, `signbit`, a byte copy) sees `0x7ff8000000000000`. A NaN a foreign function returns enters the guest unchanged, like any produced NaN.
- [FLOAT-NAN-OBSERVED-4] Equality never compares a float's bits: `==`, `!=` and `in` over lists, records, fixed arrays, options and unions holding floats compare each float numerically, so two NaNs are unequal whatever their payloads and signs, and positive and negative zero are equal.
- [FLOAT-NAN-OBSERVED-5] Sorting observes no NaN payload: `list.sort()` and `sorted` order every NaN after the ordered floats, keep NaNs of any sign or payload in their input order among themselves, and print them as `nan`.
- [BUILD-REPRODUCIBLE-1] The C units a build compiles (the metadata, entry and format units) are compiled with their work directory mapped to a fixed name (`-ffile-prefix-map`, `-fdebug-prefix-map`) and UBSan's source paths stripped, so a Debug executable is the same bytes whichever cache directory and temporary job directory built it; the object key version is `selka-object-key-v4`, so no object that still names such a path is reused.
- [NATIVE-ZIG-CACHE-2] End to end, `selka build` and `selka test` (with a fresh cache, so the runner harness is compiled) run from a directory that holds a `build.zig`, with no `ZIG_LOCAL_CACHE_DIR` in their environment, succeed and create no `.zig-cache` in that directory.
- [NATIVE-ZIG-CACHE-4] Every command `zig build` runs through its tool wrapper — the Zig test roots and their shards, and the Python and guest-test drivers — runs with `ZIG_LOCAL_CACHE_DIR` naming a fresh directory under `$TMPDIR` (else `/tmp`) made for that one command, replacing any value `zig build` inherited, and the directory is removed once the command's process has exited, however it ended; the command itself still replaces the wrapper, so it remains the build runner's direct child. A test or driver that runs `zig cc` itself from a scratch directory inside the checkout (`.zig-cache/tmp`, or a `$TMPDIR` there) therefore leaves no object in the project's `.zig-cache`, where Zig would otherwise have kept it under a key naming that scratch path. A test that needs the variable unset removes it from its child's environment.
- [ENV-PROJECTION-1] Every command `zig build` runs through its tool wrapper (the Zig test roots and their shards, and the Python and guest-test drivers) runs through `build-env project`, which replaces itself with the command and passes only the variables the environment identity contract lets a child receive: the keyed, host and placement classes of `src/environment_identity.zon`. A dropped variable such as `CODEX_THREAD_ID`, `TERM` or `PWD` is therefore absent in every test step and absent from the environment manifest, so changing it leaves every cached verdict cached; a keyed or host variable such as `SELKA_MEMORY_LIMIT` or `PYTHONPATH` is present in both, and a placement variable such as `PATH` or `TMPDIR` reaches the step but not the manifest. The command keeps the wrapper's pid, exit status and resource accounting, and `build-env` exits with status 2 and a one-line message when the command cannot be executed.
- [ENV-PROJECTION-2] The environment manifest every cached test verdict is keyed on is written by `build-env digest OUT TOOL...`: its first line is the host-scope environment identity digest (`environment_identity.digest(.host, …)`, ENV-IDENTITY-2) of the live environment and the named tools, followed by the keyed and host `NAME=VALUE` lines (a backslash and a line feed escaped as `\\` and `\n`) and one `tool:NAME=SHA256` line per tool, each in bytewise name order. The build names its configured Zig by path and `sh`, `env`, `python3` and `git` by name, resolved on the live `PATH` when the step runs; a tool that cannot be found or read is recorded as `absent`.
- [TEST-PATHS-1] The unit, core and CLI test binaries carry no absolute path of the tree or the build cache they were built in outside their debug sections. Fixture paths are relative to the build root (`test/core`, `tests/lang`, `benchmarks/runtime/ports/native_clock.c`), which is the working directory of every test step; the compiler arrives at run time through the placement variable `SELKA_TEST_COMPILER` and, for the CLI root, the component builder through `SELKA_TEST_COMPONENT_BUILDER`, both set by the tool wrapper from the artifacts' paths. The step keys each executable by its content, so changed inputs re-run a cached verdict without rebuilding the test binary. The number-text object embedded in the compiler and the core test binary is built without debug information for the same reason.
- [CORE-SUITE-CACHE-1] Core tests that do not assert cache behavior share, within their test file, the content-addressed build cache selected by `-Dcore-test-cache` (default `.selka-cache/core-tests`, passed at run time through the placement variable `SELKA_CORE_TEST_CACHE`): the helper takes the caller's `@src()` and names a subdirectory by the file's stem, because a build's stage stores index every record of their directory and one directory for all tests made each build pay for the others' records. The helper resolves it relative to the build root before a child changes working directory. Temporary sources, LLVM dumps, linked output paths and guest side-effect files remain private to each test. Converted guest test invocations use `--force` so warm receipts cannot replace execution or change expected passing counts. Cache-behavior and test-runner execution/receipt assertions retain private caches. All shards use the same suite cache directories without embedding an absolute worktree path in their test binary. The tests that link a program from emitted LLVM in process (`test_support.runLlvm`, the unit-emission, pipeline-emission and task tests) use their file's suite directory as the work directory of that link too, so a later run of the same unit text, whatever the compiler that emitted it, finds the program in the store (`programs-v1`, keyed by the objects it links) instead of linking again; the executable each test runs is a copy in its own temporary directory.
- [NATIVE-FIXTURE-CACHE-1] A Zig core test that compiles a closed C program with `zig cc` (the compiler's runtime text and a source that includes no file by quoted name except that text) reuses the binary of an earlier identical compile, whoever made it and wherever the compile happened. The key binds the runtime and source texts, the file names they are written under, the compiler flags, the sanitizer, the guest toolchain's identity and native target and the source-date epoch, never the temporary root. A stored binary is restored only when its recorded digest matches and every header the compile read (its depfile) still has the recorded bytes, and the toolchain is unchanged after the restore. Only the construction is reused: the program runs fresh in each test. A source that includes any other file, or a toolchain that is not approved, compiles privately.
- [CORE-SUITE-RUNTIME-SEED-1] A core test that keeps its own `SELKA_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE` so that its unit and program counts are exact, and that asserts nothing about the cold compile of the runtime, starts that store with the runtime object a build there would have compiled. The object comes from a build of a one-line program by the compiler under test, run with the compiler's shared stores off in a store the suite shares and names by the runtime text, the layout and the sanitizer setting; only the `core_rt-*.o` objects are copied, so the test's unit objects, programs and records stay its own. Tests whose subject is that compile (the layout, sanitizer and cold-build tests of `driver_cache_test`) keep an empty store.
- [CORE-STABLE-DIR-1] A core test that builds a single-file program from a temporary directory into the suite cache, and so asserts nothing about cold caches, keeps its sources in a stable directory instead (`stable_dir.tmpDir(@src())`, with `std.testing.TmpDir`'s `dir` and `cleanup`): `stable-LINE-HASH` in the file's suite directory, named by the call site, emptied when opened and kept after the test for inspection. The LLVM of a single-file program carries its source path, so the same text at the same path finds its unit objects and its linked program in the stores where a random path compiled and linked every run; the compiler under test still checks, lowers and emits the program each time. A directory name found in use (two runs of one tree at once, held by an exclusive lock on `NAME.lock`) is replaced by a unique one that is removed afterwards, which loses only the reuse. Tests that give a build a cache directory of their own, or whose subject is a cold build, keep random directories.
- [CORE-SHARD-ORDER-1] The order of the imports in `src/core/root_test.zig` is the shard assignment of the core test root when the runner has no shard plan: the runner gives the named test at position P of the binary to shard P mod N, and the binary lists tests in import order. The files that hold a slow test come first, in an order found by a search for the busiest shard over measured per-test times (`experiments/project-ladder-20261018/test-core-tools/order_imports.py`), and the many fast files follow, so that a test added to a fast file moves only the fast tests after it. Reordering imports changes which tests share a shard and never which tests run: the import set is unchanged, and every shard still runs its tests exactly once.
- [QBE-TRAP-DETAIL-1] Native traps carry a bounded, versioned structured detail captured from values the failing operation already evaluated: original index and receiver length for bounds failures (negative indexes preserved), and evaluated operands/width for division and checked overflow. Text and structured JSON render from the same detail; the JSON path does not reparse text. Native children emit one bounded `\x1eSELKA-TRAP ` + JSON record on stderr while ordinary stdout, diagnostics, exit statuses, trap codes, locations, call stacks, omitted-frame counts and first-trap stickiness are preserved, and budget/host failures remain detail-free.
- [TRAP-UNSIGNED-1] An arithmetic trap's operands are quoted in the operation's own signedness: an unsigned overflow (`add`, `sub`, `mul`, a range-checked narrow width), an unsigned division by zero, a cast from an unsigned source and an unsigned negation render `left`/`right` as unsigned decimals (`(add: left 18446744073709551614, right 5, width 64)` where it used to read `left -2`), in the human diagnostic, in the `\x1eSELKA-TRAP` record (which adds `"unsigned":true`) and in `--json`'s `trap_detail`. The new core marks unsigned operands by OR-ing 0x100 into the trap site's width (overflow, division, cast), and the runtime quotes them unsigned; a signed operation's site and text are unchanged.

- [USE-WITHOUT-PYTHON-1] Using the installed compiler needs no Python or any other interpreter: `check`, `build`, the executable it writes, `run`, `test`, `fmt-project --check`, `docs`, `index-project`, `context`, and the LLVM release path — `build --release`, the executable it writes and `run --release` (LLVM-ZIG-1) — all succeed on a two-module project in an environment whose `PATH` names only an empty directory, given nothing but `--zig` and `--qbe` (only `--zig` for the release path).
- [PACKAGES-LOCK-1] `selka packages lock CONFIG --output LOCK` reads `{"packages":[{"name":...,"root":...}]}`, requires every name to be a unique ASCII identifier outside the reserved `std_` prefix, resolves each root against the configuration's directory, inventories every `.selka` source under it (at most 50,000 walked entries and 16 MiB, refusing a symbolic-link source or directory and any source outside `name.selka` and `name/`), and writes a `package-lock-v1` file naming each resolved absolute root and each module's SHA-256; an invalid, reserved or repeated namespace, a missing root or a package with no source exits 2 with a `packages:` line and writes nothing.
- [PACKAGES-BUILD-1] `selka packages build ENTRY --lock LOCK [--command check-project|run-project|index-project|emit-project-qbe] [--receipt FILE]` re-inventories the entry's directory and every locked package and exits 2 before anything compiles on a package whose files changed since locking, a namespace locked twice, a module path the application and a package share, a module named like a standard one (`std_`) or a symbolic-link source; otherwise it copies the re-hashed bytes and the nearest `selka.json` into a private directory under `TMPDIR`, runs this executable's `project-dependencies` and then the command there (`check-project` by default), forwards the command's exit status (for `run-project`, with `--print-result`, the program's own), writes neither the application tree nor a package root, and removes the staging directory. `--receipt` writes `package-build-v1` (the SHA-256 of the lock's compact JSON, this compiler's identity and the dependency report) once the snapshot checks and before the command runs, and a refused build leaves an earlier receipt untouched.
- [PACKAGES-FIX-1] `selka packages fix ENTRY|DIR --lock LOCK [selka fix's switches]` verifies the lock as `build` does, stages the application and the packages privately and runs `selka fix` there by its own rules; a repair that edits a package's file is never applied and is counted per package in one closing line; each application file `fix` changed is written back only when it still holds the bytes it was staged from, otherwise nothing is written and the run exits 2.

- [EXPORT-PLACEMENT-1] A re-export `export module.Name;` naming an imported declaration may appear anywhere among top-level declarations after the module/import header. Discovery collects directives in source order with original locations; a duplicate or unknown re-export and private interface leaks still reject. The bare `export Name;` directive is removed and rejects with `E_EXPORT_REMOVED` (see `PUBLIC-DECL-3`). Directives inside function or type bodies reject, and formatting preserves directive order and comments.
- [PUBLIC-DECL-2] `list-declarations` reports a `public` declaration's `exported` flag as true while its `exports` array stays empty, and the declaration's byte range begins at the `public` modifier so a rename round-trips exactly.
- [PUBLIC-DECL-3] The bare `export NAME` directive is removed: it rejects with `E_EXPORT_REMOVED` at the directive, both in `list-declarations` and when a project is checked. Only the dotted `export module.Name` re-export, which publishes an imported declaration, remains an export directive.
- [REEXPORT-1] An export directive may name an imported declaration (`export shared.units.Distance`) as well as a local one. The facade module publishes that declaration under its own local name; a reference through the facade resolves to the defining module's identity with the target's own export privacy enforced, and interfaces, contracts and the project fingerprint report the facade name while the schema uses the defining declaration.
- [PUBLIC-DECL-1] A top-level declaration is published with a leading `public` modifier (`public def`, `public host def`, `public extern def`, `public record`, `public class`, `public enum`, `public union`, `public result`, `public resource`, `public type`, `public const`, `public constraint`). The modifier replaces the bare `export NAME` directive, so a module's published surface is written on the declarations themselves and `list-declarations` reports each declaration's export flag with an empty directive list.
- [DIRECTORY-CASES-1] An entry's cases file sits beside it as `<stem>.cases.json` (`driver_tests.selka` owns `driver_tests.cases.json`). `selka test ENTRY` without `--cases` applies that file when it exists, so a directory run applies each entry's own file to that entry and nothing else; an explicit `--cases FILE` still wins for a single entry and is still refused for a directory.
- [TEST-PRIVATE-ACCESS-1] A module's test file sees its private declarations: a module named `<name>_test` that no other module imports may reference the non-`public` functions, types and constants of module `<name>` (so `scanner_test.selka` calls `scanner.helper()` without making it `public`). Every other importer, including a `<name>_test` that some module imports and a test file of a different module, still gets `E_PRIVATE_SYMBOL`.
- [REEXPORT-2] An export directive naming a qualified path whose alias is not imported, or whose target is not exported in its defining module, rejects with the existing located E_UNKNOWN_EXPORT/E_PRIVATE_SYMBOL family. list-declarations reports the facade directive with its alias and leaves local declaration flags unchanged.

- [PROJ-1] Loads shared module dependencies once in dependency order and releases state on allocation failure.
- [PROJ-2] Reports module cycles with both the importing location and the related source location.
- [PROJ-MODULE-NAME-FIX-1] `E_MODULE_NAME` states the rule and the fix: it names the declared header, the file, and the module name that file must declare (its path from the project root without `.selka`), and ends with the exact header to write — for `sexpr/parser.selka` declaring `module parser`, the message ends `write module sexpr.parser` with the header quoted.
- [PROJ-3] Rejects duplicate exports, missing exported symbols, private types exposed through public interfaces, and a re-export whose qualifier names no import or whose target is not exported in its defining module.
- [PROJ-4] Reports related declarations in their original source files after project source mapping.
- [PROJ-5] `selka run FILE` loads the reachable project graph when the entry file opens with a `module` header or a module-only `import`/`export` directive, so a single file that imports a standard module needs no separate command; a file with no module header stays one standalone unit, and `run-project` remains the explicit project-entry alias.

- [PROJ-6] Project loading defaults to 16,384 reachable modules and 262,144 import edges (PROJ-SCALE-1); a `selka.json` `budgets` object overrides `max_modules`, `max_imports`, `max_file_bytes` and `max_total_bytes`. Each present value must be a positive integer, unknown and duplicate budget keys are rejected like every other configuration field, and an absent field keeps its default.
- [PROJ-SCALE-1] The default project budgets admit a million-line project with no `selka.json`: 64 MiB of total source (a generated million-line project is 35.1 MB), 4 MiB per file, 16,384 modules and 262,144 import edges. `selka test` bounds its source snapshot by the same configured `max_total_bytes`, so one `budgets` entry governs both.
- [PROJ-BUDGET-MESSAGE-1] An `E_PROJECT_BYTES` refusal names the budget that ran out — the project's `max_total_bytes` or one file's `max_file_bytes` — with its size in bytes and the `budgets` field in `selka.json` that raises it, instead of the reader's `TooLarge` error name.
- [PROJ-7] `selka build FILE` detects a module-project entry the same way `run` does and links the reachable graph without requiring `--project`; a plain single-file program keeps the one-unit path, and `--project` remains an explicit override.


## Discovery and formatting

- [TOOL-1] Groups exact duplicate bodies after binding renaming while preserving resolved binding identity.
- [TOOL-2] Reports literal differences once per exact-group representative when discovering near duplicates.
- [TOOL-3] Reports incomplete near-duplicate discovery when work budgets are exhausted and rejects invalid budgets.
- [TOOL-5] `fmt` prints the one canonical layout to stdout, permits semantically incomplete drafts but rejects syntactically invalid input, and `--check` is nonzero when the input bytes differ from canonical output. The fixed layout uses four-space suites; one space around binary/assignment operators and after commas and annotation colons; no padding inside ordinary delimiters; no trailing whitespace; one final newline for nonempty source; two blank lines between top-level declarations and one after the header directive group. Comment attachment, literal values, directive order and expression evaluation order are preserved, and there is no configurable wrapping.
- [FMT-MODULE-DETECT-1] `fmt` formats a module file instead of rejecting it: a source whose first line that is neither blank nor a comment opens with a `module` header or an `import` directive is formatted exactly as `fmt-module` formats it, so `fmt FILE --check` works on every file the compiler accepts.
- [CHECK-LIBRARY-1] `selka check-module FILE.selka` checks a library module and its reachable imports, including ordinary and generic bodies, effects, public type boundaries and configured policy, warning and interface-file gates, without requiring or synthesizing an executable entry point. It accepts `--value-semantics` with the same checker rules as executable checks. It leaves no executable check verdict; `check-project`, build and run retain their main requirements. Libraries containing only type or generic declarations are valid.
- [CHECK-LIBRARY-2] `selka check FILE.selka` checks a file that declares no free function `main` as a library, exactly as `check-module` does (bodies, effects, public type boundaries, configured warning and interface-file gates) and succeeds silently when it is well-typed, so the module an agent just edited needs no second command. This holds for a headerless single file and for a module or import entry, including one that imports a local `--local-stdlib` replacement, an empty file included; a type error in the library is reported as itself, never as `E_MAIN`. A file that declares `main` of any signature keeps the executable check, so a wrong signature, capability parameter list or generic `main` is still rejected with `E_MAIN` or `E_TYPE`. Commands that need an entry point (`run`, `build`, `check-project`, and `check --explain-ownership`, which analyzes a lowered program) keep `E_MAIN`, whose message names `selka check FILE`. The library verdict is never cached as an executable one. `--diagnostics=json` reports the same diagnostics as text. `check` takes a file, not a directory. `check-module` is unchanged: it always checks as a library, even when `main` is declared, and requires a module header.
- [FMT-WRITE-1] `fmt` and `fmt-module` accept `--write` (also `-i`) to replace the source file with canonical formatting through a sibling temporary and rename, so a failed write never truncates the input, and `--diff` to print a unified diff from the current bytes to canonical output and exit 1 when they differ. `--write` conflicts with `--check` and `--diff`; `--diff` may accompany `--check` and takes its place. A canonical input writes nothing and exits 0.
- [FMT-CHECK-LOCATION-1] A failed `fmt --check`, `fmt-module --check` or `fmt-project --check` reports the file path and one-based line and column of its first difference from canonical formatting, including a missing final newline at the original end of file. Clean files remain silent and unchanged; syntax errors retain their existing diagnostics.
- [FMT-TEST-MODULE-1] Formatting a headerless `*_test.selka` file permits imports of sibling project modules, matching the test runner's accepted source shape. `fmt` and `fmt-module` preserve the absent header instead of reporting `E_MODULE_HEADER` or inserting a synthetic declaration; ordinary project modules keep their header requirement.
- [TEST-SIBLING-HINT-1] When `selka test FILE.selka` selects no tests or benchmarks and a sibling `FILE_test.selka` exists outside the loaded graph, stderr names that sibling and explains how to run it explicitly or through a directory invocation. The existing exit status 3 and JSON report remain unchanged; a missing or already-loaded sibling produces no hint.
- [TOOL-6] Includes calls in untaken paths and cycles when calculating static function reachability.
## Evaluation and wire boundaries

- [WIRE-2] Rejects duplicate input names, keywords, unsupported input types, and noncanonical or out-of-range values.
- [WIRE-4] Historical scalar-domain codec validation, retired on 2026-10-14 by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1: duplicate, missing, unknown and private codec entries were rejected. Record replacements use ordinary explicit encoder and decoder functions.
## Command line

- [DECLARATION-LIST-1] `list-declarations` prints one parse-only `declarations-v1` JSON document with the declared module name or null for a single-file program, its import names and explicit aliases, its export directives (a re-export carries its qualifier as `alias`), and every top-level declaration's kind, name, export flag, byte range and attached comment start. Successful source declarations use alias, const, struct, enum, union, result, resource, fn and extern_fn kinds; retained recognition of retired domain declarations only supports diagnostics and migration (a retired `host def` fails the parse with `E_HOST_RETIRED`). An export directive sets the named local declaration's flag, a re-export marks no local declaration, and unknown and duplicate export directives reject with the existing located diagnostics. Listing requires only parsing, so modules with type or ownership errors still list, and no name resolution, checking or execution is claimed.
- [INTERFACE-SIGNATURE-1] `list-interfaces` prints each exported callable's whole signature, canonical capability type names, mutation markers, analysis status and test reachability. A body that fails to check leaves the declaration listed without reporting its body diagnostic. `--contracts` uses the same canonical signature type spelling. Effects and effect ceilings are absent ([EFFECTS-REMOVED-1]).
- [INTERFACE-SIGNATURE-CLI-1] The `list-interfaces` command renders a capability entry parameter as its Python spelling (`Filesystem`, `Clock`) rather than the removed internal tag, including for a single-file program that exports `main`.

- [DECLARATION-LIST-2] Listed byte ranges are zero-based, half-open, disjoint and ordered by start. A declaration runs from its first token through its terminating semicolon or final token before an implicit newline/EOF terminator, and its comment start covers contiguous leading `#` lines with no blank line before the declaration. The module header, import statements, export directives and comment-and-declaration ranges account for every byte of the file except whitespace and line comments between them.

- [DEPENDENCIES-1] `project-dependencies` emits a versioned checked module graph with resolved imports, source byte hashes, implementation identity and exact consumed generated-input manifest hashes. It does not execute guest code; invalid projects emit no complete receipt.


- [CLI-1] Distinguishes source errors, runtime traps, and invalid command usage through actual process results.
- [ARGV-1] A guest entry point may take `args: list[str]` as its first parameter; `selka run` and `build --run` reserve their own options before `--` and pass every value after it as guest arguments, installed executables pass OS arguments after `argv[0]`, and generated native entry points build the same managed list with validated UTF-8 text.
- [NATIVE-ARGV-1] A native executable from `selka build` hands `main(args)` every argument after `argv[0]` unchanged — `--`, `--gc-stress`, `--print-result`, `--max-steps=N`, `--restrict` and `--allow-*` included — and reads its runtime options only from the `SELKA_OPTIONS` environment variable, before `main` runs: the `--restrict`, `--allow-*`, `--max-steps=N`, `--max-frames=N`, `--timeout=SECONDS` and `--print-result` spellings `selka run` takes, separated by whitespace, where a double-quoted stretch keeps its whitespace and a backslash inside it escapes the next character. An unknown option, a budget that is not a positive decimal or is repeated, or an unterminated quote exits 2 (`selka: SELKA_OPTIONS names --engine=vm, which is not a runtime option`); `--timeout` arms the wall-clock guard; `--max-steps` and `--max-frames` are refused as QBE-STEP-ACCOUNTING-1 and QBE-FRAME-ACCOUNTING-2 say. `selka run`, `run-project` and `build --run` keep their own command lines: the grants and `--print-result` given before `--` travel to the program in `SELKA_OPTIONS`, replacing any inherited value, the budgets are compiled in, and only the values after `--` reach its argv. The internal native-test harness alone may build with `--test-step-budget`: its executable charges the same steps and reads each fresh trial's ceiling from `SELKA_TEST_MAX_STEPS` at startup (zero is unbounded; missing, malformed or overflowing values exit 2). Ordinary binaries never inspect that variable, and `build --max-steps` remains a usage error.
- [QBE-STEP-ACCOUNTING-1] Ordinary native builds omit instruction-budget accounting and refuse `SELKA_OPTIONS` `--max-steps` with `R_NO_STEP_ACCOUNTING`; `selka run` compiles its ceiling. Internal budget sweeps build one instrumented executable per compilation and execute a fresh process per ceiling with the unit harness's existing 512 MiB memory limit, capability options and guest arguments. The first and last finite ceilings also use ordinary compiled-in runs, and their output, status, trap and budget diagnostics must agree. Frame-budget and nondefault-memory runs stay on the ordinary compiled-in path.
- [CHECK-PROJECT-ENTRY-1] `selka check ENTRY` where ENTRY opens as a module project checks the reachable project graph exactly like `check-project`, executing nothing; a plain single-file program keeps the one-unit path unchanged.
- [EPRINT-1] `eprint` is the standard-error spelling of `print`: it accepts the same values and `sep=`/`end=` keywords, writes only stderr, contributes the same `output` effect, and shares the buffered one-line native output path without mixing bytes between streams.
- [STREAM-CAPABILITY-1] `Stream` is a capability passed like `Filesystem`. The entry point may declare `out: Stream` and `err: Stream`; the entry rule matches Stream parameters by name, admits at most one of each, and rejects any other name with `E_MAIN`. `stream.print(values..., sep=, end=)` renders like the old printing builtin and `stream.write(text) -> int` writes the text and returns its character count; both contribute the `output` effect and take the destination from the receiver value, so a helper declared `def show(log: Stream)` may be handed either `out` or `err`. Streams are granted unconditionally, including under `--restrict`, and the per-type native renderers write through the stream's own sentinel. A bare `print`/`eprint` reports `E_OUTPUT_CAPABILITY` with the fix to add `out: Stream` and pass one from main; a local binding or declared function named `print` still shadows the spelling.
- [STREAM-BYTES-1] `stream.write(data: bytes) -> int` writes the raw octets, including NUL and invalid UTF-8, without quoting or an added newline, flushes the selected stream, and returns the byte count. The existing `str` overload still returns its Unicode scalar count. Both preserve destination capability and the `output` effect, reject other argument types, and trap `R_OUTPUT` on a refused write or flush of standard output (a refused write or flush of standard error is ignored: the octets are lost and the program goes on), except a refusal with `EPIPE`, which is [STREAM-PIPE-1]; byte output performs no intermediate text conversion or managed allocation.
- [STREAM-BYTES-2] `stream.write(buffer)` accepts the mutable `byte_buffer` and writes the octets it holds at that moment straight out of the buffer's own storage: no snapshot is built, no copy is made, and the write uses the same explicit Stream authority and returns the same byte count as writing a byte string. Appending to the buffer afterwards cannot change what was already written, the lowered form is one raw byte-sink call naming the receiver, and a program that only writes a buffer links no copy, slice or snapshot helper.
- [DEBUG-LINE-1] `debug(values...)` renders like `print` to standard error, takes no capability, returns unit, and is emitted only by Debug builds and by `run`/`test`; a ReleaseFast `build --project` drops it as a single-file build does. It does not change the callable signature.
- [DEBUG-OPTIMIZE-1] `build`, `run`, `run-project`, `build --run` and `test` default to optimized debug: Core optimization passes and LLVM `-O2`, per-module native units, `debug(...)` output and checked language semantics. `--optimize-debug` and `--mode Debug` explicitly select that same mode and share its build/object/test cache identities. `--unoptimized` disables Core optimization and requests LLVM `-O0`, retaining a distinct diagnostic-mode cache identity; directory test children preserve that choice. `--release` and `--mode ReleaseFast` remain whole-program with requested `zig cc -O3` (the pinned driver currently maps this to Clang `-O2`). Selecting different explicit modes together is a usage error in either order; repeating the same mode is allowed. Optimized modes support `--no-facts` and `--verify-facts`; the shared C runtime stays at `-O2` in every mode.
- [RESULT-MAIN-1] A guest `main` may return `Result[int, str]`: `Ok(code)` becomes the process status `code & 0xFF`, while `Err(message)` writes one `error: message` line to stderr, followed by the error return trace (ERROR-TRACE-2), and exits 1; runtime traps and source/usage failures keep their existing distinct statuses. The synthesized entry wrapper that renders the error is the runtime's exit protocol and needs no guest Stream parameter, so a `main` with no `out`/`err` stream parameter may still return `Err`.
- [RESULT-MAIN-2] `main` may return `Result[int, E]` or `Result[None, E]` for any error type `E` that `print` can render — an enum, a union, a record, `str` or an integer. `Ok` keeps the status rules of RESULT-MAIN-1; `Err(value)` writes `error: value` and the error return trace to stderr (ERROR-TRACE-2) and exits with `value & 0xFF` when `E` is `int` and 1 otherwise. An error type `print` cannot render (a capability, a file, a callback) still rejects with `E_TYPE`.
- [OPTION-TRY-1] `?` propagates `None` only within a function returning a compatible `Option[T]`; a `Some(value)` unwraps to `value`, and mixing `Option` propagation with a result-returning function or vice versa is a located type error.
- [OPTION-TRY-3] Option propagation does not require the operand's element to match the enclosing function's: `?` on an `Option[T]` inside a function returning `Option[U]` unwraps `Some(value)` to a `T` and returns a fresh `None` of `Option[U]`, for any `T` and `U` (scalar, managed or class, concrete or generic), because `None` carries no payload. Propagating an option into a function that does not return an `Option` still rejects with `E_TRY_RETURN`.
- [BOUNDARY-CLI-1] `examples/boundary-cli` is a two-module acceptance program that combines `args: list[str]`, `Filesystem`, explicit `Environment`, `print`, `eprint`, source-file reads, and `main -> Result[int, str]`, and the same cases pass through `run`, `build --run`, and an installed executable.
- [CAPABILITY-METHOD-NARROWING-1] `fs.read_only() -> Filesystem` narrows a `Filesystem` capability to read authority by intersecting its permission mask. The derived capability is an ordinary typed value: it can be passed, returned, stored in records/classes, and cannot be stored in a list or `Cell`. It performs no I/O and contributes no effect.
- [CAPABILITY-METHOD-NARROWING-2] `restrict_resource[...]` is removed and reports `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` naming `fs.read_only()`; `acquire_resource`, `complete_resource`, `read_resource` and `write_resource` are unchanged; `environment_get[...]` is now a trusted standard-library spelling only.
- [FILESYSTEM-DIR-1] `fs.dir(path) -> Filesystem` derives a filesystem rooted at the string-literal `path`, resolved under the receiver's root. It records the scope lazily in a bounded host-side table, performs no I/O, and contributes no effect. Every acquisition through the derived capability joins the prefix and rejects a request that could leave the root (`..`, `.`, empty components or NUL; a backslash is a byte of a name); `restrict_resource` preserves the scope. A dynamic path or a literal that escapes the root is `E_FILESYSTEM_DIR` at check time, and a non-`Filesystem` receiver is rejected; a folder known only at run time is narrowed with `filesystem.dir_at(fs, path)`, which reads the path as `fs` reads one, requires a directory reached without a symbolic link and returns the same narrowed handle (docs/io.md, "A folder named at run time").
- [FILE-METHOD-1] Every whole-path file helper also accepts a qualified method spelling on a `Filesystem` value: `fs.open(path, mode)`, `fs.read_text(path)`, `fs.write_text(path, text)`, `fs.read_file(path)`, `fs.write_file(path, data)`, `fs.exists(path)`, `fs.listdir(path)`, `fs.mkdir(path)` and `fs.remove(path)`. The receiver is the capability operand, so the method form has one fewer argument than the free spelling, and both share the same arities, result types, `E_ARITY`, the literal-mode `E_FILE_ARGUMENT` rule and the trapping `with fs.open(...) as f:` head. The method belongs to the receiver, so a declared function or local named `read_text` still claims the free `read_text(fs, path)` spelling, and generic bodies keep rejecting the file builtins under either spelling.
- [NETWORK-HOST-1] `net.host(name) -> Network` narrows a `Network` capability to the single string-literal endpoint `name`. It records the scope lazily in the same bounded host-side table as `fs.dir`, performs no I/O, and contributes no effect. Every acquisition through the derived capability requests exactly the recorded endpoint, so a narrowed `Network` composes with a `connect` wrapper that takes no request string; an endpoint outside the active `--allow-endpoint` grant is still denied. Re-narrowing to a different endpoint traps `R_CAPABILITY`. A dynamic, empty or NUL-bearing name is `E_NETWORK_HOST` at check time, and the receiver must be a local `Network`.
- [NETWORK-ENDPOINT-1] `selka run`, `run-project` and `build --run` accept a repeatable `--allow-endpoint=IP:PORT` grant for a `Network` entry point under `--restrict`. The endpoint is a literal numeric IPv4 address with a nonzero port; a DNS name, an IPv6 literal, an empty value and a repeat are usage errors. The grant provisions the native scoped network provider, whose allowlist matches the request verbatim (no DNS resolution or redirect following) and dials it only on an allowed acquisition; `--allow-all` grants every literal endpoint. Without the grant, `--restrict` refuses the entry point before execution with `--allow-endpoint=IP:PORT` in the message, and an endpoint outside the grant fails as `Connected.err(2)` without opening a socket. A default invocation with no grant keeps the previous behavior: the network provider stays absent and network access is denied.
- [NETWORK-SURFACE-1] The `network` standard module wraps provider acquisition and I/O so a guest does not spell the raw builtins: `connect(net)` acquires a `Connection`, `read[const N](connection)` and `write[const N](connection, data)` transfer bytes, and `close(connection)` completes the handle. The wrappers return declared `result` types and contribute the `network` effect; because resources are linear, each wrapper consumes the connection it receives.
- [NETWORK-SERVER-1] `selka run`, `run-project` and `build --run` accept the repeatable `--allow-listen=IP:PORT` grant for a `Network` entry point under `--restrict`: one literal numeric IPv4 address with a nonzero port, matched verbatim, with `--allow-all` granting every literal listening endpoint. `network.listen(net)` acquires a `Listener` through a `net.host("IP:PORT")` narrowing, which must match a listen grant; its one-byte mode request is preserved through narrowing, and a connect-only `--allow-endpoint` grant never permits it. `accept_resource[Accepted](listener)` borrows the live listener, blocks until one peer arrives, and returns a fresh `Connection` with read, write and complete rights but no accept right; the listener remains available for later accepts. `write_resource[...]` accepts a managed `bytes` value as well as a fixed `u8` array, so a response of dynamic length needs no copy into a fixed buffer. A listen outside the grant fails as `Listening.err(2)` without binding, a missing grant refuses the entry point before execution, and the error text names `--allow-listen=IP:PORT`. Only a listening acquisition holds the accept right, and a listener holds no read or write right: `read_resource` or `write_resource` on a `Listener`, and `accept_resource` on a `Connection` (outbound or accepted), fail as `err(2)` without blocking.
- [NETWORK-BACKLOG-1] A listener bound by `network.listen` or `network.open_poller` queues the connections that complete their handshake before the program accepts them up to the system maximum (`SOMAXCONN`, which the kernel clamps to `net.core.somaxconn`), not 64: a client that connects while the program is busy waits in the kernel's accept queue rather than having its SYN dropped and retransmitted a second later.
- [NETWORK-POLL-1] `network.open_poller(net)` binds the listening endpoint a `net.host("IP:PORT")` narrowing names, under the same `--allow-listen=IP:PORT` grant as `network.listen`, and returns one `Poller` resource (rights read, write and complete, no accept) that owns the listener, an `epoll` set and a table of accepted connections; closing or dropping it closes the listener and every connection it holds. The acquisition's one-byte mode request (2, beside 0 connect and 1 listen) is preserved through narrowing, and a Network narrowed below the read, write, complete and accept rights, or a denied stand-in, refuses it. `--allow-listen=IP:0` is a valid grant: it permits only `net.host("IP:0")` (matched verbatim, so it does not permit another port and another grant does not permit port 0) and the kernel then chooses the port; `--allow-endpoint=IP:0` stays a usage error, and `network.listen` answers `err(2)` for a port-0 narrowing because only a poller can report the port. A poller holds as many connections as the process may open descriptors, less 64 kept for the rest of the program, and at most 1,048,576 (the soft limit is raised to the hard one when the poller opens, and a program started with `process` gets back the one this program started with; the table starts at 1,024 slots and grows as connections arrive), and a full table, or an `accept` the process has no descriptor for, answers with the `full` outcome.
- [NETWORK-POLL-2] `network.wait(poller, timeout_ms, ready)` blocks for at most `timeout_ms` milliseconds (0 polls, a negative value waits for ever) and fills `ready` with up to 255 events, each a connection id and a flag set: readable 1, writable 2, hangup 4, error 8 and idle 16, with the listener as id 0. Readiness is level-triggered. A hangup or error also reads as readable when readable interest is set. The default interest is readable; `set_interest(poller, id, mask, outcome)` chooses readable, writable, both or neither, and `LISTENER` takes readable alone (without it the poller reports no new connections). A connection id is `generation * 1024 + slot` with generation at least 1, so it is never 0 and a closed connection's id never names a later connection that reused its slot. `set_idle_timeout(poller, millis, outcome)` makes a `wait` report the idle flag for a connection that has moved no bytes (accept, `recv` or `send` with a positive count restart the timer) for `millis` milliseconds and restart its timer, shortening the wait so the report is on time; the runtime reads the monotonic clock for this and tells the program no time.
- [NETWORK-POLL-3] `accept`, `recv`, `send`, `shutdown_send` and `close_connection` never block and fill an `Outcome` whose `code` is ok 0, would-block 1, closed 2, failed 3 (`value` is the errno), unknown 4 (an id that is not open), full 5 or bad 6. `accept` returns the new id in `value` over a non-blocking `TCP_NODELAY` socket; `recv` appends at most 16,384 bytes to a byte builder and reports end of stream as closed; `send(poller, id, data, offset, outcome)` offers `data[offset:]` (at most 65,536 bytes per call, with no slice made) and reports how many bytes the kernel took, using `MSG_NOSIGNAL` so a closed peer is failed rather than a signal; `shutdown_send` half-closes the write side and `close_connection` closes the socket and frees its slot. Completing or dropping the `Poller` closes everything it holds.
- [NETWORK-POLL-4] `network.port(poller, id, outcome)` reports the local port of the listener (`LISTENER`) or of a connection, which with an `IP:0` endpoint is the port the kernel chose (FR-204), and `network.peer(poller, id, outcome)` reports an accepted connection's IPv4 address in `value` and its port in `extra` (FR-216); `network.format_address(address, port)` spells them `a.b.c.d:port`. `network.connection_limit(poller, outcome)` reports the table limit in `value` and the open count in `extra`.
- [NETWORK-POLL-5] A `Poller` is an ordinary network resource to the checker and the Core lowering: the wrappers take it by value and return it (FR-203's recipe, with the bare resource as the return value so a loop reassigns one local), results other than the poller come back through `mut` arguments (`Ready`, `Outcome`, a byte builder), and the wrappers speak to the runtime only through `acquire_resource`, `write_resource` (one 16-byte-header command, plus a payload for a send) and `read_resource` (the last command's response: a 12-byte header of status, value and extra, plus received bytes or 8-byte event records), so the event loop adds no compiler builtin. A malformed command is answered in band (`bad`), a command that cannot be run on the transport is `failed`, and a new command discards an unread response.
- [NETWORK-POLL-6] `network.open_client_poller(net)` returns a `Poller` that listens on nothing, for a program that only connects out: `dial`, `dial_to` and their `_within` forms add connections to it, and `wait`, `recv`, `send`, `close_connection` and `connection_limit` work as on the connections of `open_poller`, with the same limit and table. It needs no listen grant and mints no authority: the open is refused with 1 when the launch grants no endpoint (`--allow-endpoint`) at all, and, through a `net.host("IP:PORT")` narrowing, with 2 when that endpoint is not granted, and each dial is checked against `--allow-endpoint` when it is made as before. `accept` answers `OUTCOME_WOULD_BLOCK`, `wait` never reports `LISTENER`, and `port` and `set_interest` of `LISTENER` answer `OUTCOME_BAD`. The acquisition's one-byte mode request is 5. `network.memory()` offers no poller of either kind (2), and `denied()` refuses it as it refuses every request.
- [ENVIRONMENT-GET-1] On an `Environment` capability value, `env.get("NAME")` returns `Option[str]`: `Some(value)` decoded as UTF-8 for a value the provider exposes and `None` when the name is absent. The name must be a literal `str`, and a dynamic name is `E_ENVIRONMENT_NAME`. A name outside the active `--allow-env` allow-list is a denied authority and traps `R_CAPABILITY` rather than answering `None`. `environment_get[...]` is accepted only in embedded standard-library sources, like the `__float_sqrt` intrinsic.
- [ENVIRONMENT-ONLY-1] `env.only("HOME", "PATH") -> Environment` narrows an `Environment` to a literal tuple of names by intersecting it with the runtime `--allow-env` allow-list. It performs no I/O and contributes no effect, and the derived capability is an ordinary typed value. Only positional string literals are accepted; a dynamic, empty or NUL-bearing name is `E_ENVIRONMENT_NAME`.
- [ENVIRONMENT-NAMES-1] `env.names() -> list[str]` returns the visible environment names as a fresh sorted managed list, honouring both the `--allow-env` allow-list and any `env.only(...)` narrowing. It takes no arguments and contributes the `environment` effect. A native provider's opaque callback context never contributes names.
- [VALUE-EQUALITY-1] Values of the same non-callback, non-Cell aggregate type support `==` and `!=`: plain records, fixed arrays, unions, `Result` and `Option` compare structurally, and a managed `class` compares by reference identity. Reference cycles compare by identity, so a self-referential graph never diverges. A type whose fields reach a callback, `Cell` or resource still rejects with `E_TYPE`, and equality keeps left-to-right evaluation and the existing execution limits.
- [EQUALITY-CONTEXT-1] The two operands of `==` and `!=` share one aggregate type, and an operand reached through a field access over a known value supplies that type to a contextual constructor on the other side exactly as a plain binding does, so `hold.opt == Some("x")`, `hold.opt == None` and `hold.kind == Kind.atom("x")` type-check without an intermediate local. A field access whose base type or field is not statically known supplies nothing, so `Some("x") == Some("x")` still reports `E_OPTION_INFERENCE`.
- [EQUALITY-CONTEXT-2] Equality context follows mixed field and list, dictionary or fixed-array index paths from a known binding, in either operand position, so `rows[i].items != []` and `[] == table[key]` infer the empty list's element type. Recovering the type does not evaluate an index or change left-to-right evaluation; two empty lists without a typed operand still report `E_LIST_INFERENCE`.
- [OPTION-TRY-2] Generic body checking propagates `Some(value)` and `None` through `?` when the enclosing function returns a compatible `Option[T]`, and rejects propagation into a result-returning function with a located diagnostic.
- [CAPABILITY-DIAG-1] When an entry point requests several ungranted capabilities under `--restrict`, `run`, `build --run`, and installed executables stop at the first failed grant and emit exactly one identical diagnostic.
- [ARGV-LIMIT-1] Guest arguments accepted after `--` use dynamically sized storage, so `run` does not impose a lower fixed argument limit than `build --run` or an installed executable.
- [INCREMENTAL-ID-1] Module, declaration, and body identities are derived from canonical names and declaration/body content rather than source offsets; private body and comment edits preserve declaration identities and the project exported-interface fingerprint, while exported signature changes alter the interface fingerprint. The checked `project-dependencies-v1` receipt exposes these identities and fingerprints under `incremental`.
- [INCREMENTAL-INTERFACE-1] The project interface fingerprint is composed from sorted semantic fingerprints of exported interface items only. It tracks exported constants, parameter defaults, keyword parameter names, nominal types, generic requirements and mutation metadata; comments, formatting, source paths, spans and private declarations are excluded.



- [MUTATION-OVERLAP-1] A read-only argument receives an independent value even when a same-call `mut` argument reaches its source, so it never causes `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP`.
- [MUTATION-METADATA-1] Writable parameters use `mut`; the old `mutates(...)` clause and effect clauses are retired. The compiler preserves write footprints, mutation-write facts and return-origin bounds through copying, relocation, generic substitution, callable identity and interface schemas, and rejects invalid IR summaries. These are mutation and ownership contracts, not inferred effect sets.
- [UNUSED-IMPORT-1] A direct `import` whose alias or dotted name no reference in its module resolves through is diagnosed as `W_UNUSED_IMPORT` at the imported name, beside `W_UNUSED_BINDING`. The evidence is the interface index's resolved-import flags, not an identifier scan: a qualified reference the checker accepted marks the import it resolved through, a binding that reused the first segment of a dotted path stays an ordinary value, and the alias inside a string literal or comment is not a reference. A cached body that was replayed rather than re-checked recovers its usage from the qualified names in its module's own source, so a warm run reports the same imports as a cold one. The warning is `W_*` and therefore rejecting under a configured `lint.warnings: "deny"` exactly like the other codes, and `import_policy` continues to evaluate every resolved direct import, used or not.
- [MUTATION-ENFORCE-1] A function cannot write caller-owned state through a read-only argument. Value transfers through records, arrays, options, unions, collections, helper results and wrappers are independent; local copies can be changed without changing their source. A `mut` argument writes through the caller's slot. Callee footprints substitute into actual arguments to a fixed point through recursion, unused generics, specializations and indirect callbacks; analysis-budget exhaustion widens conservatively with a located limitation. The guarantee is call-duration only and makes no total/trap-free/allocation-free/concurrency claim.
- [INCREMENTAL-SPEC-1] A generic specialization key mixes the template identity, canonical concrete arguments, the concrete provider type shape (fields, variants and layout flags), the target ABI and the compiler build identity; a provider-shape edit invalidates the key even when the template is unchanged.
- [INVALIDATION-EXECUTION-1] An invalidation report records actual query work: a stage a command did not run is reported `skipped`, never as reuse; lowering, emission and linking consume body and emitted-fragment inputs, so a body edit invalidates them; and a rejected check emits a best-effort explanation against the previous baseline without publishing a successful verdict.
- [WORK-REPORT-1] Ordinary check, build, run and session commands emit a versioned `work-report-v1` that separates an unchanged fingerprint from actual result reuse and reports each of parsed modules, checked bodies, instantiated specializations, lowered functions, emitted fragments, links and executed tests as `hit | miss | skipped` with fresh-performed and reused counts. A stage a command did not run is `skipped` and is never counted as reuse; bypass reasons and cumulative-versus-request session deltas are included. Existing `frontend-cache-v1` and `invalidation-report-v1` shapes remain comparison-only and are documented for migration.
- [INVALIDATION-BASELINE-1] A baseline is compared only after its version, implementation identity, inputs array and incremental module table validate; a structurally invalid baseline makes every executed stage miss with `corrupt-baseline` instead of comparing missing fields into hits.
- [CLI-2] Reports missing arguments and missing source files as usage or host failures.
