- [EXIT-STATUS-1] The entry point's `int` result is the process exit status, the way a Python or Go program returns an exit code, truncated to the low 8 bits (POSIX `waitpid`: `status = value & 0xFF`), for `selka run`, `run-project` and a native executable from `selka build`. So an entry returning 7 exits 7 and one returning 256 exits 0. `--print-result` (in `SELKA_OPTIONS` for a built binary) still writes the full value to standard output and is orthogonal to the status, so a program can print its result and exit with it at once. A runtime trap outranks the return and exits 1, because the program never reached it; source and check errors exit 1 and usage, capability and host failures exit 2. Unit and Result entries adapt first ([PYTHON-CORE-2]): a `unit` or `Ok(unit)` entry exits 0, an `Ok(n)` or `int` entry exits `n & 0xFF`, and an `Err(code)` entry exits `code & 0xFF`.
- [CLI-3] Executes the plan acceptance programs and recursive functions through the command-line executable.
- [CLI-4] Schema generation reads declared input manifests into generated modules on every invocation and rejects malformed or duplicate inputs. A generated module is ordinary source — a `#` provenance comment, a `module` line, `export` directives and one `def` per value — and spells a boolean `True`/`False` even though its manifest spells it the way JSON does.

- [CACHE-1] Reusing unchanged module syntax preserves fresh semantic checks and changed results, with independent returned storage and bounded cache retention.
- [FRONTEND-CACHE-1] Ordinary one-shot `check`, `build`, `run` and `test` commands reuse parsed module syntax from a persistent content-addressed cache under `--cache-dir` (default `.selka-cache/frontend`), keyed by the compiler build identity, the exact source path and bytes and the module body start. Declaration collection, symbol/export resolution, interface visibility, ownership, effects and every typed body still run fresh, and a changed byte yields a miss. Damaged, truncated or foreign entries fall back to a full parse, and `--no-cache` disables reuse.
- [CLI-5] Persistent checking sessions reuse unchanged parses and allow clearing retained cache storage between requests.
- [CLI-6] The removed C-backend command names `emit-c` and `emit-project-c` fail with status 2 and a migration that names `selka build FILE.selka -o OUT --emit-llvm PATH`; no live C backend or VM command remains in the CLI surface.
- [FRONTEND-CACHE-2] An ordinary one-shot `check-project` may reuse a successful whole-program verdict from the persistent cache only after loading every module fresh and comparing each module name, path and source byte against the content-addressed key; reused warnings are replayed and interface/semantic work is skipped. Failed checks are never stored, a compiler or diagnostic-format change misses, and `--report`/`--timings` expose reuse and miss counts.
- [CACHE-CHECKSUM-1] Persisted frontend syntax and verdict entries and checked-body store entries carry a 64-bit payload checksum taken from the leading bytes of the payload's BLAKE3 digest; an entry whose payload no longer matches it is damaged and treated as a miss.
- [FRONTEND-CACHE-3] Cached module syntax round-trips every field of the parsed program, including retired expression forms retained for diagnostics and migration. A warm lookup checks and rejects the same source as a cold parse; retaining syntax does not authorize executable lowering.
- [FRONTEND-CACHE-RETAIN-1] The persistent frontend cache is trimmed once per run that stored an entry, after the command and before its receipt, to the larger of its 128 MiB floor and twice the entry bytes the run read back or stored (its working set), evicting least recently used entries first: a successful read renews an entry's modification time. A project whose working set outgrows the floor (about 440k generated lines) therefore keeps every entry it uses, and an unchanged rerun evicts nothing, instead of every run evicting every entry.
- [FRONTEND-CACHE-ENTRY-1] Once a project is loaded, the frontend cache's per-entry ceiling (and retention floor) becomes the larger of 128 MiB and eight bytes per byte of the project's source. A project's check summaries are about one byte per source byte (39 MB at a million lines) and a module's syntax about eight per byte of that module, so no entry a project produces is bypassed at any size, while a damaged length still cannot make a read allocate more than the project could have produced.
- [CHECK-SCALE-LEX-1] Reserved words and removed spellings are recognised through compile-time maps, layout lowering finds line starts in one forward pass over the source, header discovery lexes each module into a scratch arena it resets before the next module (keeping only its diagnostics and import facts) and records which imports a dotted reference qualifies from the tokens it already holds (so the unused-import warning never lexes a module twice), and a reference spelling made only of identifier bytes and dots is normalized without lexing it. Every spelling lexes to the same tokens and every reference resolves exactly as through the full lexer: a keyword segment is still not a name, and a removed spelling is still left to the lexer, which reports `E_REMOVED_SPELLING`.
- [CHECK-SUMMARY-1] A successful one-shot `check` of a project records one summary per ordinary function — its stable declaration key, dependency context, the BLAKE3 digest of its declaration region, its mutation and origin summaries, the diagnostics it contributed with region-relative spans, and its direct callees — in one slot per entry of the frontend cache. The next `check` of that entry stands a summary in for every body whose context and region are unchanged: the body is neither checked nor copied, mutation and origin validation uses its stored facts without reporting new body diagnostics for it, and its stored diagnostics are replayed at the region's current offset. The entry point, constants, conversions, host and foreign declarations, and bodies whose checked tree names a specialization, a lambda or a type interned while checking are always checked. After the passes every checked body that had a summary must still have exactly its stored mutation and origin summaries, and no call cycle may pass through both a checked and a stood-in body; a changed fact, such a cycle, or any error the passes report makes the check repeat in full with no stand-ins, so a check's diagnostics are always those of a check without summaries. `work-report-v1` counts a stood-in body as a reused `checked_bodies` body; `--explain-invalidation`, `--no-cache` and every command other than `check` use no summaries.
- [CHECK-SUMMARY-2] A command that lowers the whole checked project (`build`'s frontend `emit-project-qbe`, `run`, `run-project`) reads the same summaries but checks every body, because lowering needs its tree; a summary stands in only for an unchanged body's effect and mutation facts and its pass diagnostics, so the effect and mutation passes walk only changed bodies. The same fact and cycle conditions as `check` apply, and a violated one repeats the passes in full. Such a command publishes summaries only when it had none to stand in or repeated in full, and a request served by summaries neither reads nor publishes retained checked trees.
- [CHECK-SUMMARY-3] Semantic dependency keys resolve the longest matching direct-import qualifier, including full dotted module paths and qualified symbol suffixes, so signature and visibility edits invalidate both checked-body caches and summaries. Every cached summary check uses its persisted checked dependency graph to invalidate the transitive callers and function-value consumers of changed declaration contracts, visibility, removed declarations or touched record shapes, including references nested in formatting expressions. A failed body check with active stand-ins repeats in full before reporting diagnostics, so cached signature, privacy and missing-symbol failures have exactly the same complete diagnostics as a cold check.

- [FRONTEND-DEMAND-1] A lowering host may admit persisted body artifacts by stable declaration key, dependency context and declaration-region digest. Successful checks omit only ordinary summary-eligible bodies whose host admits an artifact or proves prior unreachability, preserving checked signatures, effect and mutation facts and relocated diagnostics. A persisted dependency graph includes direct calls and function-value references; edited, removed or changed-dependent declarations invalidate their transitive consumers, while unrelated bodies remain omitted. Changed inferred facts inside that invalidated closure are recomputed without a full repeat; unexpected changed facts, mixed fresh/stored cycles and diagnostics retain the full-check fallback. Concrete specialization edges have no ordinary summary-table position and cannot make the summary table undecodable; their callers remain on the fresh path. Demand requests decode retained typed bodies only when needed, and cycle validation avoids whole-graph clearing for checked bodies that reach no stand-in. Every successful demand check publishes current summaries and reports actual omitted instances so lowering must restore artifacts for reached omissions or repeat the frontend in full.
- [FRONTEND-UNREACHABLE-1] A successful lowering manifest separately records ordinary declarations that complete lowering discovery never reached. Unchanged summary-eligible declarations with those receipts may omit their checked bodies. If later discovery reaches one, lowering repeats with a complete frontend before producing its artifact; missing receipts, incomplete discovery and reached declarations without safely reusable body artifacts never count as unreachable. Receipt admission validates both dependency context and declaration-region digest, and damaged or foreign manifests admit nothing.

- [SEMANTIC-KEY-IDENTITY-1] A declaration's dependency fingerprint names its defining module and, for a function, its `@deprecated` reason. Retargeting an import alias to another module's byte-identical declaration therefore invalidates every body that references it (the new target has its own types and inferred facts), and deprecating a callee invalidates its cross-module callers, which then report `W_DEPRECATED` exactly as a cold check does.
- [SEMANTIC-KEY-TRANSITIVE-1] A declaration's dependency fingerprint is transitive and independent of the order declarations are keyed in: it covers its own digest and the own digest of every declaration it reaches, so a cycle of recursive or mutually recursive types folds every member into every member. A composite type spelling (`list[T]`, `dict[K, V]`, `Option[T]`, tuples, arrays, callback types) references each of its component types, a type declaration's own digest frames its source text (field types, defaults, `record`/`class`), and a name a facade re-exports binds the declaration it names. A field renamed, retyped or reshaped in a record reached only through a list element, a dictionary value, an Option payload, a return type, a type cycle or a facade therefore invalidates every body that reaches it, and an incremental check reports what a cold check reports.
- [CHECK-SHAPES-1] A check summary, a retained checked tree and a retained specialization also record the transitive shape digest (schema of the record and of every record its fields, cases, elements and callback slots reach) of every catalog record the checked tree touched. A summary is not stood in, and a retained tree is not reused (`record-shape-changed`), unless every such record still exists under its name with the same shape in the fresh catalog, however the body reached it. The checked-body store format is `semantic-cache-v6` and the summary format `check-summaries-v3`.
- [CHECK-SCALE-KEYS-1] Dependency-key preparation records, per function, the BLAKE3 digest of its declaration region and the names its syntax references; check summaries keep both. While a function's region digest is unchanged, the next preparation resolves the recorded names instead of walking the function's syntax again, which binds exactly the declarations and negative lookups the walk would, so every dependency digest is unchanged; a digest that no longer matches is ignored and the syntax is walked.
- [CHECK-SCALE-SUFFIX-1] Each semantic request indexes collection and optional type suffixes once by their complete source span, including both start and end. Nominal metadata, concrete substitution, generic inference and symbolic body/shape checking resolve suffixes by constant-time exact lookup, preserving nested suffixes with a shared start, the first suffix for a duplicate exact span, generic list/optional/fixed-array identities, qualified type names and module relocation.
- [MODULE-INTERFACE-1] `selka interfaces emit ENTRY.selka [--out=DIR]` checks the project as one program and writes one versioned module-interface artifact per module (`<module>.selkai` in `DIR`, by default `<cache-dir>/interfaces` with cache-dir `.selka-cache`) and an `index.json` of each module's interface, implementation and positions hashes; `selka interfaces show FILE.selkai` renders one artifact canonically. An artifact holds the module's declaration text: its `module` line, the import lines that text uses, every exported declaration and, transitively, every declaration of the same module they name, copied verbatim, with each function body replaced by `pass` except those a dependent reads: generic templates, constants, conversions, host and foreign declarations, and functions the bytecode inliner may splice into a caller (in a lowering with or without `debug` statements). Each kept item is followed by the gap to the source's next top-level item, blanked except for its newlines, so a position past the item's last token maps back exactly. Beside the text it carries each replaced function's whole-program facts (mutation summary, return origin, write facts), an interface fingerprint per declaration, the facade names it re-exports with their targets' fingerprints, and the CHECK-SHAPES-1 digest of every catalog record its declarations reach, its own or another module's. Where each verbatim stretch came from (offset, line, column) is a separate positions section, and an `emission` section, empty in an artifact built from a check alone, holds the facts a module compile adds (MODULE-COMPILE-2).
- [MODULE-INTERFACE-2] A module's interface hash covers its artifact except the positions: the declaration text without its blanked gaps, the imports, each function's stable key, role, export flag, facts and interface fingerprint (its declaration digest, role, facts and kept body, folded over every declaration those name in any module, in an order-independent way), each type's and re-export's fingerprint, and the shapes. An edit to a body the interface replaces that changes none of the module's exported facts leaves it unchanged, as do comments and blank lines between declarations, moved declarations and edits to private declarations nothing exported reaches; a signature, visibility, deprecation, type declaration, record shape (including a record reached only through `list[T]`, `Option[T]`, a dictionary or a union payload of another module), fact, template, constant or inlined body changes it. The implementation hash is the module's name and source bytes and the interface hash of each direct import, so a body edit changes only its own module's. Neither hash contains a path or a time.
- [MODULE-INTERFACE-3] `selka interfaces check ENTRY.selka --module=NAME --from=DIR` checks one module against the interfaces in `DIR`: the module's own source, and for every other module only its interface text, each declared function standing in by stable key with its interface facts, as a check summary stands in for a body, and the abstract template check using their retained mutation and origin contracts instead of unavailable bodies; the standard library is read from the compiler's own source; a module other than the entry is imported by a synthetic entry, and the check reads only the artifacts of the modules it loads. `selka interfaces oracle ENTRY.selka [--module=NAME] [--from=DIR]` checks every module that way, from its artifacts after an encode and decode (or those in `--from`), and compares each with the whole-program check: every function the module's check produced has the same checked contract (mutation footprint, return origin, write facts) and signature types, every declaration of the module has the same dependency context, every body it lowered and inlined (a stood-in function keeps only its signature) is the whole program's instruction for instruction, with types resolved in the lowered catalog, callees by name and positions in their own module's source, every diagnostic it reports (positions in interface text mapped back to their source) is one the whole program reports, and together the modules report every whole-program diagnostic. A difference, a check that falls back to reading the bodies it stood in, or a project that does not check exits 1.
- [MODULE-INTERFACE-4] `selka --help` lists `selka interfaces emit|check|oracle <entry.selka> | show <module.selkai>`; `selka interfaces` with no action, an unknown action or an unknown option prints its usage on stderr and exits 2.
- [INVALIDATION-REPORT-1] A checked project can be explained stage by stage against the previous `project-dependencies-v1` receipt: `--explain-invalidation` emits one `invalidation-report-v1` JSON line naming load, parse, declaration, interface, body, specialization, lowering, fragment and link hits and misses with the first changed dependency, a missing baseline is all-miss, a corrupt baseline is all-miss rather than a failure, and `--report` adds the same per-stage counts to `frontend-cache-v1`. The baseline lives under `<cache-dir>/invalidation/` and is not partitioned by compiler identity, so a compiler change can be explained.
- [INCREMENTAL-KEYS-1] Every incremental cache entry includes the compiler build identity, bootstrap-language version, target, runtime ABI, capabilities contract and relevant flags through one contract fingerprint. Changing any dimension invalidates parsed syntax, whole-program verdicts, checked function bodies, generic-specialization keys and native object fragments even when the source bytes are identical.
- [INVALIDATION-MATRIX-1] For whitespace, comments, private bodies, exported signatures, effects, constants, record/union shape, imports, generic templates, specialization arguments, runtime ABI and compiler version, a warm check equals a clean rebuild in accepted status, diagnostic JSON, checked interface manifest, normalized QBE and reuse counts; missing or damaged cache data recomputes instead of trusting a partial entry.








- [NOMINAL-TYPE-SYNTAX-1] Nominal declarations retain explicit type parameters and requirements; nested named applications retain base and argument spans through parsing, formatting, syntax caching and module relocation. Lists are bounded at 16 and nesting at 256; module interfaces check exported type privacy, constructor kind, application arity and parameter shadowing. Malformed nominal declarations follow NOMINAL-TEMPLATE-CHECK-1; concrete specialization follows NOMINAL-INSTANCE-1.
- [GENERIC-SYNTAX-1] Generic syntax retains named type parameters, nonempty explicit requirement lists, and explicit `[...]` call type arguments through owned AST copying and formatting; duplicate parameters/requirements and lists beyond 16 are rejected. A specialized instance is named `f[i64]` after the arguments it was given, so a diagnostic and a disassembly name it the way its call site is written. Executable specialization must preserve these annotations through contract validation before lowering.

- [NOMINAL-INSTANCE-1] Concrete nominal records, unions, results and enums specialize by declaration and ordered semantic argument identities into complete runtime schemas. Synthetic enum payload records retain their defining parent module in canonical identities and type display names; equal case spellings in different modules remain distinct, while constructor and pattern spellings and runtime rendering stay unchanged. Construction, assignment, matching, result handling and owned-field flow use the ordinary checked IR/VM rules; concrete types compose with existing generic functions and numeric arrays. Unused templates and unexpanded phantom arguments do not become empty runtime records. Public type lookup requires exported bases and visible arguments, including phantom arguments. Symbolic nominal uses in generic function bodies follow NOMINAL-BODY-1. Canonical type display names have a compilation budget of 1 MiB or one byte per source byte, whichever is larger (NOMINAL-NAME-BUDGET-1), and schema export of a generic type constructor remains explicitly unsupported pending generic tooling.
- [NOMINAL-NAME-BUDGET-1] The display names of a compilation's nominal types may total 1 MiB or one byte per source byte, whichever is larger, and each name 1 MiB. A program's ordinary names grow with its declarations, so a large program is never refused for their total, while nested generic instances, whose names grow with the square of their depth, still stop a small program at 1 MiB with `E_GENERIC_LIMIT`.
- [CATALOG-INTERN-1] Materializing a type the catalog already holds adds no record and does not revalidate the catalog's layouts, and each identity is indexed once, when it is resolved, with the type index keeping the highest graph node per type. An instantiation whose type is already held, with no graph node added since the catalog last completed, returns that type without another pass over the graph. Checking cost per type use therefore does not grow with the number of declared records, and every distinct type is still interned exactly once.
- [NOMINAL-LAYOUT-1] Nominal declarations, including unused templates, have bounded finite storage graphs before executable specialization. Canonical symbolic identities preserve declaration, parameter scope and ordered arguments. Only stored fields/payloads and array elements expand; phantom arguments do not create storage edges, and zero-length arrays keep their element edge. A cycle through a type with no finite value rejects with E_RECURSIVE_RECORD and original closing-edge/related locations, and any other cycle is a recursive value type (RECURSIVE-VALUES-1); growing families reject with E_GENERIC_LIMIT at 4096 nominal instances, 4096 array shapes or 100000 expansion operations per declaration or materialized type (NOMINAL-LAYOUT-BUDGET-1). Graph IDs are not runtime type IDs and do not authorize execution.
- [NOMINAL-LAYOUT-BUDGET-1] The layout graph charges its instance, array-shape and operation limits to the request whose expansion first discovered a node: a declaration's symbolic root, or one concrete type the checker materializes. Each request gets the full bound, so a program's total may exceed it and the work stays proportional to the source; an exceeding request is reported at its declaration name or type spelling, with the type its expansion had reached as the related location.
- [NOMINAL-TEMPLATE-CHECK-1] Nominal declaration metadata resolves field and payload expressions to declaration identities and local parameters even in unused templates. It rejects unknown types, shadowing, duplicate members, malformed result schemas, application arity/kind errors and insufficient argument requirements. Copy guarantees propagate through ordinary and generic records/unions without copying owners or pending results; enums retain equality without integer operations. Expression checking is bounded at 256 levels and 100000 visits per top-level expression (NOMINAL-TEMPLATE-BUDGET-1). Metadata is not an executable layout: finite storage dependencies follow NOMINAL-LAYOUT-1; concrete instances follow NOMINAL-INSTANCE-1; symbolic nominal operations in generic function bodies follow NOMINAL-BODY-1.
- [NOMINAL-TEMPLATE-BUDGET-1] Each top-level nominal type expression — a field, payload, alias target, requirement or type spelled in a signature or body — starts a fresh budget of 256 levels and 100000 visits, so the bound is per spelling rather than per program; an expression whose alias expansion exceeds it is rejected with E_GENERIC_LIMIT at that spelling, with the type resolution had reached as the related location.
- [GENERIC-REQUIREMENTS-1] Builtin generic requirements are copy, equality, and integer, with integer implying equality and equality implying copy. Matching uses concrete equality rules and forbids implicit copying of owners or pending results; nominal records are not integers. Unknown requirement names and builtin type-variable shadowing are diagnosed even in uncalled templates before specialization.

- [GENERIC-BODY-1] The initial abstract generic body checker keeps type parameters distinct and requires declared integer/equality support for the corresponding operations. It checks pure builtin/type-parameter signatures, locals, returns, branches, loops, and casts without substituting representative concrete types. Unsupported forms and 256-depth/100000-work bounds reject explicitly; concrete specialization must retain these universal checks.

- [GENERIC-BODY-2] The abstract generic body checker applies the same wrapping conversion rule to `?` as the concrete path: when a propagated result's error type is not already the enclosing function's error type but is the payload of a case of that enclosing union, the propagation is accepted and the error is wrapped in that case; a symbolic union case payload compares structurally, so distinct type parameters still stay distinct.

- [GENERIC-ARRAY-1] Fixed numeric arrays may contain generic type parameters in signatures, constructors, annotations, nested arrays and explicit type arguments. Universal checking retains element requirements, exact lengths, nominal identities, copy/result obligations and source-located traps before specialization. Concrete shapes are interned by element identity and length, with bounded specialization; symbolic enum-keyed tables and parameterized lengths remain explicitly unsupported.
- [GENERIC-CALL-1] Abstract calls between pure generic templates resolve declaration identities and module privacy, require complete explicit or uniquely inferred type arguments and value-argument arity, enforce requirement implication, and substitute parameter/result identities without merging distinct type variables. Recursive calls check signatures without expanding bodies; reachable concrete helpers must also pass the abstract pure-body rules.

- [GENERIC-HELPER-1] Abstract generic checking validates every transitively called ordinary helper against the supported pure builtin body rules. A bounded declaration worklist checks each reachable body once, including recursive cycles; uncalled ordinary bodies are left to concrete checking. Helper signatures alone cannot authorize hidden effects. Broader effect and ownership contracts remain unfinished.

- [GENERIC-INSTANCE-1] The compilation-local specialization registry interns resolved declaration identities and ordered concrete type identities, owns argument storage, preserves IDs across growth and allocation failure, and reuses recursive requests. It bounds discovery at 4096 instances per generic declaration (GENERIC-INSTANCE-BUDGET-1) and each argument list at 16. Nominal types remain distinct regardless of representation. Executable checking uses these identities without conflating them with source declaration ordinals.
- [GENERIC-INSTANCE-BUDGET-1] The specialization registry counts instances per generic declaration: each may have 4,096, so a program's total grows with its generic declarations (4,097 declarations instantiated once each check), while polymorphic recursion, which multiplies one declaration's instances, still stops with `E_GENERIC_LIMIT`.
- [GENERIC-SITE-BUDGET-1] Distinct array shapes and callback signatures are counted per source spelling that specializes them: one spelling may make 4,096 of each, and the 4,097th is refused with `E_GENERIC_LIMIT` at that spelling ("generic array specialization at this spelling exceeds 4096 distinct shapes", "callback specialization at this spelling exceeds 4096 distinct signatures"). A spelling makes a new one only when a generic body is instantiated at new types, so recursion through it still stops, while a program's total grows with its spellings: byte literals of thousands of different lengths are thousands of spellings with one shape each.

- [GENERIC-EXECUTION-1] Pure generic templates specialize on explicit or uniquely inferred concrete type arguments after universal body validation. Repeated and recursive calls reuse instances; per-instance bindings, expression resolution, widths and nominal types remain distinct. Ordinary entry/export wrappers execute instances through the checked native backend. Internal instances are excluded from direct module export/schema roots. Main cannot be generic; unsupported abstract forms still reject.





- [GENERIC-DIAGNOSTIC-1] Checked specializations retain explicit ordered concrete type arguments. Human-readable function names include those types, preserve qualified nominal names, and do not depend on discovery ordinals. Traces and reports use these names, while public-root selection uses specialization metadata rather than spelling.

## Named constraints

The NAMED-CONSTRAINT-* clauses in this section describe historical contracts retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1. Retained parser and type metadata supports removal diagnostics and migration, not successful source constraint declarations or named bounds. Current generic contracts use inline builtin/field bounds and explicit callback parameters where required.

- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SYNTAX-1] A top-level `constraint NAME[SUBJECT] = REQUIREMENTS` names one subject binder whose right-hand side reuses the `+` requirement grammar. The parser rejects non-bare subjects, applied bounds such as `Prices[int]`, named or self references on the declaration's right-hand side, and requirement lists beyond the existing limit; the declaration and its bound references survive owned AST copying, relocation, formatting, declaration listing, and module-interface checks.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-1] Constraint declarations participate in the module declaration namespace and explicit exports with a distinct kind, resolve before signatures are checked, and accept bare and `alias.Name` function type-parameter bounds; an exported generic signature may use an exported constraint.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-2] Named bounds only apply to function type parameters. Records, unions, domains and constant-length parameters reject them, and a constraint name is not a value type: `policy: Prices` and `Prices[int]` report a declaration-kind error.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-3] Importing or naming a private constraint from another module rejects with the existing privacy diagnostic, an exported generic whose named bound is not exported rejects with `E_PRIVATE_INTERFACE`, and an undefined bound name reports `E_GENERIC_REQUIREMENT` at the bound.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-4] Constraint declarations obey existing namespace rules: a collision with another declaration or an import alias reports `E_DUPLICATE_SYMBOL` or `E_IMPORT_ALIAS`.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-5] Resolving a constraint identity outside the mapped project source is an internal error rather than a silently wrong declaration.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-6] A constraint subject may not shadow a module declaration, a direct import alias, or a builtin type, and an exported constraint's nested requirement signature may not expose private types.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-MODULE-7] Same-module and imported exported constraints resolve to the same declaration identity through the ordinary direct-import and alias rules, so interface and semantic checking cannot disagree.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-CACHE-1] Constraint declarations and their named bound references round trip through the persistent parsed-syntax cache under the bumped frontend format version; the in-memory syntax cache copies them with the rest of the declaration tables.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-1] A named bound expands to the same builtin/field/operation contract as the equivalent inline bound, so generic calls, concrete conformance and forwarding accept and reject the same operations in both spellings, and independently declared names with equivalent requirements satisfy the same obligations.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-2] The declaration's subject substitutes by type-parameter identity recursively through supported composite signature forms, so an operation requiring `list[P]` accepts the provider's `list[Provider]` without rewriting source text or capturing a consumer's same-spelled name.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-3] Historical scalar-domain operation/constraint contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1. Source effect ceilings are also retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1; retained recognition supports diagnostics and migration to free functions and explicit callbacks.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-4] Forwarding uses the existing implication rules: a stronger named bound may forward to a weaker one, and a weaker bound cannot forward to a stronger one even when the forwarding function is never called. Named and inline forms behave identically.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-5] Expansion counts unique explicitly required members and builtins, including implied guarantees, before applying the existing 16-requirement limit, and reports `E_GENERIC_LIMIT` when the expanded contract exceeds it.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-6] Historical scalar-domain operation/constraint contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1. Source effect ceilings are also retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1; retained recognition supports diagnostics and migration to free functions and explicit callbacks.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SEMANTICS-7] Unused constraint declarations are still checked: an invalid subject, an unknown or shadowed name, a nested named reference, a structural field without `copy`, or an unknown requirement signature type rejects before any provider or call exists.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-FIXTURE-1] The acceptance fixture checks and tests its fake-only `fake_tests.selka` entry successfully with `production.selka` absent and with an invalid unimported `production.selka`, and the production entry still rejects the invalid imported body once `main.selka` imports it.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-NAMESPACE-1] Standalone programs reject duplicate `constraint` declarations with a located duplicate-symbol diagnostic, whether or not either declaration is used, so adding a module header does not change declaration-namespace rules.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-NAMESPACE-2] Standalone constraint names collide with records, variants, results, enums, domains, resources, type aliases and top-level functions in either declaration order, while non-constraint duplicate collisions keep their existing diagnostics.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-NAMESPACE-3] Standalone constraint lookup reports the duplicate-symbol diagnostic instead of silently selecting the first of several same-named declarations.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-NAMESPACE-4] Standalone valid unused and multi-constraint programs remain accepted.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-SUBJECT-1] Standalone constraint subjects obey the same declaration-shadowing rules as module subjects, including unused declarations and collisions with types, aliases, functions and constraint names in either source order. Builtin type names remain invalid subjects; independent constraints may reuse a subject binder.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-CALLBACK-1] Historical scalar-domain operation/constraint contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1. Source effect ceilings are also retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1; retained recognition supports diagnostics and migration to free functions and explicit callbacks.
- [NAMED-CONSTRAINT-REVIEW-1] A permanent regression suite drives the review repros through the compiler: imported exported-constraint identity reads the defining declaration's symbol table; concrete names inside a named declaration resolve in that declaration's scope and cannot be captured by a same-spelled consumer parameter; merging compares resolved member identities and subject positions rather than source spelling; implied and named `copy` guarantees reach module preflight; the expanded requirement limit counts unique resolved members; checked schemas publish exported constraints, named bindings and expanded promises; and standalone declarations enforce namespace and full structural validity even when unused.








- [SOURCE-CODEC-1] Source domain codec declarations remain recognized only for validation, DOMAIN-REMOVED-1 diagnostics and migration. The operation names `codec_encode` and `codec_decode` still report lexical `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` with no codec-call repair. Checked successful type graphs retain no resolved domain codec descriptor; ordinary record graphs and existing JSON contracts are unchanged.

- [CHECK-CACHE-1] A persistent checking session may reuse a successful verdict only after fresh project and manifest loading and exact comparison of every loaded module name, path and source byte. Hits replay owned warnings and skip interface/semantic work; failures are never retained. Retention is bounded and clear drops syntax and verdict caches while preserving cumulative observations.

- [GENERIC-CONCRETE-1] Abstract generic checking resolves concrete copyable nominal and fixed-array schemas without conflating them with type parameters. Concrete record/array construction and projection obey declared field, element and index types; malformed uncalled templates reject. Concrete owned flow follows GENERIC-OWNED-1; individual runtime operations require their own abstract rules. Concrete result obligations follow GENERIC-RESULT-1.

- [GENERIC-UPDATE-1] Generic bodies may update concrete copyable records and replace fixed-array/table elements. Field uniqueness and field/element/index types are checked before instantiation; normal specialization preserves immutable snapshots, evaluation order and runtime bounds. These operations do not infer structural capabilities for symbolic parameters.

- [GENERIC-VARIANT-1] Abstract generic checking supports construction and exhaustive matching of concrete copyable enums/unions. Cases retain nominal identities and exact payload types; each arm scopes its payload independently, duplicate/missing cases reject, and a match terminates only when every arm terminates. Named enum-table construction requires every key exactly once. Result-bearing variants follow GENERIC-RESULT-1; runtime-owned value flow follows GENERIC-OWNED-1.

- [GENERIC-RESULT-1] Abstract checking of concrete result-bearing generic values enforces single transfer, explicit handling, scope/return obligations, and agreement across continuing branches, match arms, loops and short-circuit paths before instantiation. Nested payloads retain obligations and aggregate operations cannot erase siblings or replaced results. Propagation requires compatible concrete result errors and rejects live result bindings or previously evaluated result temporaries on its early-return path. An explicit `_ =` release evaluates its operand. Symbolic result type constructors remain pending.



- [GENERIC-CLEANUP-1] Concrete resource ownership passed through generic functions retains its provider lifecycle. Explicit partial extraction/drop and implicit scope cleanup release each live resource exactly once, including allocation failures and traps; forwarding does not complete or duplicate an external resource.


- [GENERIC-PROVIDER-1] Abstract generic provider operations enforce concrete or symbolic nominal result contracts, exact capability kinds and fixed byte-array inputs before instantiation. Resource I/O requires a mutable unconsumed owner after argument evaluation. Acquisition/completion preserve resource ownership and permission attenuation cannot create authority. Direct clock/random/environment/filesystem/network effects propagate through helper calls and enforce bounds even in uncalled or unreachable source paths.







- [DIAGNOSTIC-JSON-1] CLI --diagnostics=json selects diagnostic-v1 JSON Lines on stderr without changing command results or exit statuses. Source and VM diagnostics preserve stable codes, severity, original-file byte ranges and byte-based line/columns, related/origin/requirement notes, bounded frames and omitted-frame counts. Project-loader related sites retain their own module coordinates. Auxiliary CLI messages are separate message records. Successful check-session warning replay uses the selected format, and cache identity prevents cross-format replay. Default text remains available; invalid command-line syntax and emergency output failures may use text.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-FIX-1] A diagnostic may publish a machine-applicable `fix` with one or more exact source edits. Each edit names an original file, a UTF-8 byte range and replacement text. An edit that cannot be mapped wholly into one original file suppresses the entire fix, preserving the diagnostic; text rendering and diagnostics without fixes keep their existing form.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-FIX-2] The diagnostics that carry a fix: `E_MUTATION_MARKER` for a missing call-site marker (insert `mut ` before the argument), a superfluous one (delete the `mut` and its blank), a written parameter not declared `mut` when the write is attributed to named parameters (insert `mut ` before each one's type; an unattributed write that lists every position is advice only), and a `mut` parameter the body never writes or whose type has nothing to write (delete the marker); `E_MODULE_NAME` (replace the declared name with the path-derived one); `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` where the migration is a rename — `push`→`append`, `List`→`list`, the `*_cap` names→their capability types, `f64_sqrt`/`floor`/`ceil`/`trunc`/`abs`→the float builtin — plus `&` where an argument begins (→`mut `) and a function declaration's `mutates(...)` clause (`mut ` on each named parameter, clause deleted); `E_EXPLICIT_DISCARD_REQUIRED` for a value that is not a result (insert `_ = `; discarding a result would drop its error, so that is left to the reader); `E_KEYWORD_ONLY` (insert `name=` before each positional operand the printed call names); and `W_UNUSED_IMPORT` (delete the import's whole line when the line holds only that import and an optional comment).
- [FIX-MIGRATION-1] `selka fix ENTRY.selka|DIR` runs this executable's `check --diagnostics=json`, applies every published fix whose edits collide with no edit already chosen — an identical edit reported twice applies once, and a colliding fix waits for the next round, which sees the updated source — and repeats until a check publishes none. It prints `fixed PATH: N edits (CODES)` per changed file and a total line on stdout, then the entry's remaining diagnostics on stderr in the `--diagnostics=text|json` format, each diagnostic once across entries. It exits 0 when every entry checks, 1 when errors remain, and 2 on a usage or host failure; `--check` writes nothing, prints `would fix` lines and exits 1 when a fix would apply. A directory argument fixes every `.selka` file beneath it — files declaring `main` first, then `*_test.selka` files, then the other modules, skipping hidden and `zig-out` directories and a module an already-fixed entry imports. `--cache-dir=DIR` and `--no-cache` pass through to the checks. `selka --help` lists it among the everyday commands, and `selka docs fix` prints its guide from any working directory. Fix application never guesses from diagnostic prose.
- [FIX-MIGRATION-2] `selka fix` migrates the pinned pre-marker Lox syntax corpus (`test/fixtures/fix-migration/lox/`) across the call-site `mut` marker and subsequent source-syntax changes without hand edits: 412 edits in nine files over 19 rounds. After migration, two entries check clean, four report the existing optional-provider-storage refusal E_ZERO_RC_UNSUPPORTED, and three report the existing iteration-borrow refusal E_SINGLE_OWNER_OVERLAP. These remaining ownership errors have no certain repair: `fix --check` changes no bytes, reports `fix: nothing to fix` and the same ownership diagnostics, and exits 1. Syntax migration never bypasses checked ownership.
- [OWNERSHIP-COPY-FIX-1] Ownership diagnostics carry a structured `.copy()` repair only for a still-readable copyable value whose transfer conflicts with a borrow. Migration preserves evaluation, checks clean and is idempotent. Moved sources, provider/resource values and overlapping mutations carry no speculative copy fix.
- [OWNERSHIP-CONTRACT-1] Checked ownership allows a readonly scalar argument to observe an earlier mutable call argument before the callee starts; evaluation may not move or mutate that owner. Moving an affine field from a plain record consumes its carrier and requires every sibling field to be non-owning. Borrowed carriers, retained owner aliases and overlapping mutations remain rejected. Managed mutable call arguments may name disjoint list elements through effect-free chains of nonnegative literal list indices rooted in the same owning local or exclusive mutable parameter. Same-root managed paths without that proof retain the ordinary overlap refusal.
- [OWNERSHIP-GENERATED-1] Compiler-generated contract-value locals borrow their checked source while invariant/precondition/postcondition assertions inspect it; only the generated final transfer may consume an owned source. Ownership copy fixes never rewrite source binding declarations.
- [FIX-MIGRATION-OVERLAP-1] `selka fix` never applies edits whose byte ranges overlap in one pass, so it never writes a file mangled by stale offsets. Every repair stays whole on its own diagnostic — a project module's recovered parser diagnostics are reported one by one, never merged into one fix — and a round applies a repair only when none of its edits collides with an edit already chosen; a nested legacy expression and the one around it — nested conditionals, a conditional inside a comprehension, nested comprehensions, variant payloads with legacy type spellings — therefore migrate over successive rounds of reparsed source. `fix` withholds any published fix whose own edits overlap and names it on stderr. Certain nested repairs converge through reparsing; an unsafe expression context retains its removal diagnostic for a manual statement rewrite. The pinned original port-defects inputs remain unchanged. Their certain edits preserve structure and the explicitly repaired after-source checks, runs to the original result, and reaches a clean second preview.
- [FIX-MIGRATION-CAPTURE-1] A fix never makes a call or type name resolve to a user declaration. A removed spelling's rename (`f64_sqrt`, `f64_floor`, `f64_ceil`, `f64_trunc` and `f64_abs` to the float builtins; the `*_cap` names to capability types) is withheld, and its diagnostic says to rename by hand, when the file mentions the new name anywhere but a certain use: a member after `.`, a type after `:` or `->`, or a call after an operator or expression keyword on the same line. Declarations, parameters, bindings, loop variables, pattern captures, import aliases and keyword arguments all withhold it. A project module's removed spelling carries its rename like a single file's.









- [NOMINAL-BODY-1] Generic bodies retain nominal declaration identity and ordered symbolic arguments, check fields and payloads before calls, and preserve construction, assignment, exhaustive matches, named result propagation, partial ownership and cleanup effects. Substitution and late VM catalog materialization retain stable type IDs and reuse existing numeric array shapes. Phantom arguments retain identity without contributing stored-value contracts. Shape traversal uses an explicit stack and the generic checking work budget; argument-growing runtime specialization remains bounded by the nominal graph/name limits. Ordinary unconstrained type parameters still require structural constraints for member operations. Provider-specific symbolic shapes follow NOMINAL-PROVIDER-1; public generic schema tooling remains pending.

- [NOMINAL-PROVIDER-1] Generic resource/environment operations accept symbolic nominal result and payload records only when their exact named fields satisfy the ordinary provider contract for every permitted substitution. Declaration order does not affect meaning. Byte inputs and output arrays must have known u8 elements and numeric lengths; a general integer parameter is insufficient. Concrete and symbolic checking share named contract definitions. Resource identity, authority, mutable access, handling, cleanup and effect checks remain enforced before calls. VM specialization preserves bounded bytes, absent environment values, partial I/O, failed-completion ownership and cleanup under allocation failure or instruction traps.

- [GENERIC-CODEC-1] Historical generic domain codec execution contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1. Retained declaration validation checks method visibility, signatures and ordinary wire/error graphs for diagnostics and migration. Resolved decoder/encoder execution metadata is not retained, and the removed codec operation spellings still fail lexically.


- [GENERIC-BODY-3] A generic body, and every non-generic function it calls, may sort a concrete list in place with `values.sort()` when the element is an integer, a float or `str`, the same set the concrete checker accepts: the abstract checker types the call as unit and records it as a write to its receiver, as it does `append`, but seeds no allocation, because a sort allocates nothing managed. A list whose element is a type parameter or any other type still rejects with `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`, and every other unsupported collection operation keeps `E_GENERIC_BODY_UNSUPPORTED`.
- [GENERIC-BODY-4] A generic body may sort any list in place with `values.sort(key=key, reverse=flag)`, on a named receiver or any expression: the abstract checker checks the operands in source order, `reverse` as a `bool`, records a write to the receiver and seeds `managed_allocation` for the key list, and leaves the key to each specialization, which checks it against its concrete element type under LIST-SORT-KEY-2. Without `key=` the element still needs a native order, as for GENERIC-BODY-3, and a positional or unknown operand is `E_CALL_ARGUMENT`. A generic body may also call the prelude `sorted(values, key=..., reverse=...)` when no binding or declaration claims the name, typed as its list argument, with the same operand rules and `managed_allocation`, which is how `sort.sort_by_key` forwards; and it may take `values.copy()` of a list, typed as the list and seeding `managed_allocation`.
- [GENERIC-LAMBDA-1] A lambda written inside a generic function body reports E_LAMBDA_REMOVED once at the lambda, with a top-level named function as its replacement. Generic recognition does not invent a concrete callback repair from one specialization. This includes sort keys and callback arguments whose types mention no type parameter. Named function keys retain GENERIC-BODY-4 behavior.

## Interpreted schematic pilot



- [NAVIGATION-1] Compiler navigation indexes module-qualified declaration identities, original source ranges, signatures, effects, concrete call/read/write/field/type relationships and bounded source excerpts without VM execution. Every result carries compiler and loaded-source identities. Static relationships do not establish runtime coverage. Failed body checking emits declarations without checked relationships; lexical recovery after body syntax errors emits explicitly unchecked candidates.
- [GENERIC-API-1] Checked public schemas expose generic templates separately from concrete callable functions and data types, preserving parameter requirements, symbolic signatures, fields/cases, module import scope, effect contracts and source identity. Templates require explicit specialization and cannot be invoked as concrete values through the JSON API. Exported constraint declarations are published as non-callable `constraint` templates, and an exported generic parameter exposes each named bound's spelling and declaring module together with the expanded builtin/field/operation promises it contributes, under the bumped `generic-templates-v2` version.
- [GENERIC-API-DEFAULT-COPY-1] Public generic schemas retain the implicit `copy` requirement of unbounded function and nominal type parameters, while explicit `storable` or `codec` bounds do not acquire `copy`.
- [GENERIC-API-SUBJECT-1] Expanded named requirements in public schemas substitute only parsed references to the declaration's subject, recursively through nominal applications, arrays and callback signatures. Qualified concrete names with the subject's spelling retain their identity in both the combined contract and the per-bound expansion.
- [STRUCTURAL-FIELD-1] Copy-constrained function parameters may require named fields with exact named types, including another declared type parameter. Concrete satisfaction and symbolic forwarding preserve nominal identity and checked field reads/updates. Structural fields do not expose incomplete construction of an unknown record.
- [STRUCTURAL-OP-1] Historical scalar-domain operation/constraint contract, retired by DOMAIN-REMOVED-1. Source effect ceilings are also retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1; retained recognition supports diagnostics and migration to free functions and explicit callbacks.
- [STRUCTURAL-LIMIT-1] Structural requirements currently apply to function templates with copy guarantees and named type expressions. Nominal type declarations and unknown complete constructors reject explicitly.
- [INCREMENTAL-CAPACITY-1] The syntax cache retains at most 256 modules within its unchanged byte budget; edits can reuse exact unchanged module syntax while complete semantic checking and diagnostic equivalence remain required.


### Disk application raw byte host


The `selka-io --project DIRECTORY` host compiles `main.selka` and its imports from
disk into bytecode. It supplies only `io::length() -> i64`,
`io::byte(index: i64) -> u8`, and `io::put(value: u8) -> i64`, each with host effects.
Input bytes are passed untouched, including invalid UTF-8 and NUL. The guest owns
parsing, validation, algorithms, and serialization. Input is bounded at 2 MiB;
output at 128 MiB; a put returns zero on success. Negative/out-of-range reads and
output exhaustion fail the host call. Output is published only after guest main
returns zero, never after traps or nonzero exits. The host supplies no external
filesystem, network, environment or process authority to the guest. Host calls/work
are bounded at 300 million each and guest execution at 2 billion steps; the coordinator
also enforces wall/CPU/memory limits. The executable is built outside application
sessions; editing guest files requires no Zig rebuild or language Guardian run.

- [STANDARD-MODULES-1] Reserved standard sources load identically through all project providers and participate in source budgets, dependency graphs and digests. They are ordinary Python-syntax guest modules over managed types and may import one another.
- [STANDARD-BYTE-BUILDER-1] The standard `byte_builder` module assembles an immutable `bytes` snapshot over one mutable `byte_buffer` ([PY-BYTES-6]), so `append`, `append_range`, `append_byte`, `append_spaces`, `append_text`, `append_int` and `append_float` write each fragment's octets straight into that buffer's capacity and no fragment allocates a value for itself, with `append_float` writing the canonical spelling `str(value)` prints. The checked appends reject before writing anything, so a refused call leaves the builder byte-for-byte unchanged: `append_range` traps on a negative endpoint and rejects a nonnegative `end` below `start` or past `len(data)` and accepts an empty range that changes nothing, `append_byte` rejects values outside `0..255`, and `append_spaces` and `reserve` reject negative counts. `reserve` grows capacity for a further `extra` octets without changing the finished length, and `finish` publishes the buffer as an immutable snapshot in append order with independent owned storage ([PY-BYTES-SNAPSHOT-1]): it contributes `managed_allocation` but no mutation, the builder stays usable, and later appends, including an append of a snapshot back into its own builder, cannot change an earlier result. Every operation keeps the module's published names, argument types, effects and results, carries `mutation` and `managed_allocation` where it writes, mutates only its builder argument. Checked ownership keeps the builder live through each operation.

- [STANDARD-MODULES-2] Embedded standard source bytes count against the same per-file and total project source budgets as user modules.

## Guest application test tool




- [NATIVE-TEST-5] Before checking any production implementation body, native test preparation parses every reachable module and validates exported declarations and interfaces, then reads the case configuration and fully checks only the selected replacement provider graph. The declaration manifest carries canonical declaration-level type descriptors that normalize builtin aliases and expand transparent aliases while preserving nominal identities, and discovery and provider comparison consume those descriptors without checking unselected production bodies. Replacements are validated against every public contract they replace — names, defaults, canonical types and nominal identities, data shapes, generic requirements, declared effects, mutation footprints and return-origin bounds — using the directional subset relation, so a narrower implementation is accepted and a stronger one rejects. An unselected production body may contain a typed hole or body type error while its declarations remain valid; a production interface error, a malformed or unknown replacement, or a syntax error still rejects, and a full production build still rejects the unfinished body. Test receipts record the selected dependency-closure key plus the contract and provider identities, so changing a fake value or a relevant contract re-executes its case. Identical provider graphs share one declaration validation, checked project and prepared native library per invocation; distinct provider graphs stay isolated.




- [NATIVE-TEST-10] A failed `assert` reports the message written after its condition: the per-function native metadata carries one message per program counter, `selka test` renders it as `R_ASSERT: <message>` in the failure text beside the located code and any captured output, and `selka run` shows the same message on its human diagnostic; an assertion with no message keeps the bare `R_ASSERT`, and a message containing JSON syntax leaves the versioned trap record valid.
- [NATIVE-TEST-14] A directory run also selects a `.selka` library that declares a test — a `def test_` function, public or not, or an `@test` function — when no selected root's import closure reaches it, and checks it through the stub-`main` projection of NATIVE-TEST-9. Reachability is the compiler's own module-header graph from each root's project root, so a library a root imports keeps running once, as part of that root; of two such libraries where one imports the other, only the importer is selected. A test file therefore needs neither `main` nor the `*_test.selka` suffix to run.
- [AFFECTED-TEST-1] `selka test --affected --against REF` derives its selection from the working-tree diff rather than from the standing tree: it maps the changed lines `git` reports for `REF` onto the declarations they touch ([AFFECTED-TEST-2]) and runs a discovered test only when a changed declaration is in the dependency closure the run already derives for the test's receipt. It is a selection and never a verdict: the selected count and every skipped test are printed, `--filter` still applies first, `lint --gate` and CI run the whole set, and an empty derivation exits 3 exactly as a filter matching nothing does. `--against` without `--affected` (and `--affected` without `--against`) is a usage error, and a `REF` `git` cannot resolve is a host failure (status 2) rather than an empty selection. A project that cannot be checked fails exactly as it does without `--affected`, and a declared skip, an undefaultable case or a test the checked graph cannot name is kept rather than silently skipped.
- [AFFECTED-TEST-2] The `--affected` derivation compiles nothing of its own: one `git diff --unified=0 REF` under the entry's directory plus `git ls-files --others` name every changed or untracked `.selka` file, and each changed line maps by indentation onto the top-level declaration that owns it (a column-0 header up to the next column-0 line, an attribute opening its declaration's block). A line owned by a function or constant changes `module::name` and its generic specializations; a line anywhere else (module header, import or record) changes every declaration of the module; a pure deletion changes the lines on both sides of the gap; an untracked file changes on every line. The selection is then one reverse walk of the checked graph's dependency edges from the changed functions.


- [TEST-CAPS-1] Deterministic stand-in capabilities are built by effect-free standard functions that need no capability: `time.simulated_clock(start: Instant) -> Clock` reads `start` until `time.advance(clock: Clock, duration: Duration) -> Result[None, str]` moves it (copies share one reading; a host-granted clock is `Err("only a simulated clock can be advanced")`; leaving the signed nanosecond range is `Err` and leaves the reading unchanged); `random.seeded_entropy(seed: int) -> Entropy` yields the SplitMix64 stream the host's `--allow-random=SEED` uses, so `random.from_entropy` on equal seeds is reproducible and each read advances the stream; `filesystem.memory() -> Filesystem` is an empty in-memory tree that serves every whole-path helper, `open` in `r`/`w`/`a` modes with the handle methods, `exists`, `mkdir`, `remove` (a file or an empty directory) and name-sorted `listdir`, with the scoped-root path rules and the host surface's error texts; `environment.fixed(values: dict[str, str]) -> Environment` answers `get`, `names` and `only` from exactly those pairs (a name that is empty, longer than 128 bytes or holds `=` traps `R_ENVIRONMENT`); and `network.denied() -> Network` refuses every acquisition, and every `net.host(...)` narrowing of it, with the `Connected.err` code `network.denied_here()` (6), which `network.describe(code)` spells `network access is denied in this context: this Network is a deterministic stand-in`. `selka test` accepts a program that imports `network`: the declaration contracts render its `resource Connection: network` family as written instead of resolving it as a type.
- [TEST-CAPS-2] A stand-in never reaches a host resource. The native runtime keeps each one in a registry owned by the execution context: a clock, entropy or environment stand-in is a word in a range no host-granted authority uses, and a filesystem or network stand-in carries its registry index inside its resource authority, so `fs.dir(...)`, `read_only()`, `env.only(...)` and `net.host(...)` derive stand-ins that stay stand-ins, and every read, write, acquisition and listing through them is answered by the registry without consulting a host provider, the process environment or the disk. A narrowed stand-in environment traps `R_CAPABILITY` on a name outside its scope, as a narrowed host one does. The `__stand_in[...]` and `__clock_advance(...)` intrinsics behind these functions are reserved to the standard library and rejected in ordinary source with `E_INTERNAL_NAME`.
- [TEST-CAPS-3] A `test_` function may declare `Clock`, `Entropy`, `Filesystem`, `Environment` and `Network` parameters beside `out: Stream`/`err: Stream`; none needs a cases-file argument. The runner hands each one a stand-in: a simulated clock at 2000-01-01T00:00:00Z (946684800000000000 ns), entropy seeded with the first eight bytes (little-endian) of the SHA-256 of the test's qualified name unless `selka test --seed N` names one (a directory run forwards it), an empty in-memory filesystem, an empty fixed environment and a denied network. A `Host` parameter is never supplied, so no test is handed real authority: without cases-file arguments the test is a property test whose `Host` parameter cannot be generated, and it fails naming that parameter (PROPERTY-TEST-1). The seed and the start instant are part of a receipt's identity, so an unchanged test reuses its pass while a different seed executes again.
- [TEST-CAPS-4] Historical test-capability effect-containment contract, retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1. Test stand-ins remain explicit capability values; checking does not compute contained or propagated effect masks. Native capability grants and the separate test-receipt eligibility proof are unchanged.
- [PROPERTY-TEST-1] A discovered `test_` function with data parameters and no cases-file `arguments` is a property test: `selka test` generates an input for every data parameter from its checked type, supplies the deterministic stand-ins for its capability parameters as for any test, and passes when every one of `--property-runs N` inputs (default 100, at most 1,000,000) returns `True` or `Ok(True)` without trapping; `False`, `Ok(False)`, `Err(...)` and any trap other than a discard fail it. Inputs are a pure function of the seed — the test's `Entropy` seed, the SHA-256 prefix of its qualified name unless `--seed N` names one — and the run number, drawn by a SplitMix64 stream per run; run sizes grow from 0 on the first run to 100 on the last (a collection holds at most the size at the top level and half of it per level down, and an input stops growing collections past 4096 nodes). Integers draw three in eight from the edges of their width (0, 1, -1, the minimum and maximum, a power of two, one below it and its negation), one in eight uniformly over the width and the rest near zero; `bool`, `float` (both zeros, both infinities, NaN, the extreme finite and subnormal magnitudes, and small binary fractions), `str` (seven scalars in ten printable ASCII, plus controls, well-known non-ASCII scalars and scalars from the whole range), `bytes`, `list[T]`, `dict[K, V]` and `set[T]` (a repeated key or element is dropped), fixed arrays, enum-indexed tables, `Option[T]`, `Result[T, E]`, records fieldwise and unions casewise all generate. Source tuple parameters are retired under TUPLE-REMOVED-1; retained internal tuple or scalar-domain descriptors do not make retired source types admissible. A parameter whose type reaches a `class`, a retained internal domain descriptor (other than `float`), a function value, `Host` or a type the schema does not describe is a configuration error that fails the test naming the parameter, its type and the reason, and so is a property test whose result is not `bool` or `Result[bool, E]`. A cases-file entry with `arguments` still runs the test with exactly those arguments.
- [PROPERTY-TEST-2] A property test's preconditions filter its inputs: `R_REQUIRES` with the test itself as the innermost frame discards the input, while a precondition broken inside a function it calls fails the test. Copied inputs also validate record invariants at the native boundary (`CONTRACT-DECODE-1`); `R_INVARIANT` from a compiler-generated boundary callback discards a generated input. A test retains an entry prologue that checks invariants in parameters, nested fields, collection elements and keys, arrays and union cases before its own preconditions; a valid guest-constructed value passes it. Discarded inputs are replaced on the same size schedule; after more than ten times the requested runs are discarded, the test fails with `too many discarded inputs` and the most frequent rejecting clause. An explicit cases-file input that breaks an invariant fails with `R_INVARIANT`.
- [PROPERTY-TEST-3] The first failing input is shrunk greedily: each parameter in turn is replaced by the first of its simpler candidates, tried in order, that still fails (for any reason other than a discard), until a pass over every parameter changes nothing or 2,000 candidates have run. Integers shrink toward 0 (0, then the positive of a negative, then halving the distance: 100 offers 0, 50, 75, 88, 94, 97, 99); floats toward `0.0` by their whole part, halved exponents and the integer series; strings, bytes, lists, sets and dictionaries by dropping every element, then halves, quarters and single elements, then (for integer lists) moving an element's value into its neighbour, then every slot shrunk together and one at a time breadth first — never into a set element or dictionary key another slot holds; records fieldwise; enums and unions toward earlier cases at their simplest; `Option` toward `None`. The failure reports `falsified after N passing runs (seed S)`, the minimal input as one selka literal per parameter (`xs = [100]`, `Point(x=49, y=1i32)`, `Some("aaaa")`, `0.0 / 0.0`), the original input with its run number, the reason (`returned False`, `returned Err(...)` followed by that input's error return trace (ERROR-TRACE-3), or a trap's code, message and frames), the original input's own reason when it differs, and `reproduce: selka test ENTRY --filter 'NAME' --seed S`; an input that ran past `--timeout-ms` is reported unshrunk. `--json` adds a `property` object to every property test's result: `seed`, `counts` (`runs`, `passed`, `discarded`, `processes`, `shrinks`, `attempts`) and, for a falsified one, `failure` with the minimal and original inputs as typed JSON `arguments` and `literals`, the run, the reason and the reproduction.
- [PROPERTY-TEST-4] Property inputs run in batches: one native harness process makes one copied-value call per input and flushes each input's answer — its value or trap, captured output and error return trace — as it finishes, so a passing 100-run property costs one process launch; every property's first batch runs concurrently beside the others, and replacements for discards and shrink candidates run in further batches, several properties at a time. A child that dies or times out mid-batch keeps the answers of the inputs before the one it was running; that input is rerun alone in its own process (a failure if that process dies too), and the rest of the batch runs in a new one. A passing property's receipt identity adds its seed, its run count and the generator version to the test's, so an unchanged property reports `cached`, and changing `--seed`, `--property-runs` or the generator runs it again; a failure is never reused. A directory run forwards `--property-runs`.
- [PROPERTY-TEST-5] The copied-value codec boundary carries `float` as one scalar node of its IEEE bits (typed JSON: the shortest decimal with a `.`, or `inf`, `-inf`, `nan`), `bytes` as kind `bytes` with one `u8` node per octet, and a dictionary or a set of any hashable key as kind `dict` naming its key and value (a set's value is unit and carries no node): one node counting the entries, then each entry's key tree and, for a dictionary, its value tree, in iteration order (typed JSON: an array of `[key, value]` pairs, or of elements). A repeated key in a request is refused as `InvalidArgument`. The checked schema names a dictionary's or a set's `key`, and marks a `class` record with `class: true`.
- [PROPERTY-TEST-6] A property-test parameter of type `byte_buffer` — directly or inside a record, list or `Option` — generates, shrinks and crosses the copied-value boundary as `bytes`, and each input reaches the test as its own buffer, so appending to a copy leaves the parameter unchanged. A counterexample, and a buffer returned in `Err(...)`, renders it as `byte_buffer(b"...")`. A type the boundary adapters cannot carry fails the test's build instead of crashing `selka test`.
- [NEW-CORE-PROPERTY-CAMPAIGN-1] Opt-in `selka test --property-policy indexed-v1` generates each ordinal independently of the requested passing-run budget, using the legacy 100-run size schedule repeatedly; the default legacy policy is unchanged. Eligible deterministic properties may restore a validated completed prefix and extend it in bounded batches. Both passing and discarded inputs consume ordinals; generated but unclassified inputs do not advance committed progress. Campaign identity binds the target dependency closure, schema, generator/shrinker policy, seed, compiler, planner, providers, environment and execution limits while excluding only the indexed passing-run budget. Checksummed bounded prefixes publish atomically under a per-campaign lock after source/tool validation; stale or conflicting writers cannot regress progress. Missing, damaged, incompatible or inapplicable evidence recomputes. No incomplete or failing batch publishes passing progress, and a retained prefix never certifies an unfinished campaign as passed. Smaller budgets may run fresh. Cancellation recovery reuses only committed process-crash progress; cache eviction remains permitted and power-loss durability is not promised. JSON distinguishes reused cases and fresh classification from shrinking attempts, and failure reproduction names the indexed policy. Force, fact verification, leak checking and untracked effects retain freshness requirements.

## Guest performance benchmarks


## Guest memory ceilings

- [MEMORY-CEILING-1] Every process that runs guest code runs under a byte ceiling. The soft ceiling is the managed heap's own budget: an allocation that would exceed it after a collection traps with `R_MEMORY_BUDGET`, "memory budget exhausted", and exit status 1, rather than growing. The ceiling travels in `SELKA_MEMORY_LIMIT` as bytes with an optional `K`, `M` or `G` suffix, so no spawner needs a new argument ABI and a program started by hand under an exported variable is bounded the same way; malformed text refuses the run instead of leaving it unbounded, and zero removes the ceiling. `--max-memory=BYTES` on `run`, `build --run` and `test` outranks the variable, which outranks the built-in default: 512 MiB for one test child, 4 GiB for `run` and `build --run`. A resolved test ceiling is part of a receipt key, so changing it re-executes rather than reuses.
- [BUDGET-HINT-1] A native budget trap reports the flag that raises it instead of a generic failure: `R_STEP_LIMIT` answers "instruction budget exhausted; raise it with --max-steps=N" and `R_FRAME_LIMIT` answers "call-depth budget exhausted; raise it with --max-frames=N", with or without a located guest trace, in text and in the message field of `--diagnostics=json`. Every other runtime code keeps the text it had, and `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` still answers "memory budget exhausted".

- [RUNTIME-UNBOUNDED-1] `selka run`, `selka build --run` and a binary built by `selka build` count instructions and call depth without a default ceiling: a program is stopped only by `--max-steps=N`, `--max-frames=N`, the byte ceiling, or `--timeout=SECONDS`. An embedded library run with zeroed `SelkaRunOptions` is unbounded the same way. `selka test` keeps its per-test guard of 1,000,000 instructions and 1,024 frames.

- [RUNTIME-TIMEOUT-1] `--timeout=SECONDS` on `selka run` and `selka build --run`, or in a built binary's `SELKA_OPTIONS` ([NATIVE-ARGV-1]), arms a monotonic wall-clock timer inside the guest; when it fires the process writes `R_TIMEOUT: wall-clock budget exhausted; raise it with --timeout=SECONDS` to standard error and exits 1. There is no default timeout, and a zero, empty or non-numeric value is a usage error.
- [MEMORY-CEILING-2] Behind the soft ceiling the runtime caps its own address space with `RLIMIT_AS` at the budget plus fixed headroom, before its first allocation, so a runtime defect cannot outgrow the budget. It never loosens a tighter ceiling already in force and installs none under a sanitizer, whose shadow mapping reserves more address space than any budget allows. The compiler bounds itself the same way at start-up, at 8 GiB unless `SELKA_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT` says otherwise, and the tools a native build spawns complete under the inherited ceiling. Every host that runs a guest in its own process — the byte runner, the board trial and the schematic renderer — installs that same ceiling before it runs anything, because there a runaway guest is a runaway host.
- [MEMORY-CEILING-3] A directory test run fans out over the cores the machine has, further limited by how many per-child ceilings its reported free memory holds, so a cold run on a loaded machine waits instead of oversubscribing. `--jobs N` outranks `SELKA_JOBS`, which outranks that derived width; zero and non-numeric widths are rejected, the width never exceeds the programs there are to run, and it is never less than one.
- [RUNTIME-HEAP-RETAIN-1] A program's entry (`myl_core_start`) sets the C library's `M_MMAP_THRESHOLD` to 4 MiB and `M_TRIM_THRESHOLD` to 32 MiB once, so a request-shaped program (a few blocks of tens of kibibytes allocated and freed per request) reuses heap pages instead of handing the heap top back to the operating system inside every `free` and faulting zeroed pages in again: an HTTP server answering 64 KiB files went from 33.1 minor faults and two `brk` calls per request to none. Free memory above 32 MiB is still returned, and a block of 4 MiB or more is still mapped on its own, so a large list keeps growing by `mremap` instead of by copying. The managed-heap byte ceiling (`R_MEMORY_BUDGET`), the `RLIMIT_AS` backstop and the `SELKA_CORE_LEAKCHECK` live-block count are unchanged and exact, because no freed block is cached: only the C library's trimming policy differs. Test executables do not call `myl_core_start` and keep the library's defaults.
- [RUNTIME-HEAP-RETURN-1] The memory a program freed goes back to the operating system when its event loop idles, not inside `free`. `network.wait` (the poller's wait) does it when it is about to block, with no event ready, in two cases: its own limit is one second or more (or none), or the poller's earlier waits found nothing ready for one second. It does so only when at least 256 blocks of about a kilobyte or more (the runtime's large blocks) have been freed since the last time it looked, and then only when the C library reports 1 MiB or more of free memory (`mallinfo`); it calls `malloc_trim(0)`, which gives back the top of the heap and the whole pages inside it. The look and the trim happen once per 256 such frees at most, and a look and trim that took T leave the heap alone for 100 T (a fragmented heap, which gives little back, is not walked again and again); they come after one non-blocking look at the descriptors, so a ready event is never delayed by a trim, and the time they took is taken off the wait that follows. A wait that finds something ready never trims, a wait of no time never trims, and a loop that is busy (its last wait found something) never trims on a short wait. Small blocks (about a kilobyte or less), which the runtime carves from 64 KiB chunks, are never returned, only reused; a block of 4 MiB or more is mapped on its own and goes back when it is freed. The managed-heap byte ceiling, the `RLIMIT_AS` backstop and the `SELKA_CORE_LEAKCHECK` count are unchanged: only memory the C library already held free is returned.
