- [NEW-CORE-FILE-READ-1] A whole-file read of a readable regular file (`Filesystem.read_file`, `read_text`, and `File.read` without line mode) allocates the result block from the size `fstat` reported when the file was opened (at most the read's limit, 64 MiB for the whole-file forms), reads straight into it, then asks once more for end of file: 64 KiB is two `read` calls and one copy, where it was five or six `read` calls and three further copies through a stack chunk and a growing buffer. The size is only a hint: a file that grew after it was opened, a file reported empty (`/proc`, pipes, devices), a snapshot, and the test and embedded providers use the chunked path, which keeps the same limit, the same UTF-8 check (`R_FILE` status 5 for text) and the same statuses. A shorter file than hinted yields a block whose length and capacity are the bytes read. The block is charged to the memory budget when it is allocated, before the file is read.
- [ARENA-BOUNDED-1] A project's arena (every module's source, syntax and checked program) holds no more untouched address space than one 64 MiB chunk: shared chunks grow by half up to that size, and an allocation of 4 MiB or more gets its own mapping from the child allocator, which a `remap` grows in place of a copy and a `free` returns. The compiler's address-space ceiling (`SELKA_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT`, 8 GiB) therefore bounds what the frontend touches, not a geometric reservation: `std.heap.ArenaAllocator`'s half-again node growth had a cold million-line check fail with 4.2 GB resident when its next node would have crossed the ceiling. A module's tokens, which nothing reads after its parse, are lexed from the child allocator and freed at once rather than kept in the project arena. The arena's state is shared by copies of its owner and is single-threaded.
- [TYPED-RANGE-1] Checked source `for ... in range(...)` remains one `range_loop` with its binding span, start/end stores, stride setup and checked loop plan. Core lowering evaluates endpoints and runtime stride once in source order, then uses the existing loop control and overflow plan. Continue reaches the step; break skips it. Typed traversal, navigation, origins, effects, quality and persisted semantic storage understand the construct; semantic cache layout is v4. Other iteration families and unused generic symbolic/concrete obligations remain unchanged. Concrete and unused generic literal `Filesystem.dir` checks share one scoped-path predicate and preserve their located diagnostics.
- [PARSE-RECOVERY-1] Parsing synchronizes failed statements and declarations at their own newline or suite dedent, retains original source byte spans, and reports independent syntax faults. Project and module commands retain every recovered diagnostic, with each repair attached only to its own diagnostic. An enclosing dedent remains owned by its suite. Any recovered parse fails with `SourceError`; no partial AST is published or formatted, and whole-program migration fixes require a successful parse. Lexer failures and malformed import directives still stop before declaration recovery.
- [PARSE-EXACT-1] A project module's node and statement lists grow in the project arena's freeing parent while it is parsed and are copied into the arena at their exact length when the parse ends, so the arena keeps no abandoned growth buffers (three times the final lists before; the cold 500k-line frontend's parse went from 1.08 GB to 0.44 GB).

See `docs/experiments/guest-tests.md` and `tests/native_test_cli.py`.

## Installed feature identity

- [FEATURE-REPORT-1] `selka --features` reports `language-features-v1`, the compiler
identity, pinned Zig version and embedded feature/restriction manifest, without reading
its own executable. `compiler_sha256` is that identity: a build-time SHA-256 over the
compiler's source inputs, Zig version and optimize mode, embedded when the compiler is
built. The same identity appears in every receipt's `implementation`, so a receipt and a
feature report from one compiler always agree. The current reference is
`docs/current-language.md`; historical checkpoint prose does not supersede it. The
manifest records implementation coverage, not a test certificate.
- [CLI-HELP-1] `selka --help`, `-h` and `help` print to stdout and exit 0: first a pointer to `selka docs primer`, then the everyday commands (`check`, `run`, `build` and `build --release`, `test`, `fmt`, `fix`, `docs`, `reference --query`), then a "Working in a large project" block naming the navigation commands that exist (`context --symbol`, `context --at`, `tests-for`, `list-interfaces`, `test --affected --against`), then the full command reference. Any other malformed invocation still prints the reference to stderr and exits 2.
- [CLI-DOCS-1] `selka docs` answers from the user guides embedded in the executable, so a project outside the compiler repository can read them: with no argument it lists every topic with a one-line summary, `primer` first, and `selka docs TOPIC` prints that guide's Markdown to stdout and exits 0 from any working directory; `--help` prints its usage, and an unknown topic or a second argument exits 2 naming the topics. The topics are the `docs/` pages one table in `src/user_docs.zig` names (`primer`, `syntax`, `statements`, `records`, `functions`, `errors`, `text`, `bytes`, `io`, `effects`, `contracts`, `modules`, `memory`, `sets`, `stdlib`, `tests`, `build`, `navigation`, `context`, `api`, `fix`); the build embeds each one, so an edited page ships with the next compiler and a moved one fails the build. A topic's one-line summary describes the language as it is: no summary names a retired form (reference counting, `def`, `class`, comprehensions, `module` headers, effect ceilings or postfix `?`).
- [FEATURE-REPORT-2] The manifest's `implemented` section is a JSON object keyed by feature
slug. Every entry carries `kind`, either `language` or `tooling`, a one-line `summary`, and a
`doc` pointer of the form `<page>.md#<heading-anchor>` naming the documentation section that
demonstrates the feature. A slug that stops describing a real form moves to `removed` with a
date and a note rather than disappearing.
- [DOCCOV-1] `selka doc-coverage [<repo-root>]` resolves every implemented feature's `doc`
pointer against that checkout. A pointer without an anchor, a page that does not exist, a page
reached through a `..` component, a historical page whose examples the runner never
executes, and an anchor matching no heading are all reported failures.
A heading's anchor is its text lowercased with punctuation dropped, spaces turned into hyphens,
and hyphens and underscores kept; its section runs to the next heading of the same or higher
level, and headings inside fenced blocks are not headings. A `language` section must contain a
fence tagged `selka` or `my` that the documentation runner executes — one tagged
`# doc: skip` or `# doc: not-selka` does not count. A `tooling` section must contain a fence
tagged `bash`, `sh`, `console` or `text`, or an executable program fence.
- [DOCCOV-2] Every `export` of every `src/stdlib/*.selka` module must be named as a whole word in
that module's `## <module>` section of `docs/standard-library.md`, every such section must
carry an executed example, and every exported definition must have a `#` comment line directly
above it in the module source.
- [DOCCOV-3] The command prints per-page feature counts, the standard-module and export totals,
and one line per failure, then a failure count. It exits 0 only when nothing failed, 1 when
anything is undocumented, and 2 for an unusable command line. A missing or malformed
`features.json`, an unreadable page and an unlistable `src/stdlib` are reported as failures
rather than host errors. `zig build test-doc-coverage` runs it, and the default `test` step
depends on it.
- [DOCTEST-1] `selka doctest [<repo-root>] [--filter GLOB] [--report PATH] [--verbose]` walks
`README.md`, `SPEC.md` and every live `*.md` under `docs/` recursively (DOC-HISTORY-1),
extracts fenced `selka`/`my` blocks, and applies the leading `# doc:` directives (`expect`,
`expect-error`, `check`, `project`, `file`, `entry`, `skip`, `not-selka`, `allow-read`, `allow-write`).
Blocks sharing a `# doc: project <name>` are materialised as one multi-file project under a
private temporary directory; an optional `entry` selects one declared project file instead
of `main.selka`. Every unit is then checked, run, check-project-ed or run-project-ed
by the running compiler with `--diagnostics=json` and the same `--print-result` and
`--restrict` capability-grant options the documentation gate used before. `expect` compares
trimmed standard output, `expect-error` compares `diagnostic-v1` codes, and the command reports
per-document tallies, mis-tagged prose fences and failures. `--report PATH` writes a
`doc-examples-v1` JSON report, and the command exits 0 only when nothing failed, nothing was
mis-tagged and at least one executable block was found. Console/shell fences containing
source after a `cat` command naming a `.selka` file are mis-tagged: put that program in
checked `selka` fences, using project/file directives for multiple files.
- [DOCTEST-2] Units are independent, so `selka doctest` executes them on a bounded pool of
workers that pull from one shared cursor, each unit keeping its own private directory, child
process and status. The width is `--jobs N`, then `SELKA_JOBS`, then the machine's cores capped
by how many per-child memory ceilings its free memory holds — the rule and the ceiling
`selka test <dir>` fans out under; a zero or non-numeric width is a usage error. Statuses are
collected and reported only after every unit has finished, so the per-document tallies, the
failure lines and the summary are byte-identical to a serial run whatever order the units
complete in; `--verbose` progress lines interleave but each is written whole under one lock. A
host failure in one worker leaves the units the other workers hold running.
- [DOCTEST-3] A compiler child that dies in the host — the host exit status, no `diagnostic-v1`
  record, and either the CLI's host-failure marker or no program output at all — fails its
  example with a `host failure` detail even when the example expects no output, so a dropped
  example can no longer be counted as a run. A located source diagnostic, a guest that returns
  the host status after printing its result, and every other diagnosed failure keep their
  existing meaning.

- [DIAGNOSTIC-RECOVERY-1] Semantic checking recovers at ordinary and symbolic function boundaries after a source error, reports independent function faults in source order, and deduplicates repeated errors at the same source range and code. It stops within the failed function and skips whole-program verification when any body is incomplete; syntax, type-catalog, and namespace construction remain prerequisites. A failed check never publishes typed code or permits execution. Repeating a failed compilation check preserves diagnostics rather than appending duplicates.
- [DIAG-ORACLE-1] Type mismatches render the type the way source spells it, unknown fields and variants list the declared members, unknown functions suggest close candidate names, and arity errors report the expected and given argument counts.

## Proven parameter return regions

- [STANDARD-MODULES-7] Standard generic Result values retain the language's must-handle obligation; an ignored fallible result is a source error.




- [LOOP-EXIT-1] break and continue are terminating statements targeting the nearest enclosing while loop. They reject outside loops, retain must-handle obligations, conservatively preserve ownership of outer locals, and release exited owned values and nested managed arenas before jumping. Generic bodies enforce the same contracts. Loops remain conservatively fallthrough-capable for return checking.

- [CONSTANT-1] Module-level const NAME: TYPE = EXPR declarations define immutable builtin integer or bool values. Initializers use literals, scalar casts/operators and visible constants with acyclic forward dependencies; allocation and external effects are excluded even in untaken operands; SOURCE-EVAL-1 adds bounded scalar source-function calls. Checking evaluates with width/overflow/division validation and bounded depth/work before emitting literal getters. Normal short-circuit evaluation applies after syntax/dependency validation. Exports and imported names use ordinary privacy checks; generic functions may read constants.
- [CONSTANT-2] Module-level str and bytes constants require a matching text or byte literal initializer, including empty literals and ordinary escapes; calls other than `embed_bytes`/`embed_str` ([EMBED-1]), concatenation, casts and dependencies are rejected even when unused. Transparent aliases resolve normally. Their getters retain ordinary checked literals and allocate on reads, including imported and generic reads; @noalloc is checked by Core as [FRONTEND-CORE-NOALLOC-1]. Scalar constant evaluation is unchanged. Checking, run, build --run and installed executables agree, and literal edits invalidate cached semantics and emitted objects.

- [FOR-RANGE-1] for NAME in START..END iterates a half-open ascending range of matching integer types with a scoped binding the body may assign ([PY-FOR-REBIND-1]). Bounds evaluate once left-to-right; empty/reversed ranges execute no body. Continue performs the hidden checked increment before the next condition; break skips it. Generic ranges retain integer requirements and the same ownership cleanup rules as while.


- [TYPE-ALIAS-1] type NAME = TYPE declares a transparent non-parameterized alias for an existing scalar, aggregate, fixed array or concrete nominal application. Alias chains support forward references and reject cycles or declaration conflicts. They preserve target nominal identity, ownership and result obligations; they never unwrap nominal records. Exported aliases obey interface visibility checks and private aliases cannot be named across modules. Constants accept aliases of supported scalar types.



## Runtime requested-byte accounting

- [RUNTIME-BYTES-1] Embedded allocation budgets support child quotas clamped to the parent limit. Child allocations and resize/remap operations charge both live-byte limits; failed acquisitions do not charge storage; release restores live accounting and retains peak statistics. Accounting objects must outlive every allocation made through them.
- [INTEGER-EXTENDED-1] Named integer bitwise operations, shifts, wrapping and saturating arithmetic use declared widths; invalid shift counts trap identically across optimization modes.

## Explicit implementation obligations

- [TYPED-HOLES-1] `todo[T]()` declares an unresolved implementation obligation with a checked required type and a located `E_TYPED_HOLE` diagnostic plus related type span. Ordinary and uncalled generic function bodies report independent holes at safe recovery boundaries. Formatting retains them. No checked program, bytecode or executable is published until every hole is replaced. This is an explicit declared type, not inferred expected-type completion.

## Bounded host replay

## Bounded VM explanations


- [FLOAT-DOMAIN-1] Builtin float is IEEE binary64: separate arithmetic operations whose NaNs are canonical wherever they are observed ([FLOAT-NAN-OBSERVED-1]), numeric comparisons in which signed zeros compare equal and every NaN comparison is false, and checked conversion to and from signed integers. The fifteen raw `f64_*` source spellings are removed; ordinary `float` values use numeric operators and the prelude `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` operations. `src/stdlib/math.selka` reaches the correctly rounded binary64 square root through the compiler-internal `__float_sqrt` spelling because the module declares its own `sqrt`, which shadows the prelude helper.
- [INTERNAL-NAME-1] The compiler-internal `__float_sqrt` spelling is accepted only in embedded standard-library sources; ordinary source receives `E_INTERNAL_NAME` and is directed to the public `sqrt` operation.
- [FRONTEND-FORMAT-STDLIB-1] `fmt` and `fmt-module` grant the embedded standard-library source context only when the file's module name and complete bytes match the compiler's embedded source. A matching path alone never grants access to internal intrinsics.
- [SPECIALIZATION-NAME-INDEX-1] Specialization publication and replay share one request-local record-name index. Catalog growth extends it once per new record; duplicate names remain ambiguous, and every replay still checks the full provider shape. Catalog name visits are linear in the request's final record count, independent of the number of specialization references.
- [CLOCK-INTRINSIC-1] The compiler-internal `clock_now[R](capability)` and `random_u64[R](capability)` provider reads are accepted only in embedded standard-library sources; ordinary source receives `E_INTERNAL_NAME` and is directed to the `time` and `random` standard modules. The standard `time.now` and `random.from_entropy` wrappers lower to the same provider boundary, so no bytecode or native lowering changes.
- [FLOAT-INTRINSIC-FLOAT-1] The fifteen removed `f64_*` source spellings report `E_REMOVED_SPELLING`; ordinary `float` values use numeric operators, conversions and the prelude `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` operations.

- [STRUCTURAL-COMPOSITE-1] Structural field and operation signatures substitute fixed arrays and nominal applications. Result obligations remain transferred by value. Invalid unused templates still reject.



- [CONST-LENGTH-1] Generic functions and nominal declarations accept explicit const N:u64 parameters, used as array lengths, forwarded constant arguments and u64 values in function bodies. Literal lengths are bounded to 1048576 and participate in specialization identity separately from type arguments. Symbolic checking preserves unknown lengths and rejects invalid unused templates, including type/value confusion and array literals whose length cannot be proved.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-1] Supported changed-source checks reuse independently owned checked function bodies with relocated spans, nodes, statements and locals; failed checks or snapshot allocation failures do not replace the last complete snapshot.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-2] Declaration/type/import identities invalidate body reuse, and complete region/effect/codec passes rerun after every body edit.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-16] Retained-body validation orders its checks by cost and reads the source once: the dependency digest and both stored lengths reject before any source byte is touched, and the stored region — which encloses the stored body as its trailing slice — is then compared against the fresh source in one pass that decides the body too, because equal region bytes and equal body and region lengths imply equal body bytes. No persisted digest covers the body bytes, so the byte comparison stays the authority rather than being replaced by a digest, and a same-length body edit still misses as a declaration-region change while an unchanged neighbour body is still reused; an entry whose stored shape does not place the body at the end of its region keeps its own body comparison, and the reason a rejected request reports is unchanged: a length change stays a region-length change, a dependency change stays a dependency change, and a changed body or region stays a declaration-region change.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-17] The semantic store's on-disk format version is declared once, in `src/semantic_cache_format.zig`, which imports nothing: the store takes its `format_version` from it, and `build.zig` passes the same value to the CLI tests without compiling the store, so the build runner's compile closure (which the verification coordinator binds into every action's configuration identity) holds no front-end module.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-7] Ordinary checked-body eligibility is per function: an unrelated generic, alias, constant or callback declaration no longer disables reuse, and changing one ordinary body rechecks that body while unchanged dependents reuse. Entries are keyed by stable declaration identity with dependency digests over referenced declarations, types, layouts, constants, scope/import/visibility lookups including negative lookups, and mutation/return-origin contracts. Old function and record ordinals remap to the fresh catalog; missing or ambiguous remapping is a miss, never a partial checked body. Supported dynamically interned arrays, tuples and function-value types carry portable recipes: fresh child types and current declaration-owned expected slots reconstruct the types through the normal checker, and their transitive shapes must still match. Stored callable contracts never supply a fresh return-origin bound. Unsupported, malformed or ambiguous recipes remain misses. Global mutation and origin checks still rerun after every body edit so a reused caller never keeps an obsolete callee verdict.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-8] Ordinary checked bodies persist across compiler processes as versioned `semantic-cache-v6` entries. The durable header/checksum and payload carry only stable declaration identities, dependency contexts, source-region/body bytes and owned remappable checked trees; every decoded reference/range/tag is validated and remapped through C1R or treated as a miss. Only successful checks publish atomically, incompatible compiler/effects/runtime-ABI/flags/mutation-contract versions are isolated, damaged/truncated/oversized entries fall back fresh, and `--no-cache` performs no semantic reads or writes while preserving acceptance and diagnostics.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-15] Durable checked-body publication batches a bounded group of entries and never synchronizes the filesystem, per entry or per batch: a cache needs no durability, and a sync waits for every dirty page of the filesystem, other processes' included. Each entry is written as a complete checksummed image to a same-directory staging file and atomically renamed over its key, updates stay proportional to the changed entries, and a warm request that publishes nothing writes nothing. A crash or an interrupted write can therefore leave only a torn, empty or missing image, which the checksum, version, contract, identity, context and bounds validation rejects as a miss; no partial or stale image is ever accepted.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-9] A checked-body snapshot larger than the in-memory retention budget keeps a bounded leading prefix of stable ordinary bodies. Eligible bodies outside that prefix restore and remap one durable entry at a time without retaining its decoded tree; a missing or invalid durable entry checks fresh. Successful fresh bodies beyond the prefix publish to the durable store, so a warm build need not check unchanged functions merely because their combined trees exceed the memory budget. A budget that fits no body preserves the previous complete snapshot only while it still fits this request's ordinary share, otherwise releases it so the combined bound holds. Core durable retention fits loaded source bytes with saturating arithmetic and hard byte/count ceilings while retaining the 16 MiB memory limit and single-entry decoding bound.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-10] Relocating a retained ordinary body resolves exactly the declarations that body references. The remap is sized by the body's reference set, not the program catalog, and an old declaration ordinal the body did not carry is an `InvalidRelocation` miss.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-13] `check-session clear` releases every in-memory retention kind — ordinary checked bodies, symbolic templates and concrete specializations — together with their keys, indexes and arenas. Cumulative work counters stay monotonic and `functions.retained_bytes` reports the now-empty storage. Programs already returned from a cleared cache remain valid and deinitialization after clear is safe.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-14] Ordinary checked bodies, symbolic templates and concrete specializations share one `reuse.max_bytes` in-memory budget that accounts for owned entry rows, exact arena backing allocations including per-chunk bookkeeping, stable keys, and exact hash-index backing capacity including the stdlib minimum capacity, header and alignment. Newly checked publication and durable-hit promotion converge on the bound by releasing the globally oldest generic entry or truncating the ordinary snapshot to its fitting leading prefix; ordinary bodies outside retention can still restore transiently from validated durable entries, oversized entries bypass retention without changing acceptance, a zero budget disables reuse, and `functions.retained_bytes` reports the total with a per-kind breakdown and eviction count.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-11] Checked symbolic generic templates persist their direct abstract results (mutation/origin summaries, body-local diagnostics and ordered abstract call edges) across compiler processes under an explicit `symbolic-template-v1` payload kind and a stable key namespaced away from ordinary checked bodies. Replay requires byte-identical declaration region/body, a matching dependency context, name-resolution mode and abstract-checker version; scoped-bound resolution and structural validation still run on every request, and whole-graph mutation and origin validation reruns over combined fresh and replayed summaries. A changed template body invalidates its own entries while an unrelated ordinary body edit preserves template reuse; malformed unused templates are always rechecked; `work-report-v1` reports real `symbolic_templates` checked/reused counts.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-12] Concrete specialization keys are canonical over the template identity, ordered positional type/length descriptors, transitive name-ordered provider contracts, template constraint dependencies, declared mutation/return-origin contracts and the build context, with every boundary length-prefixed. Checked concrete specializations persist under an explicit `specialization-v2` payload kind in the shared versioned semantic cache store and are replayed only when the canonical key, defining declaration region/body, dependency context and build contract match and every stored typed `FunctionRef` and record reference remaps to exactly one fresh identity. A retained ordinary or specialization body may register a referenced specialization before body replay only after its template resolves uniquely, its retained declaration and body source match, each argument remaps to a unique fresh type with the same shape, and its fingerprint is re-derived under the current provider, constraint and build context. Registration uses the normal bounded instance worklist; the callee body is independently checked or replayed. Missing, damaged or ambiguous recipes, synthesized-lambda references and catalog references absent from the fresh resolved catalog remain conservative misses; resolved aggregate argument descriptors include transitive provider identity, and catalog ordinals do not define nominal identity; a self-recursive specialization remaps to its own fresh instance. Whole-graph mutation and origin validation always reruns from the combined fresh and replayed trees. `--no-cache` performs no specialization reads or writes.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-3] Cached source spans and node/statement identities must belong to unchanged mapped intervals and their function's tables; invalid relocation falls back instead of trusting stale references. Retained ordinary bodies carry path, interval and exact-byte digest proofs for referenced original files, so nonlocal default-expression locations can relocate when those files remain unchanged. Parser spans extending at most three bytes beyond the declaration region relocate only after those trailing bytes compare equal. Missing or ambiguous paths, changed external bytes and unproven spans remain misses; declaration, dependency, type-shape and mutation/origin guards still apply.

- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-4] Reusing a caller body revalidates mutation and origin contracts against the current callee bodies. A changed callee that writes a read-only collection parameter rejects with E_IMMUTABLE even when the unchanged caller body is reused; warm and cold checks agree on the diagnostic and location.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-5] Failed cache publication releases only its pending snapshot; the previous successful function queries remain reusable after OutOfMemory.
- [SEMANTIC-CACHE-6] Project queries include module paths, import/export text, signatures and type catalogs, preserving fresh/cached diagnostics and execution through source relocation and changed module interfaces.
- [CALLBACK-1] Noncapturing function values retain exact parameter/result types and mutation/origin contracts, including parameter-dependent signatures in generic helpers. Creation stores a function identity; invocation validates its target and preserves ordinary ownership and result obligations. Callable effect ceilings are retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1, and captured environments remain rejected.

- [CALLBACK-EFFECTS-1] Historical callback effect-ceiling contract, retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1. Old callback clauses remain recognizable for removal diagnostics and certain clause-erasure repairs; callback calls do not infer or enforce effect ceilings.
- [CALLBACK-SLOT-1] Historical callback slot-effect adoption contract, retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1. Function values retain type, mutation and origin checks; no inferred body-effect verdict or slot ceiling restricts a callback. `@noalloc` is the separate Core allocation assertion in FRONTEND-CORE-NOALLOC-1.

- [DEAD-RECORD-WRITE-1] Plain record values copied into an explicit `mut` local are independent writable snapshots, including copies read from collections. Ordinary parameters and loop bindings are readonly and reject field writes with `E_IMMUTABLE`; declared `mut` parameters update caller storage. A deliberately mutable local copy remains legal even when its final value is unused.

- [SEMANTIC-CALLBACK-1] Function-name values may infer callback schemas while checking bodies. Supported snapshots reconstruct these types from current declarations and require exact transitive shapes before replay; unsupported contexts remain fresh. Editing a callback implementation rechecks that implementation while an unchanged caller may reuse its tree. Fresh and changed-source callback execution retain identical signatures and outcomes.

- [STANDARD-TIME-RANDOM-1] Standard durations and Unix nanosecond instants use checked signed arithmetic that reports overflow, reversal and unrepresentable spans as Result failures instead of wrapping or trapping; seeded SplitMix64 has stable word sequences, independent snapshots when a generator is copied, caller-state advancement through `mut` parameters, and inclusive integer sampling that covers the whole signed range without modulo bias.
- [STANDARD-TIME-RANDOM-2] Standard clock and entropy calls require explicit capabilities and keep the unavailable, denied and failed provider categories apart in their reported messages; a later failed read leaves earlier results intact and host allocation failure aborts normally.
- [STANDARD-TIME-RANDOM-3] Standard provider wrappers require their explicit capability values. One entropy-seeded generator performs exactly one provider read and replays identically from its state word. Retired source effect ceilings are diagnosed by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1.

- [TYPE-ALIAS-SCHEMA-1] Exported transparent aliases retain their checked canonical target in public schemas and native interfaces, including scalar, array, chained and concrete generic targets.





## Standard library over managed types

- [STANDARD-MATH-1] Standard math classifies, rounds and roots binary64 values with IEEE behaviour preserved at zeros, infinities and not-a-number; sqrt reduces by exact powers of four and refines by Newton iteration, pow takes an integer exponent whose magnitude saturates by parity, and abs, gcd, lcm and isqrt report the values the signed range cannot hold as Result failures.
- [STANDARD-MATH-TRIG-1] Standard `math.sin` and `math.cos` accept one binary64 angle in radians and use native sine/cosine with range reduction across finite inputs. Sine preserves signed zero and subnormal inputs, cosine of either zero is one, and nonfinite inputs produce not-a-number under the ordinary float observation rules. The implementation uses compiler-owned intrinsics: raw internal spellings and forged stdlib paths are rejected with E_INTERNAL_NAME. Existing unary opcode values stay fixed; the appended trigonometric operations never enter the checked integer-conversion helper.
- [STANDARD-STRINGS-1] Standard strings add the classification, search, trimming, padding, case mapping and numeric conversion that builtin str lacks. `to_int` delegates to `int(text)` and `to_float` delegates to `float(text)`, so both wrappers use the pinned numeric grammar and parser; the float wrapper accepts surrounding whitespace, digit underscores, infinities and NaNs that its former guest parser rejected. `format_int` covers bases 2 to 36 including the most negative integer, and `format_float` rounds halves away from zero and rejects magnitudes it cannot render.
- [STANDARD-FORMAT-FLOAT-EXACT-1] `strings.format_float` rounds the exact binary value of its argument: when the scaled product `|value| * 10**decimals` rounds onto an exact half (for example `2.675 * 100.0 == 267.5`), the exact product error decides, so `format_float(2.675, 2)` is `"2.67"` as in C and Python. Only a value whose scaled product is exactly a half rounds away from zero (`format_float(0.125, 2)` is `"0.13"`), and a result that rounds to zero carries no sign (`format_float(-0.001, 2)` is `"0.00"`).
- [STANDARD-STRINGS-2] `strings.repeat(text, times)` doubles its copies instead of appending one copy at a time: the answer so far is appended to itself while its copy count stays within `times`, and one prefix of it tops up the rest, so building an answer of `n` octets copies at most about `3n` octets and takes a logarithmic number of concatenations, where the old loop copied the growing answer once per copy and was quadratic in `times`. An empty `text` or a zero count answers the empty text at once; a negative count is still `Error.Invalid`. Every answer is the same text.
- [STANDARD-STRINGS-3] `strings.Builder` is an append-only text accumulator over the same growable `byte_buffer` as `byte_builder`. `strings.builder()` starts empty, `strings.append(mut builder, text)` writes a borrowed text piece straight into spare capacity without first copying it, and `strings.finish(builder)` returns an independent snapshot that later appends cannot change, leaving the builder usable. Because only `str` can be appended, a finished builder is always valid UTF-8 and `finish` answers `str` rather than a checked result; it copies the accumulated text once. The builder does not change `str` immutability, ownership, or the byte-buffer API.
- [STANDARD-SORT-1] Standard ordering is a stable merge over a copy that leaves its input unchanged, for integers, binary64 values, Unicode code point order on text, and any element type through an integer key callback. The concrete integer, float and text wrappers delegate to the same one-work-buffer engine as `sorted`, and the keyed form to the keyed sort (STANDARD-SORT-KEY-1). Text comparison reads stored Unicode scalars directly without allocating a byte encoding. Binary search, extrema and duplicate removal report absence through Option and keep first-occurrence order.
- [STANDARD-PATH-1] Standard path operations are pure text algebra with no filesystem authority: joining honours an absolute right side, basename and dirname agree with the trailing-separator convention, splitext ignores a leading dot, and normalize removes duplicate separators, single dots and resolvable parents while keeping unresolvable ones.
- [STANDARD-CODECS-1] Standard base64 emits canonical padding for both RFC 4648 alphabets and decodes only canonical input, rejecting a length that is not a multiple of four, a symbol outside the alphabet, padding before the final group and a final group with nonzero unused bits. Hexadecimal transcoding accepts either letter case and rejects odd lengths.
- [STANDARD-JSON-1] Standard JSON parses strict RFC 8259 text into a recursive union, rejecting comments, trailing commas, leading zeros, bare fractions and exponents, unescaped control characters, lone surrogates, trailing text and nesting past a fixed limit, and naming the byte offset in every message. Objects keep parsed key order, and a repeated key keeps the place of its first occurrence with the value of its last, as Python's `json.loads` does, a number without fraction or exponent that fits the signed range stays exact, and rendering round-trips a parsed document without added whitespace.
- [STANDARD-CSV-1] The embedded `csv` module parses RFC 4180 text and renders it back. A comma is the delimiter; a quoted field may contain commas, quotes and newlines; a quote inside a quoted field is doubled; LF or CRLF ends a row (a bare CR is accepted as a line ending too); a blank line is an empty row; a final newline adds no row; a field with a quote outside quotes is refused. `parse` keeps ragged rows and does not enforce a field count; `parse_fields` reads the same documents with the same refusals and returns each field as a `Field` of `text` and `quoted`, `quoted` being whether the document wrote it in quotes, so `a,,b` and `a,"",b` differ there and not in `parse`; a byte-order mark is not removed, and an error column counts bytes; `parse_table` uses the first row as the header and an empty document has an empty header and no rows. `Error` carries `UnterminatedQuote`, `UnexpectedQuote` or `UnexpectedCharacter` with a line-and-column message reachable through `message`; an unterminated quote names the end of the document (the position after its last byte), not the opening quote. `write` and `write_table` quote only a field containing a comma, quote, CR or LF, quote a lone empty field so it stays distinct from a blank row, use LF endings and end every row with a newline. The module performs no file access or dialect selection.
- [STANDARD-HTTP-1] The embedded `http` module is a pure HTTP/1.0 and HTTP/1.1 message codec; it performs no I/O. `parse_request` accepts one request whose request line has exactly a token method, a nonempty control-free target and an `HTTP/1.0` or `HTTP/1.1` version, followed by `CRLF`-separated header fields and an optional `Content-Length` body; bytes after that body are ignored. `parse_response(data, closed)` accepts a status line, headers and a body framed by `Content-Length`, by a single `chunked` transfer coding (including trailers), or by connection close only when `closed` is true; a 1xx, 204 or 304 response has no body, and an absent length while open is `Incomplete`. A header section is limited to 65,536 bytes, 100 fields, and a body to 1,048,576 bytes; only UTF-8 header text is accepted, obsolete line folding is refused, repeated conflicting `Content-Length` values are refused, and a chunked response carrying `Content-Length` is refused. `write_request` and `write_response` validate the method, target, status and header token/value alphabets, require a declared `Content-Length` to match the body, and add `Content-Length` when a nonempty body has none. `Error` carries `Incomplete`, `Malformed` or `Unsupported` with a reason reachable through `message`; `header` and `headers` look fields up case-insensitively, and `path` and `query` split a request target. Chunked request bodies are not supported.
- [STANDARD-HTTP-2] The `http` module's parsers, name and value validation, case-insensitive `header` and `headers` lookup and serializers work on octets, with the behavior of the text implementation they replaced: a header section that is valid UTF-8 made only of ASCII is decoded once and its fields are found, validated and sliced in one scan of its octets (names against the RFC 9110 token alphabet, values against "no control octet but horizontal tab", surrounding `str.strip()` whitespace removed), and any other section takes the line-by-line route, so a message is refused for the same reason in the same order, including a request line split on single spaces, an unfolded or blank header line, a missing or empty name, more than 100 fields (the 101st), a section over 65,536 octets, conflicting or malformed `Content-Length` and a chunked `Transfer-Encoding`. Name lookup folds only ASCII capitals, so a non-ASCII name matches only its exact spelling, and `write_request` and `write_response` validate each name, value and status reason where the builder holds it (no copy) after the same `Content-Length` checks. Public signatures, records and error texts are unchanged.
- [STDLIB-ERROR-UNION-1] A standard module whose operations can fail exports an error union and returns `Result[T, Module.Error]` from every exported fallible operation instead of `Result[T, str]`; each case carries a `str` payload reachable through an exported `message(error)` helper, so callers match on category instead of the human text and adding a case is a breaking interface change. `json` and `strings` are converted; `math`, `time`, `random`, `base64` and `hex` still return `Result[T, str]`.
- [STANDARD-POOL-1] A standard pool hands out copyable generational handles into a reusable slot store. Removing a slot advances its generation so every earlier handle stops matching; a stale or out-of-range handle reports absence and no handle traps, released slots are reused before the store grows, and copies of a pool value refer to one shared store. A handle names a slot and a generation rather than a pool, so it must be used with the pool that issued it.
- [STANDARD-SORT-KEY-1] `sort.sort_by_key(values, key)` is a thin forwarder to `sorted(values, key=key)` (LIST-SORT-KEY-1), so it returns a new stable list and leaves its input unchanged. Each key is computed exactly once, in input order — earlier versions computed them in the order a recursive merge first asked for each one, so a key function that traps on several elements now raises the trap of the first such element in input order, as Python does. The extra memory is the result, one key word per element and the native sort's scratch, for lists of any length.

- [FUNCTION-IDENTITY-1] `same_function(left, right)` accepts compatible callable values and compares their process-local function identity without invoking them or contributing effects. Non-callables and incompatible signatures are rejected. Identity remains valid through stored, returned and indirect function values in native debug and release builds; it supplies no serialized address or persistent implementation digest and does not enable ordinary callable equality or hashing.
- [FUNCTION-DIGEST-1] `function_digest(callback)` returns a compiler-issued persistent identity for the actual callable and its reachable implementation, structural source schema and relevant compiler/runtime/toolchain/target/build context. Same-signature transitive edits invalidate identities; rooted unrelated implementations may retain them, and reverts recover the prior identity. Unknown indirect targets widen dependency coverage; unsupported external context or self-referential digest computation returns an empty identity and cannot authorize persistent reuse. Tests reaching identity queries execute afresh until their receipt keys bind complete finalized identity authority, including live batch membership; unrelated tests retain ordinary receipt reuse.
- [NEW-CORE-DURABLE-STORAGE-1] Durable filesystem operations require explicit native filesystem authority, reject unsupported stand-ins, preserve scoped/read-only grants, and resolve paths without following symlinks. Bounded reads require regular files and a complete size check. Atomic publication writes a same-directory exclusive temporary file, checks complete writes, synchronizes file contents, renames and synchronizes the parent; errors after rename report uncertain publication. An owned directory lock is exclusive across processes and releases on close or process exit. These operations provide the prerequisites for recoverable application journals without claiming an experimentally established power-loss guarantee. `list_directory` takes a 0..4096 entry bound, checks complete directory enumeration including read errors and refuses oversized results. Directory leases reject overlapping grants that resolve to distinct physical directories so storage fallback cannot escape ownership.
- [STANDARD-MEMO-1] The embedded Selka `memo` module retains completed allocation-only callback results for exact tagged integer, text and byte arguments. Each entry binds its actual callable, so replacing the cache's callback cannot reuse another computation's value. `create` accepts 0 through 65536 entries and reports invalid capacity through `Error.InvalidCapacity` and `message`; zero disables retention. Misses fill bounded slots and then replace the oldest insertion; hits preserve order, computation completes before insertion, and traps propagate. `Lookup` reports the result and reuse status, `count` and `capacity` expose the entry bound, and `clear` releases this cache's entries. Keys, returned managed results and copied caches keep independent value snapshots; equality-and-storable result constraints exclude callbacks, capabilities and owned resources. The limit bounds entry count rather than arbitrary payload bytes. This process-local library performs no persistent reuse or durable recovery.
- [STANDARD-HASHES-1] The embedded Selka `hashes.sha256(bytes)` computes standard SHA-256 lowercase hexadecimal digests in 64-byte input blocks with bounded working arrays and no whole-input padding copy, including empty inputs and padding across block boundaries.
- [STANDARD-CACHE-RECORD-1] The embedded Selka `cache_record` encodes string arrays as canonical JSON inside an exact versioned SHA-256 envelope. Decode rejects truncation, trailing bytes, noncanonical spellings, nonstring members and checksum mismatch. The checksum supplies integrity, not authentication; storage callers bound input reads before decoding.
- [STANDARD-PERSISTENT-MEMO-1] Selka `persistent_memo.call` persists completed byte snapshots for exact tagged `memo.Key` inputs and compiler-issued callable identities in an existing dedicated filesystem directory. It validates record identity, schema, key, canonical representation and checksum before reuse. Missing or malformed records are misses; provider failures remain errors. Zero entries forces execution without storage; unavailable callable authority and oversized results execute without receipts. Options bound entries to 0..256 and each entry's raw key plus result to 0..1048576 bytes. Directory ownership serializes publication and deterministic eviction; lowered limits trim retained entries, orphan temporary files are collected under ownership, and atomic publication errors never report success.
- [STANDARD-DURABLE-1] Selka `durable.run` accepts captured byte input, ordered byte chunks, initial byte state, a simple output filename and a pure chunk/state callback returning byte output and state. Bounds limit chunks to 1024 and input, cumulative chunks, cumulative output and each state to a selected 1..8388608 bytes. A compiler-qualified descriptor binds implementation, source schemas, captured inputs, ordered chunks, initial state, output and limits. Under an exclusive directory lease, each artifact is durably published before its digest enters the durably replaced journal. Recovery validates all committed artifacts and state links before skipping their computations; missing, corrupt or incompatible committed evidence fails closed. Uncommitted artifacts and private temporary files can be collected without deleting retained progress. Final output is atomically published from verified committed data; interruption or uncertain publication can be retried, and a published journal never substitutes for checking final bytes. Native qualification establishes process-crash recovery, not experimentally proven power-loss durability.


## Application library parity

- [STANDARD-INTEGER-EXTREMA-1] Standard math integer min, max and clamp compare exact values at every supported integer width without arithmetic overflow, including both ends of each signed and unsigned range.

## Ordinary JSON schema helpers




## Bytecode execution




- [NATIVE-TIMING-1] `run --timings` reports native execution nanoseconds on stderr, covering the guest process and the native build it needed but excluding source compilation. Timing preserves guest results, quotas and runtime diagnostics; duplicate options and unsupported commands reject it.


- [PHASE-TIMING-2] Opt-in project timing may additionally separate source/header load, syntax replay and parse, declarations/interfaces, source merge, dependency-key preparation, ordinary-body replay, symbolic templates, concrete-specialization replay, fresh body checking, effect/origin passes, warning derivation and semantic retention/durable-store I/O. These records are bounded, disabled by default, never affect cache keys, acceptance, diagnostics or work-report counts, and use `inclusive=false` for disjoint phases while only `total` is inclusive.

- [ERGONOMIC-INTEGRATION-1] Late exports, inferred generic calls and unit procedures compose across module boundaries, preserving cross-module effect and result obligations.
- [GENERIC-INFERENCE-1] Generic function calls may omit their entire type-argument list when checked call arguments and the expected result type uniquely determine every type and const length parameter. Matching follows direct variables, fixed arrays, nominal applications, and callback signatures; repeated occurrences must agree. Abstract callers retain distinct symbolic identities and declared requirements. Argument expressions are checked and evaluated once in their original order; ordinary type, effect, ownership, and structural checks remain in force. Constraints alone, defaults, and partial explicit lists do not infer missing arguments; ambiguity and conflicting inference report E_GENERIC_INFERENCE. Unmaterialized phantom type arguments require explicit specialization.
## Unit procedures

- [UNIT-1] The builtin `unit` is a distinct no-value type. Unit functions accept `return;` and implicit fallthrough; `main` still returns `i64`.
- [UNIT-2] Ordinary standalone expressions must have type `unit`; all other values require `_ =` or result handling, including generic bodies. VM calls preserve unit identity and ownership cleanup.
- [UNIT-3] Unit is described explicitly in public schemas and is rejected by data/wire boundaries and as an `extern def` parameter (a `-> unit` result is C `void`).
- [UNIT-4] Unit fallthrough and early return release owned parameters through the ordinary cleanup paths.
- [UNIT-5] Fallible values with unit success payloads retain all result obligations; a successful `try` yielding unit may appear as a standalone statement.
- [UNIT-6] Public unit-returning functions remain callable within language modules. Their schema uses `kind: unit`; copied VM/host/native ABI invocation rejects unit-containing signatures explicitly instead of interpreting unit as a scalar or capability.

## Managed storage and lifetimes

History, 2026-10-17: The former tracing-heap/arena dictionary index ownership, collection-epoch miss cache, mutable last-probe hint, packed hash/index slot, backward-shift deletion/settle protocol, refused-index scan fallback, and native segment-extension buffer path are retired implementation descriptions; they impose no current runtime or collection-timing contract.

- [GC-ROOTS-1] Explicit collection and aggregate copies preserve independent values across later mutations. Owned intermediate arguments and aggregate contents remain live through their required uses across further allocation.
- [GC-BOUNDARY-1] Managed list operations enforce element types, and an out-of-range read or write raises a located list-bounds trap.
- [GC-SOURCE-1] Managed local copies can be mutated through a `mut` helper without explicit allocator authority. Updating their source requires an explicit store-back; the original remains unchanged until that store.
- [GC-SOURCE-3] Managed results held as intermediate call arguments or inside owned records remain live through later argument evaluation and callee allocations.
- [GC-SOURCE-4] Out-of-bounds managed reads and writes produce located R_LIST_BOUNDS traps and release execution roots and backing.
- [GC-SOURCE-6] `@noalloc` is a build assertion over ownership-expanded Core allocation sites, including explicit copies (FRONTEND-CORE-NOALLOC-1).
- [GC-SOURCE-7] Managed element declarations reject owned resources, including such values nested in ordinary aggregates. Lists may store resource-free pending Results while preserving the whole-list handling obligation (NEW-CORE-ZERO-RC-RESULT-LISTS-1); dictionaries, sets and boxed pending-result storage retain their rejection boundaries.
- [GC-GENERIC-3] Directly recursive value declarations without a finite construction remain rejected. Recursive records and unions with finite cases use the immutable boxes defined by RECURSIVE-VALUES-1.
- [GC-GENERIC-4] Source formatting preserves generic list declarations and contextual uses of list as a variant name.

## Managed standard library

- [GC-GENERIC-5] Managed list elements cannot contain allocator or provider capabilities, including nested records, arrays, and instantiated generic elements; declarations reject with E_LIST_CAPABILITY.
- [GC-CAPABILITY-1] Managed element declarations reject capability values and capabilities nested in aggregates, preserving the source authority boundary.


## Managed arenas


- [SURFACE-STMT-1] A statement ends at its newline, at the dedent that closes its suite, or at EOF; two statements written on one line are separated by `;`.
- [SURFACE-STMT-2] Postfix `?` propagates result errors and unwraps success with postfix precedence.
- [SURFACE-STMT-3] `None` and `()` both spell the unit type and unit value; a bare `return` before a newline or a dedent returns unit.
- [SURFACE-STMT-4] Calls and function parameters permit trailing commas; a delimiter continues an expression across newlines.
- [SURFACE-STMT-5] Parse-only declaration listing and module formatting preserve newline-terminated module, import, export, and type directives.
- [SURFACE-STMT-6] Project discovery recognizes newline-terminated directives and declarations while allowing multiline function signatures.

## Command-line authority

- [CAPABILITY-1] `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock`, `Entropy` and `Environment` are the canonical capability types. The removed `*_cap` spellings report `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` with the corresponding type; declared or imported types of the canonical name still win. Authority stays explicit: an entry point declares what it needs by parameter type, helpers receive grants as ordinary arguments, and no numeric cast or copy mints one.
- [CAPABILITY-2] `selka run` and `run-project` grant every capability by default: filesystem reading and writing with no directory restriction, the clock, entropy and the whole process environment. `--restrict` switches the command to deny-by-default, and only then do `--allow-read=DIR` and `--allow-write=DIR` (each repeatable, up to eight directories), `--allow-clock`, `--allow-random`, `--allow-env` and `--allow-all` narrow anything; without `--restrict` they are accepted and narrow nothing. `--allow-random=SEED` pins the entropy seed in either mode, because it selects a stream rather than withholding one; an unseeded stream is seeded from the wall clock. `--allow-all` grants the clock, entropy, every environment name and read/write access to the working directory, and cannot be combined with another grant. A repeated flag, an empty-valued flag, and any of these options on a command that does not execute a program are usage errors.
- [CAPABILITY-3] Under `--restrict`, an entry point that names a capability the command line did not grant is rejected after checking and lowering but before the first guest instruction, with a message that says `--restrict` is active, names the flag that would grant the capability, and offers to drop `--restrict`; the status is 2. Without `--restrict` that refusal cannot happen. Grants for capabilities the entry point does not request provision providers that are never read.
- [CAPABILITY-4] A path on the `Filesystem` a run hands to `main` is resolved as an ordinary process resolves it: absolute when it starts with `/`, otherwise against the working directory, with `.`, `..`, doubled and trailing slashes meaning what they mean to the system. Without `--restrict` the default grant is one direct root over the working directory that applies the whole path at once and follows symlinks, and every call, the durable `filesystem` calls included, accepts what `read_text` accepts. Under `--restrict` the resolved path must also fall inside a granted directory (docs/io.md, "How a path is read"): each grant is opened once at start and known by its given name made absolute (kept only when it names the same directory) and its real path; the path is made absolute against the working directory taken at start and normalized as text, a `..` that removes a component the program wrote is accepted only when a walk without links finds that component a directory inside a grant, the longest grant name that is the path or a `/`-prefix of it serves it, and the rest is walked down from the grant's descriptor with symlink following disabled and final opens rejecting symlinks. Modes that change the tree consider only `--allow-write` grants, which also grant reading. A path outside every grant is refused as `denied: 'RESOLVED' is outside the granted directories`, a write inside only a read grant as `denied: writing needs a write grant (--allow-write); 'RESOLVED' is granted for reading only`, and a narrowed handle (`fs.dir`, `fs.read_only()`) keeps paths relative to its folder and refuses with its own text. Neither mode is a sandbox against the host: the providers run inside the process, the host filesystem tree is trusted, directory handles retain authority across renames, and directory scoping does not distinguish hard links to shared inodes.
- [CAPABILITY-5] The clock reports Unix-epoch nanoseconds from the host wall clock, with no monotonicity guarantee. Entropy is a reproducible pseudorandom stream, seeded by `--allow-random=SEED` when given and otherwise from the wall clock; it is not a cryptographic entropy source, and a program needing one must be embedded with its own provider. The environment exposes the host process environment, restricted to the `--allow-env=NAME,...` allowlist when one is given, reporting absent names as absent and denying names outside the list.
- [CAPABILITY-6] The default filesystem grant carries the process's own authority, so like C's `fopen` it opens whatever the process can open other than a directory: a FIFO, a character device such as `/dev/null`, or the `/dev/fd/N` pipe a shell's process substitution `<(...)` names, and a read waits for the FIFO's writer and reads to end of file; truncation still applies only to a regular file. Under `--restrict`, a granted directory serves regular files only, exactly as before: a FIFO or device inside it is refused with the ordinary denial, opened without waiting for a writer, and absolute paths such as `/dev/fd/N` remain outside every grant.

## Ordinary output

- [IO-1] `stream.print(values...)` writes each argument's Python rendering to the receiver stream's destination, separated by single spaces and followed by one newline. `int` and the fixed-width integers render in decimal, `bool` as `True`/`False`, `None` as `None`, `float` with a `.0` suffix when it has no fractional digits and `nan`/`inf`/`-inf` for the nonfinite values, `str` raw at the top level and `repr`-quoted inside a container, and `bytes` as a Python `b'...'` literal with `\n`, `\r`, `\t`, `\'`, `\\` and `\xNN` escapes. Lists render as `[a, b]`, insertion-ordered dictionaries as `{k: v}` and fixed arrays as `[a, b]`. Nesting deeper than 24 levels renders as `...`, so a cyclic managed graph still terminates. `stream.print()` writes only the newline.
- [IO-2] Records render as `Name(field=value, ...)` in declaration order, enum cases as `Name.case`, union cases as `Name.case(payload)` or `Name.case`, checked results as `Ok(value)`/`Err(value)` and optionals as `Some(value)`/`None`. Module qualification is stripped from the printed name. A record with a moved field prints `<moved>` for that field rather than reading it.
- [IO-3] `stream.print` accepts the keyword arguments `sep=` and `end=`, each an ordinary `str` expression that replaces the separator or the trailing newline. Positional arguments must precede both keywords, neither may be repeated, and any other keyword is rejected with `E_IO_ARGUMENT`. Printed values evaluate in source order, then `sep=`, then `end=`. Both keywords materialize the prelude `str` type on demand, so a program whose only text is a separator still checks, and a `stream.print` call without keywords needs no text schema at all.
- [IO-4] `stream.print` and `stream.write` require an explicit Stream value. Source `noio` and `effects(...)` clauses are retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1; output is not restricted by an inferred effect ceiling. `@noalloc` retains only its Core allocation assertion.
- [IO-5] `stream.print` is checked only on a receiver whose type is `Stream`; a bare `print`/`eprint` reports `E_OUTPUT_CAPABILITY` with the fix to add `out: Stream` and pass one from main, while a local binding or declared function named `print` remains an ordinary name. Capabilities, files, owned resources and callback values have no rendering and are rejected with `E_IO_ARGUMENT`. A host that supplies no output writer discards program output rather than failing, and a writer that refuses bytes traps with `R_OUTPUT`.
- [IO-7] `with` is one statement form with two resource kinds. `with arena() as name:` opens a managed region; any other expression must produce an open file handle and must name its binding. `open(fs, path, mode)` takes a `Filesystem` capability, a relative `str` path and a literal mode — `"r"`, `"w"`, `"a"` with an optional `"b"` or `"t"` — and returns `Result[File, str]` for binary modes or `Result[TextFile, str]` for text modes. Directly as the head of a `with` statement the call yields the handle itself and a failure traps with a located `R_FILE`, the way Python's `open` raises; `with open(...)? as f:` propagates the result instead, and every other position yields the ordinary result. A binary handle's `read`, `readline` and `readlines` produce `bytes`; a text handle's produce `str`, decoded as UTF-8. `f.read()` reads to end of file, `f.read(n)` reads at most `n` bytes, `f.readline()` keeps its terminator, and `f.readlines()` returns the lines with their terminators kept.
- [IO-8] `f.write(data)` accepts the handle's own element type, writes every byte by looping over the provider's partial progress, and returns the count. `f.flush()` exists for familiarity and is a no-op because writes are unbuffered. Mode `"w"` creates the file or truncates an existing one, `"a"` appends at the current end, and `"r"` neither creates nor truncates. Whole-file reads and writes are capped at 64 MiB.
- [IO-9] `read_file`, `read_text`, `write_file`, `write_text`, `listdir`, `mkdir` and `remove` each take the capability first and return `Result[T, str]`, whose error text names the operation, the path and a readable reason. `exists(fs, path)` returns `bool` rather than a result. A helper opens, uses and closes its resource within the call, so a failure leaves no handle behind.
- [IO-10] A directory granted without write authority rejects every writing mode — `w`, `a`, create, truncate, `mkdir` and `remove` — before opening anything, and the returned error says the mode needs a write grant.
- [IO-11] A file `with` scope closes its handle exactly once at every exit: falling off the end, `return`, `?` propagation, `break`, `continue`, an explicit `f.close()`, and a trap or host allocation failure unwinding through it. A method on a closed or stale handle traps with `R_FILE`; so does any provider failure during a method, since `f.read()` yields bytes rather than a result. The trap message carries the same readable reason as the `Result` error text.
- [IO-12] File operations require their explicit filesystem capability; source effect ceilings are retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1. `with` on a value that is not an arena or an open file is rejected with `E_WITH_RESOURCE`, which tells a `Result` operand to propagate with `open(...)?` or match first. A non-literal mode, an unsupported mode and an unknown handle method are rejected with `E_FILE_ARGUMENT`. Generic function bodies reject file operations. The QBE native backend runs the whole file surface too, over a native scoped-directory provider with the same request grammar, `Result` error text and located `R_FILE` traps; an open handle is one owned resource word that the ordinary local drop closes at every scope exit. A native executable grants every capability by default and `--restrict` switches it to deny-by-default with the same `--allow-*` flags, which `selka build --run` forwards after `--`.
- [IO-13] A `class` value renders its own fields wherever it is nested — a union or option payload, a record or class field, a tuple or fixed-array element, a list or dictionary element — exactly as it renders at the top level. Every generated renderer receives the address of its value's slot, and a class slot holds the body pointer, so the class renderer loads that pointer before reading any field; no renderer reads a field at the slot itself.
- [IO-14] `stream.print` renders every finite `float` in its whole positional spelling, however long: the renderer's frame holds the longest finite double (the smallest subnormal's `0.` and 323 zeros, or the largest double's 309 integer digits), so `1.2345e300`, `-1.7976931348623157e308` and `5e-324` print digit for digit as `str(x)` spells them, where a value longer than 64 characters used to leave the line unprintable and trap `InternalError`.
- [IO-15] `print` and `write` are methods of any `Stream`-typed receiver, not only of a bare name: a `Stream` held in a class or record field prints and writes through the field expression (`sink.stream.write(data)`, `pair.first.stream.print(...)`) exactly as a bound local does, with the same `output` effect, return value and lowering. Such a receiver takes positional arguments only (`sep=`/`end=` still need the stream bound to a name), and any other method on it is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` with a message naming `print` and `write` instead of the collection methods.
- [CLI-CACHE-DIR-1] `--cache-dir=DIR` (or, for the frontend caches, `SELKA_FRONTEND_CACHE=DIR`) keeps every persistent cache a command writes under `DIR`, including the ones a command's own compiler children open: `selka test` gives its `list-interfaces --contracts` child the same `--cache-dir` as its facts, check and build children, and the checked-body store and the `--explain-invalidation` baseline follow `SELKA_FRONTEND_CACHE` exactly as the syntax cache does. With no directory named the caches keep their per-project `.selka-cache` defaults. `--no-cache` leaves native objects in the default object cache, as before.
- [RUN-FLAGS-1] `selka run` and `run-project` take every execution budget in both spellings, `--flag N` and `--flag=N`, for `--max-steps`, `--max-frames`, `--timeout` and `--max-memory`, and check the value exactly as the `=` spelling is checked (a missing, zero or non-numeric value is a usage failure). A rejected `run` or `run-project` command line, including `selka run` with no file, prints the usage of those two commands, which lists each budget in both spellings, not the `selka test` usage the general text opens with; a bare `selka` still prints the general usage and exits 2.
- [RUN-FRAME-BUDGET-1] A `--max-frames` budget reserves 32 KiB of native stack per frame plus 8 MiB inside the guest's address space, which is its `--max-memory` ceiling plus 256 MiB and never more than the limit the process inherited (the compiler's own 8 GiB ceiling, `SELKA_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT`, or `ulimit -v`). `selka run`, `run-project` and `test` refuse a budget that cannot fit before any build or guest starts, with exit 2 and `selka <command>: a call-depth budget of N frames (--max-frames) reserves X of native stack (32 KiB per frame), but <guest> may map only Y: ...`, naming either the `--max-memory` that would hold it and the largest `--max-frames` that fits, or, when the inherited process limit is the cause, that limit and `SELKA_HOST_MEMORY_LIMIT=0`. The runtime's own stack mapping reports the same facts instead of a bare `OutOfMemory` for a budget that slips past the check (for example a built program run with `SELKA_OPTIONS`). The former "hard cap near 269,622 frames" was this inherited 8 GiB limit, not a size overflow. `R_FRAME_LIMIT` advises `--max-frames=N` together with a `--max-memory` that covers N x 32 KiB.
- [PROJECT-LIMITS-1] `selka.json` may carry a `limits` object with a `run` and a `test` entry, each any of `max_steps` and `max_frames` (positive integers up to 4294967295) and `max_memory` (a byte count or a K/M/G size; zero removes the ceiling). `selka run`, `run-project` and `test` read the configuration nearest the entry file and use these as defaults for the matching flag: a flag on the command line always wins, `SELKA_MEMORY_LIMIT` outranks the file's `max_memory`, and the built-in defaults apply to whatever the file leaves out. The values go through the same checks as the flags, so a configured `max_frames` the memory cannot back is refused exactly as the flag is. A directory run leaves each program to its own configuration, and a missing or invalid configuration changes nothing here because it is reported where configuration errors already are. An unknown or duplicate key, an entry that is not an object, and a zero, non-integer or out-of-range budget are configuration failures.
- [CLI-INPUT-REFUSED-1] Every command that takes a source entry (`check`, `check-module`, `check-project`, `run`, `run-project`, `build`, `test`, `fmt`, `fmt-module`, `fix`, `lint`, `api`, `invoke`, `list-*`, `emit-*`, `dump-*` and the rest) probes it before any cache opens or any work starts. A missing path, a path the process may not read and a directory where a file is required are refused with `selka <command>: cannot read '<path>': <reason>` on stderr (or the `diagnostic-v1` message form under `--diagnostics=json`), where the reason is `no such file or directory`, `permission denied` or `is a directory, not a file`, and exit 2 like every usage failure; `test` and `fix` accept a directory. `invoke` answers with its `native-invocation-v1` `setup-failure` whose `failure.diagnostics` carries the reason. A refused input creates no cache directory, and no command prints a Zig error-return trace for a host failure.
- [CAPABILITY-FIELD-METHOD-1] A capability held in a record field takes method syntax through the field expression exactly as a bound local does: `ctx.fs.read_text(path)`, `ctx.fs.open(path, "rb")` (also as a `with` head, where a direct `open` traps `R_FILE`), `ctx.fs.dir("data")`, `ctx.fs.read_only()`, `ctx.env.get`, `ctx.env.only`, `ctx.env.names` and `ctx.net.host(name)` check and lower to the nodes the local spelling builds, so capability permissions, literal-argument rules and arity errors are unchanged. The same holds for any receiver expression, such as `make_fs().dir("x")` or `pair.first.fs`. `Clock`, `Entropy` and `Host` have no methods, and an unknown method on a capability receiver is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` whose message names that receiver's own methods rather than the collection methods. Under checked ownership, a direct flat local record may borrow provider fields rooted in immutable function parameters and hold unmanaged scalar fields. This local context can be read, passed to ordinary borrowed helpers and used for scoped acquisition; it cannot be moved, copied, stored, returned, rebound or structurally mutated. Nested borrowed contexts and aggregate-parameter resource-return inference are outside this bounded rule. Independently owned provider aggregates keep whole-value transfer and direct cleanup.
- [REQUIRED-PROJECT-IMPORT-POLICY-1] A `selka.json` discovered by walking up from the entry source file to the filesystem root makes its `import_policy` required for ordinary `check`, `check-project`, `build`, `run`, `run-project`, project variants and native test preparation, evaluated before any cached success and never selected from the shell working directory. Missing or malformed configuration is a host failure (status 2); a policy violation is status 3 and language or import-cycle errors stay status 1. Policy and configuration identity participates in verdict caches and receipts so a stricter policy invalidates an earlier pass, import-cycle checking cannot be disabled, and projects without a configuration keep their prior behavior.
- [POLICY-IDENTITY-OWNED-1] The configured-policy identity that the successful-verdict caches key on is copied into memory each cache owns when it is published, and freed when the cache is dropped, so a warm `check-project` or `check-session` request under a `selka.json` replays its stored verdict exactly as an unconfigured project does instead of keying on stale stack bytes. Every request first clears the identity, so a long-lived session whose project stops configuring one never keys on the previous request's configuration.
- [SELFHOST-BOOTSTRAP-1] Ordinary source installation and component-only builds use a native Zig bootstrap coordinator without Python. It verifies the pinned seed and immutable artifacts, hashes files with bounded streaming buffers, preserves a component's selected manifest when that component fails, and can rebuild/install a component with an already qualified seed when PATH contains no interpreter or external tools. Python is permitted only in test/development drivers.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-PLAN-1] Core test receipt reachability uses the independently compiled Selka cache-plan component over an immutable complete call/function-reference graph. Each batch excludes all supplied roots and returns reached nonroot IDs sorted by digest then ID, preserving distinct equal-digest nodes. The host retains the original ordered root contributions, including repeats, and the existing u64 nonroot-count/digest encoding. Versioned frames, correlation identities, graph indices, output order and finite input/output/traversal bounds are validated; malformed, missing, corrupt, changed, failed or timed-out components fail explicitly. `SELKA_CACHE_PLAN_MODE=reference` selects the host oracle, `shadow` compares exact batch answers, and `component` is the default without automatic fallback.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-FACTS-1] Canonical pre-pass function digests and complete call/function-reference edges may be restored from independently addressable packed metadata only after validated raw discovery, current catalog, compiler, source and locator proofs agree. Stable logical identities distinguish equal-body declarations; ambiguous or unsupported metadata requests complete lowering. Missing or damaged records recompute facts, and missing deferred bodies request complete-lowering retry. Warm fact hits do not materialize unrelated raw instruction arrays; oversized edge lists use checked chunks.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-GRAPH-1] Persisted test-key candidates belong to a complete graph generation with stable logical node identities, canonical bodies, outgoing sets, exact test envelopes and ordered roots. Immutable metadata chunks publish before their generation manifest; missing, damaged or inconsistent chunks discard prior authority. Changed nodes and edges are compared after logical remapping, and retained reverse/SCC indexes require the exact current ordered topology. Only the bound planner authorizes reusing an exact derived key. Shadow mode compares every eligible derived key with independent eager lowering and canonical traversal; global checking and final source/tool revalidation remain required. Current-invocation work appears once per provider group and is absent on whole-report replay.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-PLAN-2] Incremental cache planning uses a bounded version-2 exchange while preserving version-1 batch semantics. Selka validates or rebuilds reverse indexes and recursive groups, propagates changed nodes to current callers, and reuses only unaffected candidate tests. Recursive groups share demand-driven closure work; topology changes rebuild groups, including cycle splitting. Reused tests omit closure vectors, sorting and key hashing; changed tests preserve ordered/repeated roots and the legacy sorted nonroot digest multiplicity. An independent Zig planner qualifies exact decisions, indexes and nonroot IDs in shadow mode. Malformed input, exhausted budgets and failed components are explicit errors, never empty affected sets.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-CONTRACT-1] The Wave 1 reuse contract admits only pure computation, disposable storage, successful results, cache publication and pure recomputation. Durable retention/checkpoints, run/step occurrence identities, measurement, exploration and effect/retry policies are explicit reserved distinctions and are refused until their guarantees are implemented; a compiler cache receipt never claims application crash recovery.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-IDENTITY-1] Every test replay path binds the verified planner mode/schema/implementation identity before reuse, including directory tool tickets, child expectations, whole-file reports and per-test receipts. Changing only the planner invalidates that evidence; missing or corrupt deployment refuses even an otherwise warm run. The deployment is revalidated before accepting replay and publishing receipts, and component execution uses a private sealed memory snapshot verified against the bound digest and retained until child completion.
- [SELFHOST-CACHE-REPORT-1] Test JSON results carry additive per-test cache explanations identifying the action, reason, receipt key when available and bound planner identity. Whole-report replay replaces the earlier execution explanation with the current reuse reason, and directory aggregation preserves the explanations without treating skipped or unsupported work as previously passed.
- [SELFHOST-COMPONENT-1] Informational test reachability in `tests-for` (checked and syntax), `context`, `lint` and checked `list-interfaces` uses the separately compiled Selka test-index component. Its versioned bounded binary protocol preserves discovery order, cycles and repeated direct edges; malformed, stale, oversized, missing or digest-mismatched artifacts and failed or timed-out executions produce an explicit command failure, never empty answers or automatic reference fallback. `SELKA_TEST_INDEX_MODE=reference` selects the native bootstrap oracle and `shadow` checks exact parity. Installed components are resolved beside the host, or through `SELKA_COMPONENT_DIR`; an independently pinned compiler builds them, and component-only edits rebuild the component without changing the host executable.
- [TESTS-FOR-1] `selka tests-for <entry.selka> <qualified-name>` loads and checks the project, then prints the discovered tests that reach that declaration: a `direct` list of tests whose body calls it directly, and a `reached-by` list of every test that reaches it transitively.
