It is a selection aid, not a verdict: an unknown qualified name prints a message and exits 2, and a declaration no test reaches prints empty lists and exits 0.
- [TESTS-FOR-SYNTAX-1] `selka tests-for` on a project whose bodies do not type-check still answers, exit 0 with the diagnostics on stderr, from a call graph read from the merged syntax: each call resolves by name through the calling module's own declarations and its import aliases, following re-exports, and the tests are the checked discovery rule's. The answer is the checked answer's three lines followed by a `basis: syntax` line; a project whose syntax does not parse says so and exits 1.
- [REF-DIFF-1] The commit-time rules identify changed declarations from `git diff --unified=0 <reference>` run in the entry file's own repository: the changed working-tree line ranges come from the `+` side of each hunk header, and a declaration is changed when its source line range intersects one. A failed git invocation is a host failure, and the reference revision is never recompiled.
- [CHANGE-WITHOUT-TEST-1] Under `selka lint --gate --against REF` only, every non-test function whose declaration changed since `REF` must be the direct callee of a test whose own body changed or was added in the same diff, be exempt under `test-coverage.exempt`, or carry a `change-without-test` waiver; otherwise it reports `L_CHANGE_WITHOUT_TEST` at error severity with a related note naming the tests that currently call it directly. A plain `lint --gate` without `--against`, and a baseline row under `.selka/lint/`, never suppress it, because the rule judges a diff rather than the standing tree.
- [API-DIFF-1] `selka api-diff <entry.selka> --write=FILE` records an `api-diff-baseline-v1` snapshot of every declaration's stable identity and its interface and body fingerprints; `--against=FILE` classifies the current project against one snapshot as `additive` (a new identity), `compatible` (only the body fingerprint changed), `breaking` (the identity disappeared or its interface fingerprint changed) and `unchanged`, printing a one-line summary and the changed declarations by default or one `api-diff-v1` JSON object with `--json`. It is a report and never blocks: exactly one of `--write`/`--against` is required, and a malformed or unreadable baseline exits 2.
- [API-FILE-1] `selka api` renders one `.api` artifact per project module outside the embedded standard library. Each artifact contains the generated-file header, module metadata, the first module-docstring paragraph and public declarations in source order. Accepted declarations include records, variants rendered as `enum` or `result`, resources, type aliases, constants, re-exports and functions. Function signatures preserve type parameters and requirements, `mut` and `named` parameters, defaults, return types and `fails` types. The renderer includes `@deprecated` and contract attributes with folded whitespace; it does not emit `@noalloc`, retired effect clauses or effect ceilings. Types use canonical capability names and module qualification. Rendering reads the declaration index, so body-only edits and edits to unreferenced private declarations do not change the output; identical inputs render identical bytes across cache states. Retired classes, scalar domains/codecs and named constraints are not accepted source declarations.
- [API-FILE-2] `selka api <entry.selka> --write` writes every missing or stale interface file, leaves current ones untouched, and deletes an orphaned one: a `.api` file under the entry's root, outside hidden directories and `zig-out`, that carries the generated header, belongs to no loaded module and whose `.selka` source no longer exists (a hand-written file, or one whose source exists but another entry loads, is never touched). `--check` writes nothing and exits 1 when any file is missing, stale or orphaned, printing each such module with a short unified diff from the checked-in file to the regenerated one (at most 40 lines per module) and the `--write` command to run, or exits 0 with a one-line summary. `--json` prints one `interface-files-v1` object whose `modules` list each out-of-date file with its `state` and the `added`, `removed` and `changed` declaration names (`__doc__` for the module docstring). Exactly one of `--write`/`--check` is required (exit 2 otherwise), a declaration error exits 1 with its diagnostics, and a file that cannot be read, written or deleted exits 2.
- [API-FILE-3] A `selka.json` carrying `"interface_files": true` makes the interface files part of checking the project: once its bodies check, `check`, `check-project`, `run`, `run-project` and a whole-program `emit-project-qbe` (the `build` frontend) report each missing or stale file as `E_INTERFACE_FILE_STALE` at `PATH:1:1`, naming the module and the `selka api <entry> --write` to run, and exit 1. Every checked-in interface file's bytes are folded into the configuration identity the successful-verdict caches key on, so editing or deleting one never replays an earlier success. The value must be a JSON boolean; without the key, or with `false`, nothing changes. Navigation, listing, lint and native-test preparation do not enforce it.
- [LINT-2] A `selka.json` version 2 may carry a `lint` object: `profile` (`agent` default, `strict`, `safety`), `on_build` (`report` default, `block`), `warnings` (`report` default, `deny`), positive integer `min_chars` (the `undocumented-export` docstring floor, 12 by default), `block`/`ratchet`/`report` arrays of rule names, and a `rules` object whose per-rule settings carry positive integer `max`, `hard_max` and `recover_pct`, reasoned `exempt` and `allow` entries, and `modules` globs. Unknown or duplicate keys, malformed values, a non-object `lint`, and an exemption without a name or a non-empty reason are located configuration failures (status 2); `lint` requires `version: 2`, and a version 1 document keeps parsing without it. A configured `lint` object participates in verdict cache identity exactly as `import_policy` does.
- [LINT-1] `selka lint <entry.selka|dir>` loads the project through the shared frontend, runs every enabled rule over the checked program, prints each finding as an `L_*` diagnostic on stderr and one summary line on stdout. It exits 0 when the language is valid and no finding blocks, 1 for a source, type, effect or ownership error with no rule evaluated, 2 for a usage or configuration failure, and 3 when a blocking finding exists. Without `--gate` a `block` rule is reported at warning severity and never changes the exit unless the project sets `on_build: "block"`; `--gate` raises each block finding to error severity and refuses. `--json` (or `--diagnostics=json`) renders findings as `diagnostic-v1` JSON Lines on stderr, and `--profile=agent|strict|safety` overrides the configured profile for one run. A `# lint: allow <rule> reason="..."` comment on the line above a declaration waives that rule for it; a waiver without a reason is `L_WAIVER_REASON` and always blocks. The rules are `untested-export` (report under `agent`, block under `strict` and `safety`), `undocumented-export` (report under `agent`, block under `strict` and `safety`; a missing, too-short or name-restating docstring on an exported function, record or union), `dead-private` (a private function with no call site), and the test-hygiene rules `test-no-assertion` (a test body whose every `return` is the literal `true` and which never asserts), `test-conditional` (a branch or match, or more than one loop, at a test body top level) and `test-empty` (an empty test body), all blocking under every profile. The advisory size and reach report-only rules — `function-length`, `parameters`, `nesting-depth`, `decision-points` and `dead-export` — are `.warning` under every profile and can never refuse; their thresholds come from `lint.rules.<name>.max` and default to 80 lines, 6 parameters, nesting 4, 10 decision points, and no caller anywhere in the project respectively.
- [LINT-3] `selka lint --accept` writes the baseline and ratchet stores under `.selka/lint/`, keyed on the checker's resolved declaration identity rather than message text. A baseline row `<module>|<qualified>` marks one existing finding as accepted debt: under `--gate` a blocking finding with a row is reported at warning severity and does not refuse, while a finding without a row refuses. A ratchet row `<qualified> <ceiling>` records a per-declaration ceiling for a threshold rule that may only shrink. A run without `--accept` never writes, prunes or lowers a store, baseline rows are sorted so the committed file is merge-friendly, and a store file containing merge conflict markers is refused rather than merged.
- [LINT-4] `integration-tests` (`L_TEST_CAPABILITY`) reports a discovered test whose declared parameters request `Filesystem`, `Network`, `Clock` or `Entropy` and whose module does not match the rule's `modules` globs (default `*_integration`, `integration.*`), because such a test is an integration test and belongs in an integration module. It reports under every profile and never blocks; a reasoned `exempt` entry or a matching module glob leaves the test alone.
- [LINT-5] The tests-reachability index built for `selka tests-for` is shared: every `selka lint` finding that names a declaration carries the discovered tests that reach it as a related note (a rule that set its own related note keeps it, and a declaration no test reaches gets none), and `selka list-interfaces` prints a `tests(N)` column on each exported callable when bodies were checked, where `N` is the number of discovered tests that reach it directly or transitively.
- [LINT-6] The advisory section-9 report rules ship under every profile and can never block: `record-size` (`L_RECORD_SIZE`, a record or class over `rules.record-size.max`, default 7 fields), `file-length` (`L_FILE_LENGTH`, a module over `rules.file-length.max`, default 1000 non-blank non-comment lines), `bool-ops-per-condition` (`L_BOOL_OPS`, a single branch or loop condition combining more than `rules.bool-ops-per-condition.max` `and`/`or` operators, default 3), `naming` (`L_NAMING`, a function, parameter, local, record or union that is not snake_case, PascalCase or off the vague-name list), `repeated-literal` (`L_REPEATED_LITERAL`, an integer literal other than `0`/`1` appearing at least three times in one module), `same-type-adjacent-params` (`L_SAME_TYPE_ADJACENT_PARAMS`, adjacent parameters sharing a non-capability type), `duplicate-body` (`L_DUPLICATE_BODY`, two or more checked functions with an identical normalized body, never blocking) and `output-in-library` (`L_OUTPUT_IN_LIBRARY`, a function whose checked body contains a print operation in a module outside `rules.output-in-library.modules`).

## Collection and statement surface

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list `append(value)`, `len()`, indexing and indexed assignment obey checked ownership: owning stores transfer values, reads borrow, and explicit copies preserve independent values.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-2] Record construction names its type and fields — `Box[i64](value=42)`, `Empty()` — covering generic records, empty records declared with `pass`, and trailing argument commas.
- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-3] Generic managed list methods and indexing preserve abstract element type checking.

- [SURFACE-STMT-7] Condition expressions admit record constructors inside nested call arguments; record and field names remain case-sensitive identifiers.
- [SURFACE-STMT-8] Record constructor syntax accepts nested generic type arguments, including List fields initialized with contextually typed empty lists.
- [SURFACE-STMT-9] A newline separates postfix call suffixes from completed expressions outside parentheses, while parentheses permit multiline calls and suffixes.

- [SURFACE-COLLECTIONS-4] Managed list methods reject invalid argument counts, unknown methods, mismatched element types, and noninteger indices.

## Prelude types and contextual constructors

- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-1] List<T> and Result<T,E> are available without imports or explicit declarations and use existing managed-list and fallible-result semantics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-2] Empty lists and Ok/Err payload constructors use expected binding, return, and concrete call-argument types.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-3] Record fields and managed list elements propagate their expected types into nested empty list expressions.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-4] Ok(()) constructs successful Result<unit,E> values with normal handling obligations.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-5] Generic List and Result expressions preserve symbolic type parameters through empty list creation, mutation, and Ok construction.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-6] Contextual constructors reject missing or incompatible expected types, invalid generic arity, and forbidden managed capability elements.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-7] Explicitly declared List and Result types retain their own nominal identities and contextual constructors.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-8] Builtin List and Result types share canonical identity across imported module signatures without imports of a prelude module.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-9] A module-local List declaration shadows the prelude in that module while unrelated modules continue to resolve the builtin List.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-10] Module-local functions named Ok or Err take precedence over contextual constructors in both ordinary and generic bodies, without spurious constructor diagnostics.
- [BUILTIN-CONTEXT-11] A list method's operands are expected types too: `append`, `remove` and `index` expect the element type, `insert` an integer position and the element type, and `extend` the list type, so `rows.append([])` infers the empty literal as `rows: list[list[int]] = []` does and `octets.append(200)` on a `list[u8]` types the literal as `u8`; `bytes([])` and `byte_buffer([])` infer `list[int]`.

- [MODULE-DOT-1] Direct import aliases accept dot-qualified function and type references, including generic records and imported free functions; local list methods retain their meanings. An aliased import reserves its local alias for bindings, without reserving the original unqualified module name; shadowing the actual alias remains E_SHADOWING.
- [MODULE-DOT-2] Dot-qualified imported functions can be passed as values, and generic function bodies resolve dotted module type references through their declaring module.
- [MODULE-DOT-3] Dotted qualification preserves private-symbol and import-alias-shadowing checks.
- [MODULE-DOT-4] Dotted module parsing, namespace normalization, checking, and execution release all allocations on allocation failure.

- [MODULE-DOT-5] Semantic caching recognizes dotted imported function values and keeps callback type catalogs fresh across repeated requests and imported function edits.
- [MODULE-DOT-6] An import admits the full dotted module path (`a.b.name`) and its local alias — the explicit `as` name, or the trailing segment (`b.name`) when there is none — as qualified function and type references. A bare prefix that does not continue a registered module path (`shared` when `shared.geometry` is imported) is not reserved, so a local or parameter may reuse it; only a segment that continues the path is joined. A multi-segment qualified name normalizes by splitting at its last dot.

## Python-oriented core values

- [PYTHON-CORE-1] Prelude List, Result and Option have one nominal identity shared across modules; unrelated user nominal declarations remain distinct.
- [PYTHON-CORE-2] Unit and Result<unit/int,int> entry functions adapt to the scalar runner status convention while retaining their ordinary guest signatures. The synthesized wrapper is not a source declaration, so no public interface names it and a project with such an entry emits native units like any other.
- [PYTHON-CORE-3] Representable unsuffixed integer constants inherit numeric contexts; explicit suffixes retain their types and out-of-range constants are rejected.
- [PYTHON-CORE-4] Decimal float literals and ordinary arithmetic/comparisons use binary64 numeric semantics, including equal signed zeros and unordered NaNs.
- [NUM-LITERAL-1] Integer literals take Python's spellings: decimal, `0x`/`0X` hexadecimal, `0o`/`0O` octal and `0b`/`0B` binary, digits in either case, with single underscores between digits and one directly after the base prefix (`1_000`, `0xDEAD_BEEF`, `0x_FF`, `0b1010_1010`). A spelling denotes the same value as its decimal form and keeps its type rules. A leading, trailing or doubled underscore (`1__0`, `1_`, `0_x`), a base prefix with no digit (`0x`) and a digit outside the base (`0b12`, `0o8`) are located `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` errors. A decimal integer may not start with a zero unless every digit is zero (`0`, `00`, `0_0` and `0u8` are legal), as in Python 3: `0755` is an `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` error whose message says to write `0o755` for octal or `755` for decimal, rather than silently meaning 755. Float literals keep leading zeros ([NUM-LITERAL-3]).
- [NUM-LITERAL-2] An integer width suffix (`i8` `i16` `i32` `i64` `u8` `u16` `u32` `u64`) follows the last digit directly and combines with every base: `0xFFu8`, `0b1010u16`, `0o17i32`, `1_000u32`. A suffix begins at the first byte that is not a digit of the base, and the suffix letters `i` and `u` are digits of no base, so a hexadecimal literal never swallows its suffix; an underscore between the digits and the suffix (`1_u8`) is a doubled or trailing underscore, and an unknown suffix stays `E_INTEGER_SUFFIX`. A `j` suffix (`1j`, `1.5j`) is rejected with a message that selka has no complex numbers.
- [NUM-LITERAL-3] Float literals take Python's spellings: a leading dot (`.5`), a trailing dot (`5.`, `1.e2`), exponents (`1e10`, `2.5E-3`) and single underscores between digits in the mantissa and the exponent (`1_000.5`, `1e1_0`); leading zeros are allowed (`007.5`). `.5` is a float only where no operand ends directly before the dot, so `x.5` and `f().5` keep their field-access reading, and `5.name` keeps `5` and `.name` apart instead of forming a malformed float. A float takes no integer suffix and a hexadecimal, octal or binary spelling is never a float.
- [NUM-LITERAL-4] An unsuffixed integer literal in any base takes the integer type its context expects when its value fits it, in `const` initializers as in typed locals, fields, arguments and returns: `const MASK: u32 = 4278190080`, `const LOW: u8 = 0xFF`, `A + 1` beside a `u32` constant. Compile-time function bodies use the same rule for annotated bindings, call arguments, returns and assignments. A value outside the expected type stays an `E_INTEGER_RANGE` error, and an unexpected context leaves the literal `int`. The hint that gives a literal a constant's type inspects declaration policy before resolving a known constant and looks names up silently: a name that is not a constant, such as the module in `helpers.Kind.B`, leaves no diagnostic, so comparing against a module-qualified enum case beside module constants checks as before.
- [NUM-LITERAL-5] Malformed numeric spellings are reported once, at the literal, with a stable code: `E_INTEGER_LITERAL` for a misplaced underscore, a missing or invalid digit and a decimal leading zero, `E_FLOAT_LITERAL` for a malformed float, exponent or suffix, `E_INTEGER_RANGE` for a value that does not fit its type, and `E_INTEGER_SUFFIX` for an unknown suffix. No operator is added: `**`, `<<` and `>>` keep the [PARSER-HINT-10] alternatives.
- [NUM-LITERAL-6] `selka fmt` reproduces a numeric literal byte for byte, so `0xFF`, `1_000` and `.5` are never rewritten to `255`, `1000` or `0.5`, and the checked constants in an interface listing keep the spelling the declaration wrote.
- [NUM-LITERAL-7] An array length `[N]T` and an explicit const generic length argument (`make[i64, 0x2](values)`) accept any unsuffixed integer spelling: `[16]u8`, `[0x10]u8`, `[0o20]u8`, `[0b1_0000]u8`, `[1_024]u8`. Every spelling of one value names the same type, and an interface listing prints the decimal form. A width suffix (`[2u8]`), a malformed spelling and a decimal leading zero (`[02]`) are rejected with a message naming the rule, as `E_ARRAY_LENGTH` for an array and `E_GENERIC_LENGTH` for a const generic argument; the host and 1048576-element bounds are unchanged.

## Surface syntax

- [PYTHON-1] Sources use `def`/`record`/`class` heads, colon and space-indented blocks, `#` comments, implicit continuation inside delimiters, and source-preserving virtual block delimiters. A suite opens at the `:` that ends its line and closes at its dedent; `{` and `}` are only a dictionary literal, so a brace-bodied declaration is rejected. A suite's reported bytes end at the last token it wrote, so the blank lines and comments before the next declaration belong to neither, and a function's measured length and navigation excerpt cover only the function. Missing blocks, inconsistent dedents, and indentation tabs are diagnosed. Indentation is the only block syntax, so `fmt` returns the text a program already has.
- [PYTHON-2] Functions support ordinary mutable assignment, annotated bindings, `True`/`False`/`None`, `and`/`or`/`not`, `pass`, `elif`, omitted unit return annotations, `int` as i64, and square type parameters whose omitted requirement defaults to copy. `pass` spells an empty suite wherever one is written, including a record with no fields. A `;` separates two statements written on one line, as it does in Python.
- [ATTR-1] A declaration may carry a fixed-vocabulary attribute on the line above it: @deprecated("reason"), @test, or @allow("rule", reason="…"). The parser rejects any other @name with E_UNKNOWN_ATTRIBUTE and a repeated attribute on one declaration with E_DUPLICATE_ATTRIBUTE; the contract clauses @requires, @ensures and @invariant (CONTRACT-SYNTAX-1) are the attributes that may repeat. Attributes are part of the declaration header and the formatter round-trips them.
- [ATTR-2] @test makes a zero-parameter bool function a discovered test regardless of its name or export flag; selka test, the native test schema and the W2 test rules agree on that set, and @test is reported in list-declarations and list-interfaces --contracts.
- [ATTR-3] A call to a @deprecated declaration from another module reports W_DEPRECATED naming the reason; the attribute participates in the declaration and project interface fingerprints, so removing a declaration deprecated in the baseline is an interface-compatible change once api-diff exists.
- [ATTR-4] @allow("rule", reason="…") on a declaration suppresses that lint rule for findings on the declaration and replaces the # lint: allow comment form; a reason is still mandatory (L_WAIVER_REASON). The comment form is still accepted alongside the attribute this round.
- [DOC-1] A triple- or single-quoted string literal that is the first statement of a module or of a function body is that declaration's docstring; it is inert, contributes no effect, and later standalone strings stay ordinary discarded expressions. list-interfaces shows its first line.
- [DOC-2] L_UNDOCUMENTED_EXPORT reports an exported function, record or union with no docstring, one shorter than min_chars (12 by default, counted over non-whitespace characters), or one that merely restates the declaration name. It reports under agent and blocks under strict.
- [PY-RECORD-1] `record Name:` is the canonical declaration head for a nominal value record with annotated fields and named construction. `record` remains an ordinary identifier outside declaration position, so variables and parameters may still be named `record`; `class Name:` remains accepted as the compatibility spelling during the migration window.
- [PYTHON-3] Value records use `record` (or the `class` compatibility spelling) with annotated fields and named construction; square-bracket type applications and `match`/`case` preserve contextual variant names and underscore payload discards for semantic resolution. Every explicit type argument uses square brackets, including `todo[T]()` and the capability and resource operations.
- [PY-LAMBDA-1] Expression-bodied lambdas are retired with E_LAMBDA_REMOVED. Typed recognition uses the expected callback signature to offer a certain named top-level function with explicit parameter and result types; invocation of the replacement follows the ordinary checked callback path.
- [PY-LAMBDA-2] A retired lambda without complete callback context receives no speculative helper. Recognition preserves result-type and local-capture diagnostics: a helper may read its parameters and module constants but cannot capture an enclosing function local. Use a named function or an explicit state parameter.

- [PY-VARIANT-1] `Type.case(value)` constructs an enum or tagged-union variant carrying its declared payload; `Type.case` without parentheses stays the payload-free construction and contextual `Ok`/`Err`/`Some`/`None` are unchanged. The payload evaluates once and `match`/`case` patterns keep the same spelling.
- [PY-GENERIC-1] `f[T](x)` applies explicit type arguments to a generic function, constant lengths included (`first[i64, 2](values)`), and imported free functions take them the same way: `m.make[i64, 2](v)`. An argument may name any type the lexer spells as a keyword, capabilities included: `hold[clock_cap]()`. A square-bracket application in call position names a function when the base is a declared function and a generic record otherwise, so `Box[T](field=v)` construction is unchanged and the nominal type catalog never resolves a function application as a type.
- [PY-GENERIC-2] Explicit call type arguments reach the same instance as inference, and the wrong number of arguments or an undeclared base name is rejected.
- [PY-FLOAT-1] `sqrt`, `floor`, `ceil`, `trunc` and `abs` are prelude operations on `float`, so guest code needs no `u64` bit pattern. Each takes one float and answers a float; a non-float argument is rejected, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows the operation.
- [PY-WILDCARD-1] `case _` in a `match` statement covers every variant no earlier arm named, discarding the payload of each one it covers.
- [PY-WILDCARD-2] A wildcard arm must be the last arm, must cover at least one variant, and is rejected when it would drop a result-bearing payload; that variant needs its own arm.
- [PY-SUBSCRIPT-1] A subscript index may begin with a parenthesis: `values[(n - 1) % 3]` is an index, because a square-bracket type application never contains a parenthesized group.
- [PY-RESERVED-1] `len` and `replace` are intrinsics only at the head of a call. Both name ordinary declarations, record fields, keyword arguments, members, exports and imported module members, so `strings.replace(...)`, `pool.len(p)`, `text.replace(old, new)` and a field named `len` are all spellable.
- [PY-LOOP-1] A `while True` loop with no `break` targeting it never falls through, so a function whose body ends in one needs no unreachable trailing return.
- [PY-PRELUDE-1] The `bytes` and `float` prelude types are registered wherever their names are written in source, including a variant payload, a record field and a bare annotation, rather than only where a literal or conversion appears.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-1] `sum(xs)` adds every element of a `list[int]` or `list[float]` in order, starting from zero of the element type; an empty list sums to zero and any other element type is rejected.
- [PRELUDE-FOLD-2] `any(xs)` and `all(xs)` fold a `list[bool]` with `or` and `and`, stopping as soon as `any` sees `True` or `all` sees `False`; an empty list answers `False` and `True` respectively.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-1] `min` and `max` take a list of `int`, `float` or `str` values, or two scalars of one type, and answer an element. Text order compares the stored Unicode scalars directly and contributes no managed allocation, so a text extrema fold is accepted under `@noalloc`. An empty list has no smallest element, so it traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` at the call site. Under checked ownership, list extrema borrow the selected element from the input. An explicit `.copy()` creates independent returned storage; a borrowed selection remains valid only while its owner is valid.
- [PRELUDE-EXTREME-2] `min(a, b)` and `max(a, b)` of two scalars evaluate each argument exactly once, left to right, like any other call: `max(len(xs), push(mut xs) + len(xs))` runs `push` once. The second argument used to be evaluated for the comparison and again for the answer.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-1] `sorted(xs)` returns a new ascending list of integer, `float`, `str` or `bytes` elements, leaving the input list unchanged. It uses the same stable `O(n log n)` native merge engine as in-place sorting with one reusable work buffer. Equal elements and signed zeros keep their input order, NaNs follow every ordered float, and `str` values order by Unicode code point, matching `sort.compare_strs`.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-KEY-1] `sorted(xs, key=callback, reverse=flag)` copies the list and sorts the copy exactly as `values.sort(key=callback, reverse=flag)` sorts in place (LIST-SORT-KEY-1): any ordered key, each computed once in input order, stable with and without `reverse`. The input remains unchanged, the list is positional, and `key` and `reverse` are its only named arguments, both optional and keyword-only.
- [PRELUDE-ABS-1] `abs` is total for `int`: it answers the magnitude and traps `R_OVERFLOW` on the one value with no positive counterpart, `-9223372036854775808`. A `float` argument still lowers to the prelude float operation.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-1] `round(value)` takes a `float` and answers the nearest integral `float`, with halves rounded away from zero, matching `math.round`. Not-a-number and the infinities pass through unchanged.
- [PRELUDE-ROUND-2] `round(value, ndigits)` takes a `float` and an integer and answers the `float` nearest the exact binary value rounded to `ndigits` decimal digits, a negative count rounding left of the point, with halves away from zero as `PRELUDE-ROUND-1` rounds: `round(2.675, 2)` is `2.67` because the binary value lies below the half, while `round(0.125, 2)` is `0.13` where CPython's ties-to-even gives `0.12`. As in CPython a count above 323 answers the value, one below -308 a zero of its sign, and not-a-number and the infinities pass through.
- [PRELUDE-ITERATION-1] `reversed(xs)` answers a new list of a list or string `xs` from last to first and is also a `for` source; `zip(a, b)` is a `for` source that walks two lists in step and stops at the shorter one; `list(...)` copies a list and materialises `list(range(end))` or `list(range(start, end))`.
- [PRELUDE-SHADOW-1] Every prelude builtin answers only when no local binding, declared function or module top-level name claims it, the same shadowing rule the other prelude operations follow.
- [PRELUDE-KEYS-1] `list`, `sorted`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` of a dictionary read its keys, and of a `keys()` or `values()` view that view's snapshot, in insertion order as a `for` loop visits them; a set reads its elements the same way ([SET-1]).

- [PY-COMPREHENSION-1] Both list comprehension spellings are retired with E_COMPREHENSION_REMOVED. A certain repair declares a typed accumulator and explicit scoped loops, evaluating the source first, each Boolean filter before its produced value, and each produced value before append. No comprehension execution lowering remains.
- [PY-COMPREHENSION-2] Retained comprehension recognition checks active-name shadowing, Boolean filters, storable output elements and Result-handling obligations before offering a typed repair. Explicit loops and collection writes are the supported language; nested sources and filters keep their original order in certain repairs.
- [PY-COMPREHENSION-3] A retired range comprehension receives E_COMPREHENSION_REMOVED. Its certain replacement evaluates range bounds once and preserves the element type, half-open iteration and empty-range result; a pure loop-independent fill may use lists.filled. Ordinary list(range(...)) retains overflow-safe nonnegative length calculation.
- [PY-RANGE-STEP-1] `range(start, stop, step)` counts from `start` by `step` in a `for` loop and `list(range(...))`: upward while below `stop` for a positive step, downward while above it for a negative one, and never past the end of the counter's integer type, so the last step cannot trap. All three bounds share one integer type. A literal zero step is `E_RANGE_STEP`, and a zero runtime step traps `R_ASSERT` with CPython's message `range() arg 3 must not be zero` before the first iteration.
- [PY-GATHER-1] Dictionary and set comprehensions are retired with E_COMPREHENSION_REMOVED. Certain repairs use scoped explicit loops and filters, preserving destructured iteration and nested clause order. Dictionary keys evaluate before values; ordinary writes preserve first key position and last value, while set insertion preserves first position.
- [PY-GENERATOR-1] Generator arguments and stored generator expressions are retired with E_GENERATOR_REMOVED. Certain repairs use typed scalar accumulators or explicit collection loops. Sum starts at typed zero; any/all exit every nested loop at the deciding value; empty min/max retain R_ASSERT and their original empty-sequence messages. Unsupported consumers and unsafe extraction contexts receive an explicit-loop alternative without a speculative fix.
- [PY-GENERATOR-2] A generator passed to len reports E_GENERATOR_REMOVED with an explicit counting-loop alternative. A generator argument without its own parentheses beside another argument remains malformed syntax and reports E_SYNTAX; recognizing that historical grammar does not make generator expressions supported.
- [PY-SET-OPS-1] On two integers `|`, `^` and `&` are bitwise or, xor and and (the `bit_or`, `bit_xor` and `bit_and` builtins), and on two sets the combining operators of [SET-OPERATORS-1]. They bind as Python binds them: `|` below `^` below `&`, all below `+` and `-` and above the comparisons, each left-associative, so `1 | 2 ^ 3 & 1` is `1 | (2 ^ (3 & 1))`. A `&` that follows an operand is the operator; a `&` where an operand begins is the removed argument marker, `E_REMOVED_SPELLING`.
- [PY-FSTRING-1] `f"..."` interpolates `{expression}` holes and constructs the pieces into one `str` with one exactly-sized result allocation rather than a chain of growing-prefix concatenations. A single piece returns its rendered text directly without a join or intermediate list and remains allocation-free under `@noalloc`. A multi-piece interpolation copies a borrowed text piece into its temporary part list before joining, so returning the joined result does not escape the borrow. Holes evaluate and render exactly once from left to right. A hole takes any value `print` can render — lists, dictionaries, records, variants, results, options and byte strings as well as scalars — and renders it through the same renderer, so one value has one spelling wherever it is written. A hole holds one ordinary expression, including calls, operators, indexing and a nested format string, and is checked in its own source position. `f` before a quote is the prefix; anywhere else it is an ordinary identifier.
- [PY-FSTRING-2] `{{` and `}}` stand for single braces, ordinary string escapes apply to the literal pieces, and an empty format string is empty text. An empty hole, an unmatched or unterminated brace, more than one expression in a hole, a format specification its value cannot take (`PY-FSTRING-SPEC-2`), and a value with no rendering — a capability, an open file or a callback — are all rejected. Rendering releases its partial buffer on allocation failure and reports it as `OutOfMemory`, the way every other allocating writer in the compiler does.
- [PY-FSTRING-SPEC-1] `f"{value:spec}"` formats a `str`, any integer or a `float` under CPython's format-specification mini-language, `[[fill]align][sign][z][#][0][width][grouping][.precision][type]` with the types `d b o x X n e E f F g G %` and `s`, byte for byte as CPython 3.14 answers it: fill and `<`/`>`/`^`/`=` alignment by scalar count, `+`/space signs, `z`, `#` prefixes, sign-aware zero padding that extends `,`/`_` groups, and an integer under a float type converted as `float(value)` does. A float is rounded from its exact binary value with ties to even, so `f"{2.675:.2f}"` is `2.67`, `f"{0.125:.2f}"` is `0.12` and `f"{-0.001:.2f}"` is `-0.00`; with no type it is `repr`-style. An empty specification is the value's ordinary text.
- [PY-FSTRING-SPEC-2] A literal specification is checked when the program is: one the value's family cannot take (a sign or `#` on text, a precision on an integer type, `d` on a float, `,` with `x` or `n`, a missing precision, trailing characters) is `E_FORMAT_STRING` with CPython's reason, as is a `bool` or any value other than a `str`, an integer or a `float`, a width or precision above 4095, a nested `{...}` inside the specification, the `c` type and the fractional-digit grouping CPython 3.14 added.
- [PY-SCOPE-1] Locals have lexical block scope and explicit `const` or `mut` declarations. Assignment updates an existing writable binding without changing its type. An active local, parameter or top-level name cannot be shadowed; declarations disappear when their block ends.
- [PY-FOR-REBIND-1] A `for` loop variable is rebound from the loop at the top of every iteration, exactly as in Python, so assigning it in the body — `i = 10`, `i += 3` — changes the variable for the rest of that iteration and never which iteration comes next or how many run. This holds for every loop form: `range(end)` and `range(start, end)`, both names of `enumerate`, the element of a list, string, bytes, dictionary, dictionary view or fixed array, `reversed`, both names of `zip` and of dictionary items, nested loops, and the same forms in a generic function body or a compile-time function's `range` loop. A counting loop — `range` or an `enumerate` position — whose body never assigns its variable counts on that variable itself, which is then immutable, so its lowering is unchanged and `PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1` may treat it as never negative; a body that can assign it (read from the syntax before the body is checked: an assignment, annotated binding or destructuring target naming it, or a place write rooted at it) gets its own mutable variable, copied from a hidden counter at the top of each iteration and not treated as non-negative. `continue` still advances the hidden counter; `break` still skips it.
- [PY-SCOPE-2] A declaration inside a branch or loop is unavailable after that block and reads reject with `E_UNKNOWN_NAME`. Declare a writable result in the enclosing block before conditional or iterative updates; loops may execute zero times.
- [PY-SCOPE-3] Each match statement arm owns its readonly payload bindings and ordinary declarations, which never escape the arm. To retain a result, assign an enclosing mutable binding on every continuing path or return from the enclosing function. Value-producing match expressions and yield arms report E_MATCH_EXPRESSION_REMOVED.
- [PY-STR-1] `str` values answer `strip`, `lstrip`, `rstrip`, `upper`, `lower` and `zfill(width)` with a fresh string, leaving the receiver unchanged. Case mapping covers ASCII letters and leaves every other scalar alone; `zfill` keeps a leading sign in front of the padding.
- [PY-STR-2] `replace(old, new)`, `find(sub)` (-1 when absent), `count(sub)`, `startswith`, `endswith`, `isdigit`, `isalpha` and `isspace` follow Python's answers, including `False` for every classification of empty text. Empty search/count needles follow PY-TEXT-RANGE-1; an empty split separator or replace needle traps `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR`, and these methods are rejected on lists and dictionaries.
- [PY-STR-3] `splitlines()` splits on `\n`, `\r` and `\r\n` without keeping the terminators and yields no line for empty text. `int(text)` returns `Option[int]` and `float(text)` returns `Option[float]`, answering `None` rather than raising when the whole string is not exactly one number.
- [PY-NUM-1] `int(text)` and `float(text)` accept one pinned base-10 grammar: optional surrounding ASCII whitespace, an optional `+` or `-` sign, then decimal ASCII digits separated by single `_` characters that must sit between two digits. `float` additionally accepts a `.` fraction, an `e`/`E` exponent with its own optional sign and digit run, and the case-insensitive spellings `inf`, `infinity` and `nan`. An alternate base such as `0x10`, a non-ASCII digit, an interior space, a trailing character and empty text are all `None`, and one shared vector table drives the checker and the native runtime so they cannot drift.
- [PY-NUM-2] `int(text)` answers `None` for a value the signed 64-bit range cannot hold rather than wrapping. `float(text)` is correctly rounded: a value beyond binary64 answers an infinity and one below it a signed zero, an absurd exponent saturates instead of wrapping, and digits past the rounding horizon are folded into a sticky digit that keeps the rounding the full text would have produced.
- [PY-ASSERT-1] `assert condition` checks a Boolean expression, in ordinary and generic function bodies, and traps `R_ASSERT` when it is false. The trap's source span is the checked expression and its default message is that expression's source text.
- [PY-ASSERT-2] `assert condition, "message"` reports the string literal instead of the expression text. Native lowering raises the same `R_ASSERT` code without the message, matching every other native trap.
- [PY-ASSERT-3] The message after assert may be any str expression, including an f-string; it is evaluated only when the condition is false and rendered to standard error immediately before the `R_ASSERT` trap, so a passing assertion pays nothing and a failed one names the values it saw. An allocating message contributes a static Core allocation site to the enclosing function's `@noalloc` allocation assertion; there is no frontend effect ceiling.
- [PY-DIV-1] `divtrunc(a, b)` and `remtrunc(a, b)` are prelude helpers for integer division and remainder that round toward zero, the rounding `//` and `%` deliberately do not provide. They take two operands of one integer type, trap on a zero divisor and on the signed minimum divided by -1, and a local binding or declared function of the same name shadows them.
- [PY-CONVERSION-1] A `conversion` function body constructs its destination record as `T(a=v)`, under complete source-field accounting.
- [PY-VARIANT-3] A generic variant names only its template, because `Slot[T].some(v)` cannot be told apart from an index followed by a call. `Slot.some(v)` and the pattern `case Slot.some(v)` take their type arguments from the expected type — a binding annotation, parameter, field or result — in ordinary and generic function bodies alike, and report `E_GENERIC_INFERENCE` when no context supplies them. A project resolves the template through its own module names, so a declared or imported `m.Slot.some(v)` infers its arguments the same way.
- [PY-ARRAY-1] A list literal whose expected type is a fixed array `[N]T` — from an annotation, a parameter, a record field or a return type — constructs that array rather than a managed list, and a list literal in every other context still builds a managed list.
- [PY-ARRAY-2] Fixed-array literals check their length statically against the expected array, reporting `E_ARRAY_LENGTH`, and each element against the declared element type.
- [PY-ARRAY-WRITE-1] A fixed array `[N]T` or enum table `[Kind]T` is written in place with the list spelling: `a[i] = v`, `a[i] op= v`, `a[i].f = v`, `a[i].f.g = v`, `a[i][j] = v` for an array of arrays, `r.cells[i] = v` through a value record, and `xs[k][j] = v` or `xs[k].cells[j] = v` below a list or dictionary slot. The write is `a = replace(a, i, v)` for each array step and a record update for each field step, stored back into the binding the path starts at — or into the last list or dictionary slot on the path, which is an owned place — so value semantics are those of `replace`: an explicit independent copy taken before the write keeps its value. The binding must be mutable (`E_IMMUTABLE` otherwise) and copyable. The assigned value is evaluated before the target's indices, an index is evaluated once even though it both reads and rebuilds the path, and a value that could write the binding (a call) is computed before the binding is read, so nothing it writes is lost. An index follows the fixed-array index rule of `PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1` exactly as a read and `replace` do: a computed negative index or an index outside `0 <= i < N` traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index as written and the length at the write's source position. A write whose path ends inside a `str` or `bytes`, or that is rooted anywhere other than a binding or a list or dictionary slot, keeps its existing diagnostic.
- [PY-ARRAY-WRITE-2] Augmented assignment to a field below an array element, `a[i].f op= v` (and `r.cells[i].f op= v`, `xs[k][j].f op= v`), keeps its operator: the element's field is read once through the once-evaluated path, the operand is evaluated, the pair is combined with the operator's ordinary checked rules and the result is written back exactly as `a[i].f = combined` is, the way `record.field op= v` and `a[i] op= v` already are.
- [PY-ARRAY-NEGATIVE-1] Fixed-array reads, writes and replace require nonnegative positions; computed negative positions trap R_LIST_BOUNDS with the original index and length. Constant negative positions receive E_NEGATIVE_INDEX_REMOVED. Enum tables remain indexed by their key.
- [PY-ARRAY-ITER-1] `for x in a`, `for i, x in enumerate(a)` walk a fixed array `[N]T` from index 0 to `N - 1`. A loop over an array place follows D1: later element writes are visible, and a named copy taken before the loop stays independent. A syntactic root assignment in the body and a non-place source select the entry value. The terminal needs no length guard because its length is fixed; indexed managed ancestors retain D1's exact length guards. An enum table `[Kind]T` is not walked by position and keeps `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. Generic bodies accept the same statement loops over `[N]T` with a `const N` length. Comprehensions are recognized only for E_COMPREHENSION_REMOVED and certain loop repairs.
- [PY-ARRAY-ITER-2] A `for` loop over a fixed array held in a stable local or parameter reads elements directly from that variable, including after element writes or mut-callee writes, so walking the array copies nothing. A syntactic root assignment in the body retains an entry copy. A field or indexed array place is refreshed each step, with indices evaluated once; a non-place iterable retains its entry value.
- [PY-TABLE-1] A dict literal whose expected type is an enum-indexed table `[Kind]T` constructs that table, its keys spelled `Kind.case` and its values arranged in enum layout order independently of source order.
- [PY-TABLE-2] Table literals require exactly one entry per case of their own key enum; missing cases, duplicates, non-case keys and cases of a different enum are rejected.
- [PY-VARIANT-2] Variant construction resolves through the declared type identity, so a local binding shadowing the type name keeps ordinary member resolution and a name that is not a declared case stays an ordinary call. Supplying no payload, more than one payload, a payload to an empty case, or a payload of the wrong type is rejected.
- [PY-VARIANT-4] A union case may declare named fields as `case(f1: T1, f2: T2 = default)`, with the same default-literal and required-after-defaulted rules as record fields. `Type.case(v1, v2)`, `Type.case(f1=v1)`, and positional-then-named mixing construct it, omitting a defaulted field fills its literal; a `case: T` payload and a payload-free `case` keep their existing spelling and meaning. `case Type.case(p1, p2)` binds one name per named field (`_` discards that field), a single-payload case still takes one binding, and a payload-free case takes none. Arity, unknown-field, duplicate-field, missing-required-field, pattern-arity and dropped-result diagnostics are located. Exhaustiveness, wildcard arms, result obligations, equality, layout and native lowering behave exactly as for a single payload.


- [PY-COLLECTIONS-1] Managed list literals infer homogeneous element types or use contextual list types; append, nested indexes, receiver expressions, and indexed writes evaluate operands once. Legacy writes retain receiver-first order; Python function writes evaluate the assigned value before the target. Positions follow NEGATIVE-INDEX-FIX-1 and NEGATIVE-INDEX-TRAP-1: constant negatives are diagnosed, and computed negatives trap with the written index.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-2] List slices copy the selected elements into a fresh managed list, require nonnegative explicit endpoints and clamp oversized endpoints to bounds; negative endpoints follow NEGATIVE-SLICE-TRAP-1. `len(list)` returns signed i64.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-3] For-each and enumerate evaluate a list once and retain its initial iteration bound, rejecting structural mutation through aliases before the next body or step; range accepts end or start/end. Break and continue retain correct step semantics.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-4] Contextual nested literals preserve list types across fields and nested indexes.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-5] Field assignment reconstructs mutable local records or list-slot records with receiver/index operands evaluated once, retaining value semantics for previous copies.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-6] Generic list literals, iteration, slicing and writes preserve symbolic element types and allocation effects.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-7] Receiver calls, computed indexes and assignment values run exactly once in source order and survive collection at every allocation.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-8] Structural list mutation through an entry snapshot traps during continued iteration, including continue and the final step; break exits without another iteration check. D1 live places allow growth and trap a decrease from the last observed length.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-9] Generic record field updates preserve managed alias copying and concrete value semantics.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-10] Slicing rejects negative explicit endpoints under the explicit-position diagnostic and trap rules and clamps oversized nonnegative endpoints, produces empty reversed intervals and creates independent storage, recursively copying owned element contents.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-11] Contextual types attach to complete expressions so an annotated postfix expression does not incorrectly constrain its receiver literal.
- [PY-COLLECTIONS-12] End-only range syntax starts at signed zero in runtime and compile-time scalar functions.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-13] Record field writeback preserves unrelated sibling-field updates made while evaluating the assigned value, subject to checked overlap rules.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-14] `values[start:stop:step]` copies every `step`-th element of a list or `str`, using the explicit-position endpoint rules exactly once: an omitted endpoint defaults to the end the step walks from, explicit endpoints must be nonnegative and clamp to the length, and a negative step walks backwards. The receiver, both endpoints and the step are each evaluated once, and a zero step traps with `R_SLICE_STEP`. Byte strings reject a step with `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-15] `values.extend(other)`, `values.insert(index, value)`, `values.remove(value)`, `values.reverse()`, `values.sort()` and `values.clear()` mutate the list in place; `insert` clamps a nonnegative position to the bounds; explicit negative insert and indexed-pop positions follow NEGATIVE-POSITION-METHOD-1, `remove` deletes the first equal element or traps with `R_LIST_VALUE`, and every method returns unit. `sort` stably orders integer, binary64, str and bytes elements by their scalar values in `O(n log n)` time with one reusable work buffer; NaNs follow ordered floats and equal values and signed zeros retain source order. `values.pop()` removes the last element and `values.pop(index)` removes the indexed element, both returning the element and trapping with `R_LIST_BOUNDS` when no element exists.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-16] `values.index(value)` returns the first equal element's index or traps with `R_LIST_VALUE`; `values.copy()` recursively copies owned element contents into a fresh list in the same order.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-17] Dictionary `pop(key)` removes the matching entry and returns its value, `pop(key, default)` returns the default when absent, and `pop(key)` traps with `R_MISSING_KEY` when absent. `update(other)` writes every source entry into the receiver, replacing existing values while preserving their insertion position and appending new keys at the end; `setdefault(key, default)` returns the existing value or stores and returns the default. Insertion order remains the iteration order.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-18] `values += other` extends the writable receiver in place, and the right-hand value must have the receiver's list type; checked overlap rules still apply. `d.items()` produces a live insertion-ordered view whose elements have `key` and `value` fields; iterating it with two names, `for key, value in d.items()`, unpacks those fields, while `list(d.items())` creates an independent snapshot with owned key/value contents.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-19] `push` is removed; calling it reports `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` and names `append` as the replacement.

- [PY-COLLECTIONS-20] Reading an absent list value (`index` or `remove`) traps with `R_LIST_VALUE`, popping an empty list traps with `R_LIST_BOUNDS`, and popping an absent dictionary key without a default traps with `R_MISSING_KEY`; no absent value is silently replaced by a sentinel.

- [PY-AUGASSIGN-1] `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `//=` and `%=` assign the operator's own result back to an existing binding, so `float` bindings take the float operators, `str` bindings concatenate, and integer bindings take the integer operators. `/=` on a non-float binding is rejected with `E_TYPE` because its result is a float, and a non-integer, non-float, non-`str` target is rejected with `E_TYPE` as before.

- [PY-AUGASSIGN-2] An augmented assignment writes through a list slot or dictionary entry: `values[index] op= value` and `counts[key] op= value` read the slot once, evaluate the assigned value first, apply the operator's rules to the element and write the result back through the same receiver and index, which are each evaluated once. The element type of `str` values concatenates and integer or `float` elements take their own operators.
- [AUGMENTED-FIELD-1] An augmented assignment writes through a field wherever plain assignment to that field is accepted: `counter.n += 1`, `h.inner.n -= 2`, `p.x *= 3` on a mutable record binding and `points[0].x += 5` evaluate the owner once, read the field, then evaluate the right-hand side, combine the pair with the operator's ordinary rules (integers, floats and `str` as for a binding, `/=` on an integer field is `E_TYPE`) and store the result, under the same mutability, `mut`-marker and arena rules as `target.field = value`. An overlapping evaluated `mut` argument is rejected by MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1; finish that call in its own statement before reading the current field. `+=` on a list field extends the current binding in place, as it does for a list binding, while named value snapshots remain independent.

- [CANONICAL-LEN-1] `len(value)` returns `int` for lists, dictionaries, strings, bytes and fixed arrays. The removed `.len()` member spelling reports `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` at the member and names `len(value)` as its replacement.
- [PRELUDE-SHADOW-2] A user function declaration shadows a same-named prelude operation, including `len` and `replace`; its declared signature supplies the call arity and types. Module-local and imported callable references resolve those two spellings through the same visibility checks as other names, while unrelated modules retain their builtin operations.
- [PRELUDE-TYPES-2] The nominal catalog always registers `str`, `dict`, `bytes` and `float` unless a program declares that type name. Registration depends on parsed declarations and AST use, never on scanning raw source words or comment-like operator text.
- [PRELUDE-TYPES-CATALOG-1] Unused user nominal templates remain deferred while the concrete `str`, `bytes` and `float` prelude schemas are materialized unconditionally.
- [REMOVED-SPELLING-1] Removed operation, type, capability, codec, raw-float and punctuation spellings report a located `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` diagnostic naming the canonical replacement, or state that dead punctuation has no replacement.


- [PY-TEXT-1] Managed immutable text validates UTF-8, indexes Unicode scalar values at nonnegative positions (negative positions follow the explicit-position diagnostic and trap rules), compares exact codepoints, concatenates, and splits/joins literal separators. Strings cannot be mutated through list operations. Scalar str conversions use Python Boolean/None spellings and numeric text. Python strings support Unicode escapes, codepoint slices, membership, and iteration; generic bodies preserve these types. Indexing an ASCII scalar returns a shared immortal one-character value, so a character-level scan does not allocate per index.
- [PY-TEXT-ORDER-1] `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` order two `str` values by Unicode code point and two `bytes` values by octet, lexicographically with a proper prefix first, exactly as Python does; mixing `str` and `bytes`, or ordering any other collection, is still `E_TEXT_OPERATOR`.
- [PY-REPEAT-1] Source `sequence * count`, `count * sequence` and sequence `*=` are retired under REPETITION-1. Certain repairs use the explicit repetition libraries or snapshot/count loops, evaluate original operands once in source order, yield empty values for nonpositive counts and preserve independent list elements. Library allocation and overflow behavior remain governed by their own contracts; no sequence-multiplication execution lowering remains.
- [PY-CHAIN-1] Comparisons chain as in Python: `==`, `!=`, `<`, `<=`, `>` and `>=` share one precedence level, and `a < b <= c` means `a < b and b <= c` with each operand evaluated once, left to right, and every later operand skipped once a link is false. A parenthesized comparison is an ordinary `bool` operand, so `(a < b) < c` is not a chain.
- [STDLIB-TEXT-SCALAR-1] The `strings` module exposes `ord(text)` and `chr(code)`: `ord` returns the Unicode scalar value of a single-character text, `chr` returns that one-character text for a scalar value, both reject any other length, and `chr` rejects surrogates and code points past `0x10FFFF` with the module's `Invalid` or `Overflow` reason. Both are total `Result` values under the module's declared `managed_allocation` effect.
- [PY-DICT-1] Managed dictionaries preserve insertion order and the independence of explicit copies, compare keys by value (HASH-KEYS-1), distinguish missing keys from defaults, and fail allocation or capacity growth atomically. Owning assignments transfer the dictionary; ordinary arguments borrow it. Managed data equality compares contents; dictionary equality ignores insertion order. Membership searches dictionary/set keys or a text substring; list and dictionary-values scans use explicit library functions or loops. The needle evaluates before the container exactly once. Dictionary iteration reads keys in insertion order, and checked ownership rejects conflicting mutation while the iteration borrows it; generic dictionary literals preserve symbolic key/value types.
- [DICT-VIEWS-1] Dictionary keys, values and items produce nonallocating ephemeral typed views for direct iteration and immediate len, keyed membership, list, set and sorted consumption; values-view membership requires an explicit scan. Items yield DictItem key/value records and support two-name unpacking. Iteration holds a read borrow of the iterated dictionary; overlapping value replacement or size change is rejected with E_SINGLE_OWNER_OVERLAP. Runtime size checks remain a defensive backstop. list(d.keys()), list(d.values()) and list(d.items()) create independent snapshots in insertion order, including generic checking. In checked ownership, two-name item iteration binds nonescaping borrowed key/value projections; retaining either field independently requires an explicit copy. Direct root rebinding retains the existing D1-LIVE-PLACE independent entry snapshot semantics and its copying cost.
- [D2-VIEW-STORAGE-1] Builtin dictionary-view types are rejected at parameter, return, record-field and collection-element declarations, including unused generic declarations. A dictionary view expression cannot be bound, rebound, stored in any aggregate or collection, returned, discarded, projected through its mapping field or passed to a user function (including a mut argument). Concrete and symbolic generic checking reject these uses with E_VIEW_STORED. Certain snapshot migrations carry a structured list(...) fix around the exact keys, values or items expression; direct iteration and immediate len, in, list, set and sorted remain legal. User declarations that shadow builtin view names remain ordinary values.
- [LIVE-VIEW-ACYCLIC-1] The defensive internal catalog check rejects recursive storage cycles through compiler-constructed dictionary-view referents with E_LIVE_VIEW_CYCLE. Its iterative walk follows record fields, variant payloads, nonempty fixed arrays and collection keys/elements using builtin declaration identity. D2-VIEW-STORAGE-1 rejects source view annotations before layout checking, including phantom arguments and empty arrays; snapshots, ordinary recursive values and user declarations with view names remain valid.
- [PY-BYTES-1] `b"..."` and `b'...'` produce managed immutable `bytes` with the `[BYTE-LITERAL-1]` escapes and decoded-size limit; a literal keeps the fixed `[N]u8` array reading only where the context demands one, including readonly `&[]u8` slice parameters. Managed byte literals add no unused fixed-array schema to the checked catalog, so changing their length does not invalidate unrelated lowering artifacts; explicit fixed-array contexts retain their decoded length, element type and generic inference. `len(b)` is signed, `b[i]` yields an `int` in 0..255 at nonnegative positions, with negative positions following the explicit-position diagnostic and trap rules and an `R_LIST_BOUNDS` trap outside the length, `b[i:j]` copies a clamped byte range, `a + b` concatenates, `==`/`!=` compare content, `in`/`not in` search a byte scalar or a contiguous subsequence, and `for x in b` yields `int` under the list iteration-bound rules. Byte strings are immutable: no operation mutates a receiver, and in-place appends belong to the separate mutable `byte_buffer` of [PY-BYTES-6]. Byte strings use checked ownership like `str`; construction, concatenation, slicing and conversion remain allocating operations for internal Core analysis and the `@noalloc` allocation assertion, without a source effect ceiling.
- [PY-BYTES-2] `bytes(values)` builds a byte string from a `list[int]` and traps with `R_BYTE_RANGE` outside 0..255; `b.to_list()` returns `list[int]`. `s.encode()` produces the UTF-8 bytes of a `str` and `b.decode()` returns `Result[str, str]` whose error reports invalid UTF-8 rather than raising. `b.hex()` formats lowercase hexadecimal text and `bytes.fromhex(s)` returns `Result[bytes, str]`, rejecting odd lengths and non-hexadecimal digits. `str(b)` is not provided.
- [PY-BYTES-21] `bytes.find_any` answers a one-octet set and a two-octet set with the libc bulk search instead of the general membership-table scan: one `memchr` for a single octet, and for two distinct octets two `memchr` passes from `start` whose earlier hit, if either, is the answer; a two-octet set whose octets repeat, an empty set, or a set of three or more octets keeps the bit-table scan. The answer is exactly the index the bit-table scan would report for every input, including a clamped or past-the-end `start`, a hit on either delimiter at the same index, and a high-octet set, so results and traps are unchanged.
- [PY-BYTES-22] A two-octet `bytes.find_any` searches a window at a time, the window doubling from 64 octets, and searches for the second octet only up to the first octet's hit inside the window, so a search stops within about twice the distance from `start` to its answer rather than scanning to the end of the input for an octet that is absent. A loop of searches that walks an input missing one of the two octets — `find_any(b"\n,", at)` over text with no commas — therefore costs time linear in the input instead of quadratic. The answer is exactly [PY-BYTES-21]'s for every input.
- [PY-BYTES-3] `b.split(sep)` returns `list[bytes]` and rejects an empty separator, `sep.join(parts)` rebuilds a byte string, and `b.startswith(x)`, `b.endswith(x)` and `b.find(x)` (returning -1 when absent) mirror the text operations. `b.find_any(values, start)` reads `values` as a set of octets and answers the first index at or after `start` holding any octet of that set, or -1 when none does: because `values` is a set, an empty one finds nothing and a repeated octet changes nothing; because this is a search and not an indexed read, a negative `start` traps with `R_TEXT_BOUNDS` and a nonnegative `start` at or past `len(b)` answers -1. `bytes` is a prelude type usable in `list[bytes]`, `dict[str, bytes]`, record fields and `Option`/`Result` payloads; dictionary keys remain `int` or `str`. Like `str`, `bytes` is accepted in exported module signatures and rejected at JSON, host-function and native-embedding boundaries with those boundaries' existing diagnostics.
- [PY-BYTES-5] Every byte-string operation builds and runs natively, not only construction, `len`, indexing, iteration and equality: concatenation, clamped slicing, `in`/`not in` over a byte scalar or a subsequence, `split`/`join`, `startswith`/`endswith`/`find`, `find_any` over a whole byte set, `to_list`, `bytes(values)` with its `R_BYTE_RANGE` trap, `hex`/`fromhex` and `encode`/`decode` all run natively and report the traps and `Result` error text the language specifies.
- [PY-BYTES-6] `byte_buffer` is the mutable octet-buffer type beside the immutable `bytes`: the same one-octet elements, length, indexing, iteration, content search and content equality, but its own nominal type and a growable backing whose capacity exceeds its length. `byte_buffer(values)` builds one from a `list[int]` with the same `R_BYTE_RANGE` trap as `bytes(values)`, and only it declares the seven in-place methods: `append(data)` appends all of `data` exactly as `append_range(data, 0, len(data))`, `append_byte(value)` refuses a value outside 0..255, `append_range(data, start, end)` appends `data[start:end]` and traps on a negative endpoint and refuses a nonnegative `end` below `start` or past `len(data)`, `append_utf8(text)` appends the UTF-8 encoding of a `str`, `append_int(value)` appends any `int`'s decimal spelling including the minimum signed value, `append_float(value)` appends the binary64's canonical spelling — byte for byte what `str(value)` prints, including the `.0` of an integral value and the `nan`/`inf`/`-inf` spellings — and `reserve(extra)` grows capacity for `extra` more octets while leaving `len` and every octet unchanged. Each answers a `bool` that is true exactly when it accepted the request, so a no-op success — `reserve(0)` or an empty range — answers true and writes nothing, while a refusal leaves the receiver byte-for-byte unchanged; each contributes `managed_allocation` and mutates only its receiver. An append writes the fragment's octets straight into spare capacity, growth doubles the backing with `O(log n)` allocations, and no append allocates a value for the fragment: `append_utf8` and `append_int` build neither a temporary `bytes` nor a temporary `str`, and both `append_int` and `append_float` write their spelling with no intermediate managed value at all — the float formatter builds the text on its own stack and appends it through the runtime's raw octet entry point. Length, indexing, iteration, comparison, search and every conversion read the buffer's current content, and updates affect the exclusively borrowed receiver while independent copies and earlier immutable results retain their contents. Because the append methods belong only to `byte_buffer`, an immutable `bytes` literal, slice or view can never be written through, and an immutable `bytes` receiver is rejected with `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` for every one of them. `bytes(buffer)` publishes the buffer's current octets as an immutable snapshot ([PY-BYTES-SNAPSHOT-1]), so a later append cannot change an earlier snapshot; a comparison between the two byte kinds compares their octets as content.
The retained-view contract PY-BYTES-7 was retired on 2026-10-17 with reference counting. Scoped checked views follow NEW-CORE-ZERO-RC-VIEWS-1; ordinary slices and text pieces remain independently owned values.
- [PY-BYTES-8] `b.scalar_count()` answers `Result[int, str]` with the number of Unicode scalars in the receiver's octets, validated in the same nonallocating pass instead of decoding the range: ASCII runs advance a word at a time, and every other scalar is decoded with exactly the acceptance rules of the decoder, so an overlong encoding, a surrogate, a value above U+10FFFF, a truncated sequence and a stray continuation byte all answer `Err` carrying the decoder's invalid-UTF-8 reason, while the empty byte string answers `Ok(0)`. `str` has no such method because `len` already counts its scalars.
- [PY-BYTES-9] `b.parse_int()` and `b.parse_float()` answer `Option[int]` and `Option[float]` by reading the receiver's own octets through the one pinned number grammar, so a numeric leaf of a byte string or a view of one never decodes a temporary `str`: surrounding ASCII whitespace, the sign, the digit runs with their single `_` separators, the fraction, the exponent and the `inf`/`infinity`/`nan` spellings all behave exactly as they do for `int(text)` and `float(text)`, an overflowing integer answers `None` rather than wrapping, and a byte outside ASCII is rejected exactly where the scalar grammar rejects the non-ASCII scalar it encodes. Neither parse allocates on either representation, so `@noalloc` accepts `int(text)`, `float(text)`, `bytes.parse_int()` and `bytes.parse_float()`, and a function whose only calls are these starts no collection and keeps no root protocol.
- [PY-BYTES-RANGE-1] `bytes.parse_int_range(start, end)`, `parse_float_range(start, end)` and `scalar_count_range(start, end)` inspect the clamped range from start to end without allocating a view or copying its bytes. Constant negative endpoints report E_NEGATIVE_INDEX_REMOVED and computed negatives trap R_LIST_BOUNDS; nonnegative endpoints clamp to the receiver and make a reversed range empty. Number parsing retains the existing grammar and overflow behavior and is accepted under `@noalloc`. Scalar counting returns `Result[int, str]`; its valid path allocates no managed value, but malformed UTF-8 may allocate the existing error string, Core pools fixed UTF-8 errors and the `@noalloc` allocation assertion uses actual Core allocation sites, without a frontend effect ceiling.
- [PY-BYTES-SNAPSHOT-1] Converting a byte buffer with `bytes(buffer)`, including `byte_builder.finish`, produces owned immutable bytes containing the buffer's current octets. The buffer remains usable; later appends cannot change an earlier result, including when that result is appended back to the builder. The conversion has no zero-copy or shared-storage guarantee.
- [PY-BYTES-FUSED-RANGE-1] An immutable byte slice or view consumed immediately by `parse_int()` or `parse_float()` lowers to the existing range parser before Core allocation analysis, so the canonical expression is accepted under `@noalloc` and emits no slice copy or view header. Receiver and bounds run exactly once in source order, with omitted, clamped and reversed bounds retaining ordinary semantics and explicit negative bounds following the explicit-position rejection and trap rule. A retained intermediate or a mutable `byte_buffer` slice is not fused.
- [UTF8-DECODE-1] The native bytes decoder validates UTF-8 in the same pass that counts and fills scalars instead of validating first and rebuilding second. ASCII input is one high-bit scan plus one widening pass; every other input runs the validating decoder once to count and once to fill, with no separate prior validation pass. It still rejects exactly the byte sequences the former validate-then-build pair rejected — overlong encodings, surrogate code points U+D800..U+DFFF, values above U+10FFFF, truncation and stray continuation bytes — publishes the same scalar sequence for accepted input, and still separates malformed input (`MYL_BYTES_INVALID`, reported as the `Result` error text) from a refused allocation (`MYL_BYTES_MEMORY`).

- [PYTHON-CALL-1] Declared functions accept positional and keyword arguments with trailing scalar-literal defaults; argument effects run once in source order even when parameter order differs, including inferred generic calls.
- [PYTHON-CALL-2] Calls reject unknown, duplicate, missing, excess, and misplaced arguments. Defaults require immutable scalar literals, their declared types are checked even in unused functions, and required parameters cannot follow defaults.
- [PYTHON-CALL-3] Python constructor calls resolve records by declaration identity, support positional and named fields, generic records, and empty `class` declarations using `pass`.
- [PYTHON-CALL-4] Keyword/default binding, source-order lowering, execution, and native emission release partial allocations on allocator failure.
- [PYTHON-CALL-5] Editing a default value invalidates checked caller reuse and produces fresh call behavior.

- [C-FFI-1] Explicit `extern def` declarations use fixed-arity C scalar signatures: signed/unsigned 8/16/32/64-bit integers, bool, float as C double, and unit results as C void. They reject retired source effect ceilings under EFFECTS-REMOVED-1, bypass provider registration, retain internal transitive external-effect facts, and reject borrowed/aggregate/generic signatures. Trusted host-provider registration rejects direct imports. Declaration listing and module discovery treat `extern` as a declaration head, so a newline-terminated declaration ends where the next one begins.
- [HOST-CAPABILITY-1] Every `extern func` declares `host: Host` as its first parameter. The checker requires a `Host` value at every call and the C ABI lowering drops that operand before the foreign call. Under `--restrict`, `--allow-host` grants the authority; it is granted by default like the other capabilities. A callback type whose parameters include `Host` is the only function-value route to C.
- [QBE-FRAME-ACCOUNTING-2] Call depth follows the same rule as instruction budgets: `selka run --max-frames=N` and `selka test` compile a per-call count in, an executable `selka build` publishes carries none, and such an executable given `--max-frames=` in `SELKA_OPTIONS` refuses it before `main` runs with `R_NO_FRAME_ACCOUNTING` ("run it under `selka run --max-frames=N`") and exit status 1, after RUN-FRAME-BUDGET-1's reservation check.
- [LINT-7] `untested-export` reads the shared tests-reachability index instead of only the direct callees of discovered tests: under the `agent` profile an export that any discovered test reaches, directly or transitively, is tested and produces no finding, while an export no test reaches is reported as not reached; under `strict` and `safety` the direct-call fact is kept, an export only reached transitively is reported with the wording "reached only transitively", and an export no test reaches keeps the "not called directly" wording. The rule and the related notes on `selka lint` findings read the one index `selka tests-for` answers from, built once per run, so the three cannot disagree.
- [FRICTION-LOG-1] Friction logging is opt-in and its destination is decided in one place: `--friction-log=PATH` outranks the `SELKA_FRICTION_LOG` environment variable, an absent or empty destination means off, and a command that was not asked for a log writes nothing, creates no log file, and never changes its exit status because a log could not be written.
- [FRICTION-LOG-2] When logging is enabled the compiler appends exactly one `friction-log-v1` JSON line per command, carrying only the compiler's build identity, the command spelling, the source path exactly as the user typed it, the SHA-256 of the checked source snapshot, each rendered diagnostic's code, severity and byte span, the command's exit status, and its monotonic wall time in nanoseconds; no other field and no diagnostic message text is written.
- [FRICTION-LOG-3] A friction record never contains source text, diagnostic message text, environment values, or an absolute path the user did not pass, and the log destination itself is not recorded; a session logged over a source file whose text and diagnostic message both quote a sentinel string leaves the sentinel absent from the log.
- [FRICTION-LOG-4] A friction record carries every diagnostic its command rendered, whichever layer rendered it: the checker, the ownership contract, allocation contracts, module discovery (a missing or unreadable import) and lint findings; this holds for `check`, `check-project`, `lint`, `build`, `run`, `run-project` and `test`, where `--friction-log=PATH` is consumed before the command's own options. A command that never reached an entry file (a usage error, a repeated `--friction-log`) writes no record, and a child compiler that `selka test DIR` starts writes one only when it rendered a diagnostic.
- [PARSER-HINT-1] A `union`, `enum` or `result` body whose next case sits on its own line at the case column without its separating comma reports `E_SYNTAX` at that case with the declaration's own noun — `union cases are separated by commas`, `enum cases are separated by commas`, `result cases are separated by commas` — and an `alternative` note `insert a comma after the previous case` located at the previous case. The code and the offending span are the ones the former `expected a dedent` failure reported; a token that is not an identifier at the case column keeps that former message, so this is targeted messaging at one known shape; declaration recovery may then report independent faults in later declarations.
- [PARSER-HINT-2] A bare module-level assignment (`MAX = 42`) and its annotated spelling (`MAX: int = 42`) report `E_SYNTAX` at the assigned name with the `alternative` note `declare it as a constant: const MAX: int = 42`, instead of the bare `expected def` the function head produced.
- [PARSER-HINT-3] A `def` inside a `record` or `class` body — whether it is the first body entry or follows declared fields — reports `E_SYNTAX` at the `def` with the `alternative` note `declare a top-level function taking the record: def norm1(p: Point) -> int`, instead of `expected a name`.
- [PARSER-HINT-4] A top-level `from module import name` reports `E_SYNTAX` at `from` with the `alternative` note `import the module and declare a record: import dataclasses`, instead of the bare `expected def`, so the rejection names both accepted forms before the decorator line is reached.
- [PARSER-HINT-5] The hinted declaration forms are one table: `declaration_hints` in `src/parser.zig` names the same idioms in the same order as the `hints` array in `velocity/friction/classifier.json`, each with an `E_SYNTAX` rule behind it, so the idiom suite's classification and the parser's messaging cannot drift. A unit test fails when either side gains a form the other lacks, and fails — rather than skips — when the classifier cannot be read.
