- [HEADERLESS-STD-IMPORT-1] A file with no `module` header whose leading imports all name compiler-embedded standard modules (`import strings`, optionally `as alias`) parses and loads as a single-unit entry: the imports resolve from the embedded standard library exactly as they do for a project entry, no module declaration or declared-name match applies, and the rest of the file is an ordinary program body whose declarations are checked and run by `run`, `check` and `build`.
- [HEADERLESS-STD-IMPORT-2] The headerless form is exactly that narrow: a file that begins with anything else — a declaration, an `export` directive, a dotted import path, or an import of a project module rather than an embedded one — still reports `E_MODULE_HEADER` at its first token (`file must begin with a module declaration`, or for an entry that imports a non-standard module the message of HEADERLESS-STD-IMPORT-HINT-1), and a headerless file that another module imports instead of the command naming it as the entry keeps that same diagnostic, so the module graph, its budgets and its error spans are unchanged.
- [HEADERLESS-STD-IMPORT-HINT-1] When a headerless entry's import list names a module that is not a standard one (`import heapq`, usually a Python import), `E_MODULE_HEADER` names that module, lists the standard modules a headerless file may import, and gives the header to write to import a project module instead.
- [REFERENCE-1] `selka reference --query TERM` answers an implemented feature with the manifest record the executable already ships: the slug, its `kind` and its summary are the same triple `selka --features` publishes, so a query and the manifest cannot disagree, and the `doc` anchor it returns resolves to a real section of the documentation shipped beside that executable rather than a path in whatever checkout happens to be current.
- [REFERENCE-2] A matching standard-library declaration answers with its checked signature from the compiler interface listing and `analysis: checked`. Surviving mutation metadata remains available; no inferred effect set or effect ceiling is published after EFFECTS-REMOVED-1.
- [REFERENCE-3] When the embedded library parses but cannot be checked, `library.status` and each declaration's `analysis` read `declaration-only`. A declaration-only answer does not claim checked body facts; effects and effect ceilings are not fields of the surviving contract.
- [REFERENCE-4] A generic declaration with no checked instantiation answers as a `template`; it does not claim that an unchecked specialization was analyzed. Effect ceilings and inferred effect sets are retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1.
- [REFERENCE-5] Builtin and collection-method facts are read from the checker's own tables, never a copied list: a builtin answers with the arity its table gives it and names that table as the source, and a collection-method spelling answers with one entry per operation the table spells that way and the receiver families the resolver accepts it on, so `split` reports both the no-argument whitespace form and the separator form.
- [REFERENCE-6] A name the executable reports as unsupported or removed answers only as an exclusion and never as a feature: the features section is empty for that query, the exclusion carries status `unsupported` or `removed`, and a removed name also carries its removal date and detail.
- [REFERENCE-7] The same query renders byte-identical text twice, and the JSON envelope is `reference-v1` carrying the query, the match kind, the language version, the library analysis status and a section count in a deterministic order, so two runs over one executable differ in nothing and no date, path or iteration order leaks into an answer.
- [REFERENCE-8] A query that matches nothing is a normal answer rather than an error: the text form says no fact matches and the JSON form reports a `none` match with count zero, while a query that is empty or only whitespace is a usage failure the engine reports as its own error.
- [REFERENCE-9] The reference reads only data embedded in the executable: the standard modules it exposes are served from memory, the same file requested by its checkout path is not found, and the embedded texts are checked against the checkout's own standard-library copies so drift between them fails a test instead of silently answering from the wrong source.
- [REFERENCE-10] Every curated syntax entry's example is the body of a fenced `selka` block in `docs/reference.md`, so the documentation gate executes each one and a curated example cannot rot away from the language it describes; the page must also carry at least one runnable example.
- [REFERENCE-11] `selka reference` reports a usage failure for a missing query, an unknown flag, an unknown format and a blank query, and `--help` prints its usage line and succeeds; a well-formed `--query TERM --format=json` request succeeds and its answer contains exactly the features matching that term, so the command's exit status distinguishes a usage mistake from an answer.
- [REFERENCE-12] An allocation failure while rendering either the text or the JSON answer reaches the caller as an out-of-memory error rather than being swallowed, rendered as a partial answer, or reported as a normal empty result.
- [REFERENCE-13] Short nominal export names such as `memo::Key` remain declared type facts beside generic function facts. The reference distinguishes the export kind before parsing a function signature, so a shorter type listing cannot be sliced as a function line or crash reference queries.
- [SPLIT-WHITESPACE-1] `str.split()` with no argument follows Python: it breaks the receiver on maximal runs of the same six ASCII whitespace scalars `str.isspace()` classifies with — space, tab, line feed, carriage return, vertical tab and form feed — keeps only the nonempty pieces, and answers `list[str]`, so leading, trailing and doubled whitespace produce no empty string and `"".split()` is empty. The result allocates and carries `managed_allocation`.
- [SPLIT-WHITESPACE-2] `split(separator)` keeps its exact-separator meaning: one argument is still required for the separator form, an empty needle still traps `R_EMPTY_SEPARATOR`, adjacent separators still produce empty pieces, and a call with two or more arguments is rejected with the callee's rendered signature.
- [SPLIT-WHITESPACE-3] `bytes.split()` with no argument follows Python's byte semantics: the receiver is broken on runs of the same six ASCII whitespace octets and answers `list[bytes]`, while `bytes.split(separator)` is unchanged, so the two receiver families agree on which bytes separate.
- [PRELUDE-SCALAR-1] The prelude answers `ord(text: str) -> int` and `chr(code: int) -> str` without an import when no binding, declaration or imported name shadows the spelling. `ord` of a one-scalar text literal folds at check time to its `int`, usable in comparisons, `case` arms and `const` initializers; `chr` accepts `0..0x10FFFF` excluding the surrogate range, and the `Result` forms in the `strings` module are unchanged.
- [PRELUDE-SCALAR-2] `ord` traps `R_TEXT_SCALAR` on text that is not exactly one Unicode scalar value — empty text, two scalars, or a runtime text that is not a one-scalar literal — and `chr` traps the same code on a negative code point, one past `0x10FFFF`, or a surrogate, so both scalar builtins report the one located trap rather than returning a partial answer.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-SITES-1] A method argument whose checked type does not match the callee's parameter type reports the existing `E_TYPE` diagnostic and attaches the callee's rendered signature as an `alternative` note beside the offending argument, so `"abc".find(1)` names `str.find(needle: str) -> int` where the type was rejected.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-SITES-2] A collection method call whose argument count differs from its checked signature reports the existing `E_ARITY` code with a message that names the receiver type and renders the signature — `` `str.split(separator: str) -> list[str]` takes 1 argument, but this call passes 2 `` — and attaches the accepted spelling as an `alternative` note when the language has one, so both `split` forms are named at the rejection. The signature text comes from one renderer (`buffer_operations.signatureText`) whose parameter counts are tested against the arity table.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-SITES-3] An unknown method on a known managed receiver reports the existing `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` or `E_LIST_METHOD` code with a message that names the receiver type and lists the methods that receiver accepts — `unknown method for a str receiver; accepted methods: …` — rather than a union list or the words "list method", and attaches an `alternative` note when the rejected spelling has a known accepted form (`str.format` names the f-string).
- [DIAGNOSTIC-SITES-4] A collection method signature is rendered through one shared renderer used by every call-site rejection, so a diagnostic and the interface listing cannot disagree: each rendered signature's parameter count equals the arity table's count for that operation, and an operation no source spelling names renders no signature rather than a guessed one.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-SITES-5] A bare `print` or `eprint` keeps its `E_OUTPUT_CAPABILITY` code and its existing message text, and additionally carries that same accepted spelling as an `alternative` note, so a structured consumer reads the repair from `diagnostic-v1` instead of parsing prose; a note that repeats the primary sentence is omitted from the text rendering, leaving that diagnostic's terminal output unchanged.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-ROLE-1] `diagnostic-v1` note objects may carry the role `alternative` alongside `related`, `origin` and `requirement`: a note that declares a role keeps it, a note that declares none reports the evidence slot it occupies, and diagnostics written before roles existed render byte-identically in text and JSON.
- [PY-SPLIT-1] `text.split()` with no argument breaks on runs of all six ASCII whitespace scalars — space, tab, line feed, carriage return, vertical tab and form feed — and keeps only the nonempty pieces, so `"".split()` and `"   ".split()` are empty and `" a "` yields one piece.
- [PY-SPLIT-2] `text.split(separator)` keeps Python's exact-separator answers: adjacent separators produce empty pieces, a single space separator reports every gap, and the no-argument form remains a different answer (`"a  b".split(" ")` is three pieces where `"a  b".split()` is two).
- [PY-SPLIT-3] `bytes.split()` with no argument breaks a byte string on the same six ASCII whitespace octets and answers `list[bytes]`, while `bytes.split(separator)` keeps its exact-octet-separator answers.
- [PY-SCALAR-1] `ord` and `chr` agree with Python for ASCII, a two-byte scalar, an astral scalar and the maximum code point: `ord("A") == 65`, `ord(marker) == ord("m")` for a `str` binding, `chr(65) == "A"`, `ord(chr(128512)) == 128512` and `len(chr(1114111)) == 1`.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-SITES-6] Every call-site rejection whose accepted callee is known carries that callee rendered as a `signature` note beside its primary message and its `alternative` note, whether or not the message already names it: a bare `print` reports `stream.print(values..., sep: str, end: str) -> None`, a rejected `sorted(..., reverse=True)` reports `sorted(values: list[T], key: callback) -> list[T]`, and the prelude `range`, `sorted` and `min`/`max` arity rejections render from the same shared table (`buffer_operations.stream_print_signature` and its neighbours) rather than a literal at each site. A rejection with no accepted callee at all — an unknown method, or an operation the language forbids outright — carries no signature note, because there is nothing to render.
- [DIAGNOSTIC-ROLE-2] `diagnostic-v1` note objects may carry the role `signature`: the text rendering prints it as `  signature: <rendering>` with no source location, the JSON form publishes `"role":"signature"` with the call's span, and it occupies the evidence slot beside `related` and `alternative`, so a consumer that knows only the older roles still reads a well-formed diagnostic.
- [FACT-STATUS-1] One shared vocabulary (`src/fact_status.zig`) spells the analysis status of a published declaration `checked`, `template`, `declaration-only`, `declared` and `lexical-only`, exactly the names `src/reference.zig` publishes as `analysis` and `docs/reference.md` §Fact status defines, so the text and JSON surfaces of `list-interfaces` and `index-project` cannot disagree about the words while `src/reference.zig` mirrors them from the root module that cannot import the compiler's copy.
- [INTERFACE-FACTS-1] `list-interfaces` reports `analysis(declaration-only)` and `tests(unavailable)` when no checked program is available. Checked callable summaries report `analysis(checked)` and test reachability. Neither status publishes effects or effect ceilings ([EFFECTS-REMOVED-1]).
- [INTERFACE-FACTS-2] An exported generic declaration without a checked instantiation reports `analysis(template)` even when the project checks completely. With a reachability index available, an uninstantiated template does not claim unavailable test reachability.
- [INTERFACE-CONTRACT-STATUS-1] The `interface-contracts-v2` manifest carries envelope-level `analysis: declaration-only` when no checked program is available; the field is omitted for a checked manifest. Callable `permissions` preserve mutation positions and origin metadata, with no effects or ceilings ([EFFECTS-REMOVED-1]).
- [NAVIGATION-FACTS-1] `selka index-project` never publishes an unknown as an empty set: a symbol whose bodies were not checked carries `analysis: "declaration-only"` (`"declared"` for a type declaration, whose shape needs no body) with `null` for `mutates`, `return_origin` and `mutation_writes`; a symbol with a checked typed entry publishes those facts, including empty sets when proved empty. Effect fields are absent ([EFFECTS-REMOVED-1]).
- [NAVIGATION-FACTS-2] A generic declaration with no checked instantiation in an otherwise completely checked project reports `analysis: "template"` with `null` inferred mutation and origin fields, so an unchecked template is never read as a checked summary.
- [NAVIGATION-FACTS-3] A project whose body syntax failed publishes lexical-only candidates with `analysis: "lexical-only"` and `null` inferred fields, so the partial renderer's best-effort declarations cannot be read as resolved facts either.
- [NAVIGATION-SCALE-1] Navigation has no fixed symbol or relationship cap: `index-project`, `context`, `tests-for` and lexical candidate recovery grow with the project and are bounded only by the compiler's memory ceiling. `navigation.Limits` defaults to no cap, a cap a caller passes still refuses with `NavigationLimit`, and no command passes one, so a project past the former 50,000-symbol, 250,000-reference and 50,000-candidate caps answers instead of failing with a host error.
- [NAVIGATION-SUMMARY-1] `selka index-project ENTRY.selka` prints a `navigation-summary-v1` document by default: the snapshot digest, the compiler identity, totals, one row per loaded module with its path, exported count and non-zero declaration counts by kind, and the `index-project --module`/`--symbol`/`--kind`/`--full`, `context` and `tests-for` queries that drill down with the entry path filled in. Its size grows with the module count rather than with locals and relationships, and it skips the relationship walk.
- [NAVIGATION-FULL-1] `selka index-project --full` prints the complete `navigation-v1` document byte for byte as the command printed it by default before, streamed to stdout instead of held whole, and takes no filter; `scripts/navigate.py`, `scripts/store.py` and the other `navigation-v1` consumers pass it.
- [NAVIGATION-SELECT-1] `selka index-project --module=NAME`, `--symbol=NAME` and `--kind=KIND` (each also written with a space) print a `navigation-selection-v1` document: the `navigation-v1` symbols every present filter selects, `--symbol` keeping the declaration and the members it owns (fields, cases, parameters, locals), every reference into or out of one of them, and the snapshot rows of the modules those records locate while `snapshot_sha256` still pins the whole project. A filter that names nothing publishes `status: "missing"` or `"ambiguous"` with the reason and candidates and exits 2; a `--kind` outside the published kinds, or `--full` with a filter, is a usage error.
- [NAVIGATION-SPELLING-1] A declaration spelled the way code spells a call, `module.name`, names `module::name`: the dotted text is tried at every split into a module path and a member path, one match is the answer, and several are reported as ambiguous and never silently chosen. `context --symbol`, `tests-for` and `index-project --symbol` share the rule, and the exact `module::name` spelling keeps working unchanged.
- [TYPED-HOLES-2] A valid `todo[T]()` retains a bounded context snapshot in ordinary and symbolic generic checking alike: the declared result type, the enclosing callable, every in-scope initialized binding with its type, the generic requirements in force, the permitted mutation footprint, and the contextual expected type only when the checker knows one. Uninitialized, consumed, arena-confined and synthetic bindings are counted in `unusable_bindings` and never published, a snapshot past 32 bindings or 32 requirements counts the surplus in `omitted`, and a separate invalid function keeps its own diagnostic with no obligation attached.
- [TYPED-HOLES-3] `check --diagnostics=json` publishes the hole's snapshot as a nested `typed-hole-obligation` version-1 record on the `E_TYPED_HOLE` diagnostic only — identity (snapshot digest, callable, hole range, omitted count), declared type, contextual expected type, bindings, generic requirements and contract — with the facts a hole cannot promise (`expected_type`, `declared_effect_ceiling`, `transitive_effects`) named in an `unavailable` list instead of guessed. The text diagnostic and every other `diagnostic-v1` record keep their exact bytes.
- [TYPED-HOLES-4] An obligation is identified by its snapshot digest — source path, checked text and hole range — together with the exact hole range: a query presenting a stale digest or a moved range answers null, and replacing the hole removes the obligation and lets the program check and run again.
- [TYPED-HOLES-5] A typed hole is reported even when its snapshot cannot be retained: a failed snapshot allocation still appends the `E_TYPED_HOLE` diagnostic carrying no obligation, and only a failure to append the diagnostic itself is `OutOfMemory`.
- [TYPED-HOLES-6] A file holding both a hole and an unrelated invalid function reports the two independently: the hole keeps its own obligation and the neighbouring diagnostic carries none, so one function's evidence never attaches to another's diagnostic.
- [CONTEXT-ENVELOPE-1] `selka context` answers one declaration with a single `agent-context-v1` JSON object whose envelope carries the compiler's build identity and the loaded sources' identities, the selector the answer responds to, the analysis status, the emitted byte count of the serialized response, and the omissions of each section.
- [CONTEXT-SELECT-1] `selka context --symbol 'module::name'` selects exactly the declaration that module-qualified spelling names, in the same spelling `selka index-project` and `selka tests-for` use; a name matching several declarations is reported as ambiguous and a name matching none as missing, neither is silently resolved to a near match, and exactly one of `--symbol` and `--at` is required.
- [CONTEXT-AT-1] `selka context --at 'path:line:column'` selects the innermost declaration whose source span contains that one-based position, or the diagnostic whose span contains it when no declaration does, so a position inside a nested definition answers about the innermost one.
- [CONTEXT-SECTIONS-1] `selka context` publishes its sections in the fixed priority order declaration, contract, types, dependencies, tests, changes; the contract labels each of signature, declared bound, effects, mutation footprint and return origin with its fact status, and the tests section labels its direct and transitive callers as static reachability rather than coverage.
- [CONTEXT-BUDGET-1] The serialized `selka context` response never exceeds `--context-bytes`, and when the answer does not fit, whole records are dropped from the lowest-priority section first, every omission is reported per section together with a continuation query that asks for a larger budget, and a declaration's contract is never truncated into a misleading fragment.
- [CONTEXT-STALE-1] A source change detected while a `selka context` answer is being assembled rejects that answer instead of combining facts from two versions.
- [CONTEXT-PARTIAL-1] An incomplete project still answers `selka context` with the declarations it recovered, the analysis status and the reason resolved relationships are unavailable, and never reuses edges from an earlier successful check as though they described the current source.

<!-- WS1-CONTEXT-SPEC-APPEND -->
- [CONTEXT-CONFIRM-1] A `selka context` answer re-reads every source it names through the provider the project was loaded from before it is published, so a source that changed while the answer was being assembled is rejected as a stale snapshot rather than the answer mixing two versions, and a source that can no longer be read at all is reported as a failure to confirm rather than as a changed source.
- [PARSER-HINT-6] A Python statement shape whose terminator is missing names the accepted spelling at that point instead of only `unexpected token`: a chained assignment (`x = y = 21`) reports at the second `=`; `del xs[0]` reports at the first token after `del` with the alternative `remove with _ = xs.pop(index) for a list or _ = d.pop(key) for a dictionary`; a `try:` that opens a suite reports at its colon with the `Result` alternative; and an unknown string prefix (`r"a\nb"`) reports at the adjacent literal with the ordinary-string alternative. Each shape already failed to parse, so the hint chooses the message for an existing rejection and never admits a program the parser rejected before. The conditional-expression and comma-list rows are gone: `y = 42 if x > 0 else 0`, `a, b = b, a` and `return a // b, a % b` are spellings the parser now accepts.
- [PARSER-HINT-8] Set literals remain supported. Both list comprehension spellings, dict/set comprehensions and generator arguments are recognized only for removal diagnostics and certain fixes. Their alternatives use typed explicit collection loops or scalar folds, preserving source and value order.
- [PARSER-HINT-9] Four more Python forms name their accepted spelling: a literal `case` arm reports `E_SYNTAX` at the literal with the `if`/`elif` alternative; a member named `unwrap` reports at that name with the `match`/`?` alternative because `unwrap` is the domain keyword; a walrus inside parentheses reports at its `:` with the assign-first alternative; and a top-level `if __name__ == ...` guard reports at the `if` with the delete-the-guard alternative, instead of `unexpected token; expected def`.
- [PARSER-HINT-10] A Python form whose rejection is not `E_SYNTAX` carries the same counted alternative without a row in the declaration table: a loop `else` clause, a nested `def`, a `match` used as an expression, `**`, and `<<`/`>>` each keep their `E_EXPRESSION` code and span and add one `alternative` note naming the accepted construct (`use a flag`, a top-level function, an `if`/`elif` assignment, a multiplication loop, `shift_left`/`shift_right`). A `**` or `<<` written where an expression is expected keeps its former message when the preceding token is not the same operator, so the code, the span and every other `expected an expression` rejection are unchanged.
- [DIAG-ALTERNATIVE-1] A `dict.get` call that omits the fallback reports `E_ARITY` at the call with the receiver's rendered signature and an `alternative` note that names the accepted call, because the fallback is a required operand of the one spelling `get` has: `a dictionary get always takes its fallback: d.get(key, default)`. Every other rejected arity of every other collection operation keeps its former message and its former note, so this adds a spelling where one is known and stays silent where none is.

<!-- WS-MEASURE-SPEC-APPEND -->
- [GENERIC-EXPECTED-1] A binding, return, known parameter, or field context contributes the expected result type to generic call inference, including array lengths. A complete contextual substitution guides nested arguments without duplicate evaluation; inferred and explicit calls reuse the same concrete specialization.
- [GENERIC-EXPECTED-2] Expected-result inference in generic bodies preserves caller type and length identities, and rejects conflicting substitutions even in uninstantiated templates.
- [GENERIC-EXPECTED-3] Argument and expected-result inferences must agree without coercion; incomplete substitutions and conflicts report E_GENERIC_INFERENCE, and ordinary generic requirements remain enforced.
- [OPTION-FLOW-1] A local copyable Option tested with `is None` or `is not None` exposes its payload on proven-present paths, including early-return continuations and generic bodies. A context expecting its declared Option type, repeated presence tests, and explicit match retain the wrapper. Record payload field updates retain the wrapper, and managed payload mutations preserve shared identity.
- [OPTION-FLOW-2] Option facts follow negation and the evaluated paths of short-circuit `and` and `or`; facts at joins must hold on every continuing path, and an alternative that permits absence cannot justify a payload read.
- [OPTION-FLOW-3] Reassignment removes Option presence facts. Loops invalidate facts about bindings their bodies may replace before checking any iteration and expose guard facts only in the body; zero-iteration paths do not establish presence, while unrelated incoming facts survive.
- [OPTION-FLOW-4] Presence tests evaluate their Option operand once and refine only local bindings, never shared fields. Owned or checked-result payloads require explicit match; presence tests reject non-Option operands and preserve existing effect, ownership, and Result obligations.
- [PRELUDE-SORT-KEY-2] Named sort-key callbacks use the ordinary canonical callback signature, including return-origin bounds. A key returning its argument, a key held in a local, one key used at several calls, and an equivalent callback stored, invoked or passed to sort.sort_by_key retain identical type identity. Legacy lambda key inference remains only to produce E_LAMBDA_REMOVED and certain named-function migration.
- [TEXT-SCAN-1] `str.count(sub)` and `str.find(sub)` scan with one bulk octet search — `memchr` for the needle's first octet with the rest confirmed in place, and `memmem` for the remainder once that octet recurs without matching — instead of a comparison call at every offset. The answers are unchanged: `count` is non-overlapping left to right (`"aaa".count("aa") == 1`), a match at either end counts, a needle longer than the receiver occurs nowhere, and because a valid UTF-8 needle can only match where a scalar starts, the octet search answers the scalar question exactly and `find` still answers a scalar index.
- [TEXT-SCAN-2] `str.replace(old, new)` sizes its result with one counting scan and builds it by copying each unchanged run between matches whole, so the result, its scalar length and Python's non-overlapping left-to-right matching (`"aaa".replace("aa", "b") == "ba"`) are unchanged while no offset is compared on its own.
- [TEXT-SCAN-3] `str.split(separator)` and `bytes.split(separator)` find each separator with the same search and count the cuts first, so the result list is allocated once at its final length and never grows. Every empty piece is kept, and a piece of a non-ASCII text counts the scalars of its own octets only, so a separator split, `splitlines` and the whitespace split of a non-ASCII text cost time linear in its length rather than quadratic.
The retained-view contract TEXT-VIEW-1 was retired on 2026-10-17 with reference counting. Scoped checked views follow NEW-CORE-ZERO-RC-VIEWS-1; ordinary slices and text pieces remain independently owned values.
The retained-view contract TEXT-VIEW-2 was retired on 2026-10-17 with reference counting. Scoped checked views follow NEW-CORE-ZERO-RC-VIEWS-1; ordinary slices and text pieces remain independently owned values.
The retained-view contract TEXT-VIEW-3 was retired on 2026-10-17 with reference counting. Scoped checked views follow NEW-CORE-ZERO-RC-VIEWS-1; ordinary slices and text pieces remain independently owned values.
The retained-view contract TEXT-VIEW-4 was retired on 2026-10-17 with reference counting. Scoped checked views follow NEW-CORE-ZERO-RC-VIEWS-1; ordinary slices and text pieces remain independently owned values.
- [TEXT-INDEX-1] ASCII scalar offsets equal byte offsets. Non-ASCII offsets are found by UTF-8 walks from the nearest available endpoint, caller-supplied range endpoint or cached cursor. Eligible owned texts may update a last-position cursor before worker-pool startup. After startup, queries may read existing metadata but never update the text block; no sparse position table or task-local cache is required.
- [TEXT-INDEX-2] Scalar slices locate UTF-8 boundaries; materializing a slice produces independent owned octets. Bounded searches report scalar positions. Cache state never changes results, bounds traps or ownership, and no retained owner view or constant-time slicing guarantee is implied.
- [TEXT-INDEX-3] Constructors preserve validated UTF-8 and sound ASCII metadata. A missing scalar-count cache is computed from the bytes; lazily computed counts obey OWNERSHIP-TEXT-READONLY-1. Text values and bounds behavior do not depend on metadata availability.
- [LIST-SORT-RUNS-1] `values.sort()` — and `sorted`, `sort.sort_ints`, `sort.sort_floats` and `sort.sort_strs`, which sort a copy with it — is an adaptive stable merge in the native runtime: an ascending run already in the list is kept, a strictly descending run is reversed, a run shorter than the minimum run length, between 16 and 32 elements, is extended by insertion, and runs merge in powersort order through one scratch buffer of at most half the list, so an ordered, reversed or run-structured list sorts in `O(n)` and every list in `O(n log n)`. Each element type has its own comparison and moves — signed and unsigned 64-bit integers, binary64 values and `str` — and the narrower integer widths share one generic path. The order is exactly PY-COLLECTIONS-15's: integers by value, every NaN after every ordered float, equal values and signed zeros in input order, `str` by Unicode scalar value. The list keeps its identity, length, capacity and generation, subsequent reads see the sorted elements, and a sort allocates no managed storage.
- [SORT-COMPARE-1] `sort.sort_by_compare(mut values, compare)` and `sort.sort_by_compare_with(mut values, compare, context)` sort a list in place by a top-level `compare(first, second)` (or `compare(first, second, context)`) that returns a negative, zero or positive `int`, written in the language: a bottom-up merge sort of the positions, then the elements are moved into place with `exchange` cycles, so each element is lent to `compare` and moved once and none is copied. The sort is stable (an element goes before an equal one only when it came first), an empty or one-element list is left alone, and a comparison that is not an order (it contradicts itself) still ends without a trap and leaves every element once, in an order it does not determine. The element type is `copy + storable`: a resource or a result cannot be lent to a comparison. The `cmp=` operand and a `key=` that returns a value with no order are `E_CALL_ARGUMENT` and `E_SORT_KEY` as before, and their messages name `sort.sort_by_compare`.
- [PY-DICT-2] A dictionary keeps insertion-ordered entry storage, using a small-table scan or an open-addressing index. Indexed lookup is expected constant time in the entry count apart from key hashing and comparison; writes may grow storage or rebuild the index. The dictionary owns its auxiliary metadata and index, which are released with its storage. Iteration, keys/values/items, snapshots and printing follow insertion order; equality ignores insertion order. Updating a present key preserves its position, and removing then reinserting a key appends it after the surviving entries. Allocation failure follows the runtime out-of-memory path; there is no refused-index scan fallback.
- [PY-DICT-3] A dictionary key is hashed a word at a time. A scalar key's bytes, zero-extended to one 64-bit word, are multiplied by `0x9e3779b97f4a7c15` modulo 2⁶⁴; that product is xored with itself shifted right by 32 bits. A `str` key's UTF-8 payload is read eight octets at a time — a payload of at most eight octets as two overlapping halves folded once, a longer one word by word into a state seeded by its length, ending on the word that finishes at its last octet, so no read passes the payload and every octet reaches the answer — and an existing cached hash is reused. Before worker-pool startup a missing hash may be cached in dynamic owned text; after startup a missing hash is computed without writing into the block. Both use fixed constants and no seed. Equal keys have equal bytes and therefore equal hashes, and no observable answer depends on the hash: insertion order, iteration, `keys`/`values`/`items`, explicit copies, printing, equality and every lookup answer exactly as they did under the octet-at-a-time FNV-1a hash. A `float` key is refused ([HASH-KEYS-1]), so there is no `0.0`/`-0.0` or NaN key case, and every other key type hashes structurally ([HASH-KEYS-2]).
- [PY-DICT-4] Dictionary probes do not write a mutable lookup hint into the dictionary. The compiler can share a dominating probe for identical dictionary and key SSA values, including across default-value branches; consuming updates carry the corresponding entry or insertion probe explicitly. A runtime index rebuild performs a fresh lookup before inserting. Read-modify-write spellings, setdefault, defaults and missing-key behavior retain their ordinary semantics, and intervening insertion, removal, growth or clear cannot make an earlier absence hide a current entry. This is compiler probe reuse, not a universal promise of one probe or one hash for every spelling.
- [PY-DICT-5] The index uses byte control tags and 32-bit entry numbers; entry metadata stores a 64-bit hash for each physical entry. Probing filters candidates by tag and cached hash before key equality. Rebuilding places live entries from their cached hashes without rehashing their keys. Removal marks the index slot deleted, preserving collision-chain reachability; subsequent rebuilds remove tombstones and compact surviving entries in insertion order. The first removal may allocate the dead-entry bitmap. Index allocation failure is fatal rather than selecting a scan fallback.
- [DICT-RANGE-KEY-2] A borrowed text-span lookup hashes the supplied octets and compares candidate keys by length and octets. It retains no span pointer, offset, miss record or collection epoch in the dictionary. Equal spans and whole strings find the same equal stored key, while different ranges are compared by their own contents. An earlier miss cannot hide a key inserted before a later lookup or write. The borrow remains synchronous and does not escape the lookup.
- [HASH-KEYS-1] A dictionary key or set element type must be hashable: every integer kind, `bool`, `unit`, `str`, `bytes`, and a record, union, enum, `Option`, `Result` or fixed array whose parts are all hashable. `float` (NaN is not equal to itself), a `list`, `dict`, `set` or `byte_buffer` (a shared reference whose contents can change after it is stored), a function value (no equality) and a capability, file or resource are refused where the dictionary or set is built — a dict literal, a set literal, `set()`, `set(iterable)` — with `E_DICT_KEY` for a key and `E_SET_ELEMENT` for an element, whose message names the key type, the path to the first offending part (`.field`, `.case` for a union payload, `[]` for an array element) and the reason. In a generic template a concrete key type follows the same rule, `str` is accepted, and a type parameter must promise `integer`.
- [HASH-KEYS-2] A key that is neither a `str` nor a plain scalar — a record, union, enum, `Option`, fixed array, `bytes`, or any value holding a `str` — is hashed and compared by walking its descriptor: scalar bytes a word at a time, a `str` through its cached text hash and its octets, `bytes` through the octet hash a text of the same octets gets and its octets, each field in declaration order, a union's case tag and then only that case's payload, and each array element. Padding and an inactive case's bytes are never read, the constants are fixed and there is no seed, so equal values hash equally on every run and the comparison is exactly `==`. The table owns stored keys. Keys without managed contents are stored by value; insertion of a key with managed contents transfers ownership, so use `key.copy()` when the original must remain available. Changing an independent copy cannot alter a stored key. Dictionary equality looks each key up in the other table instead of scanning it.
- [DICT-REMOVE-1] Removing a dictionary key or set element marks its index slot deleted and its physical entry dead without moving other entries. Key/value portions transferred to the result remain owned by that result; portions not transferred are destroyed, and the removed entry bytes are cleared. len counts live entries. Readers skip dead entries or translate a live ordinal; they do not settle the table first. Index rebuilding compacts survivors in insertion order. Reinserting a removed key appends after the survivors. Removal may allocate a dead-entry bitmap and is not specified to be allocation-free.
- [SET-1] `set[T]` is a dictionary-kind collection from its hashable elements to unit, kept in first-insertion order and independent on assignment. `{a, b}` builds one in source order (a first element followed by `,`, `}` or `for` starts a set literal or a set comprehension, and `{}` stays the empty dict); `set()` builds an empty set of the type the context expects and is `E_SET_INFERENCE` without one; `set(iterable)` copies a list, `str`, `bytes`, fixed array, dictionary's keys, dictionary view, `range(...)` or set; the historical `{value for name in source if condition}` spelling reports E_COMPREHENSION_REMOVED with explicit-loop alternatives under PY-GATHER-1. The methods are `add`, `remove` (a missing element traps `R_MISSING_KEY`), `discard`, `pop` (removes and answers the most recently added live element; an empty set traps `R_MISSING_KEY`), `clear`, `copy`, `update`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference`, `symmetric_difference`, `issubset`, `issuperset` and `isdisjoint`, and `union` is accepted as a member name after a dot although it is a declaration keyword. `update`, `union`, `intersection`, `difference`, `symmetric_difference`, `issubset`, `issuperset` and `isdisjoint` take another set or any iterable `set(...)` accepts, which they collect into a set first, as Python's methods do (`s.union([4])`); the operators still take two sets. `in`, `len`, `for`, `enumerate`, `list`, `sorted`, `sum`, `min`, `max`, `any` and `all` see the elements in insertion order, and a resize during iteration traps `R_LIST_ITERATION_MUTATION`. A set prints as `{1, 2}` and as `set()` when empty, wherever it is nested. `{}` against a set type is `E_DICT_TYPE`, indexing a set is `E_SET_INDEX`, and any other method is `E_LIST_METHOD` listing the set methods. A function that changes a set parameter declares it `mut`. A program's own function named `set` keeps its name and its calls beside the builtin type; in such a module set literals still build sets but the type spelling `set[T]` is not available.
- [SET-OPERATORS-1] Between two sets of one type, `|`, `&`, `-` and `^` build a new set (union, intersection, difference, symmetric difference) holding the left operand's surviving elements in its order followed by the right operand's new elements in theirs, each operand evaluated once, left first; `<=`, `<`, `>=` and `>` test subset, proper subset, superset and proper superset, stopping at the first element that settles the answer; `==` ignores insertion order; the methods of the same names answer the same. `|`, `^` and `&` parse with Python's precedence, below arithmetic and above comparison. On operands that are neither sets nor integers ([PY-SET-OPS-1]), `|`, `&` and `^` are `E_SET_OPERATOR` naming `bit_or`, `bit_and` or `bit_xor`, any other operator on a set is `E_SET_OPERATOR`, an augmented assignment to a set is `E_SET_OPERATOR`, and `|=`, `&=` and `^=` report that a set has no augmented assignment operator.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-1] `values.sort(key=f, reverse=b)` sorts a list in place and `sorted(values, key=f, reverse=b)` sorts a copy, with Python's keyword-only `key=` and `reverse=`, both optional. The key maps one element to an ordered key — any integer width, `bool`, `float`, `str`, `bytes`, or a record whose ordered fields (including nested records) compare lexicographically; source tuple keys are retired under TUPLE-REMOVED-1 — and is evaluated exactly once per element, in input order, before any element moves. The order is stable: elements with equal keys keep their input order, and `reverse=True` orders the keys descending while equal keys still keep input order, so it is not sort-then-reverse. Float keys order as PY-COLLECTIONS-15 orders floats — signed zeros equal, every NaN after every ordered value — and `reverse=True` puts the NaNs first. Without a key, `values.sort(reverse=True)` and `sorted(values, reverse=True)` order an integer, `float`, `str` or `bytes` list by its elements under the same rules; a bare `values.sort()` and `sorted(values)` are unchanged. The list keeps its identity, length and capacity, and subsequent reads see the new order. A key that traps propagates the trap before any element has moved.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-2] A keyed sort's operands are checked where they are written: a positional operand, an unknown keyword or a repeated one is `E_CALL_ARGUMENT` with the accepted signature, and `cmp=` also names the key alternative; a key that is not a callback of exactly one element of the list's element type, that declares its argument `mut`, or that returns a type with no order is `E_SORT_KEY` naming the types; `reverse=` of any type but `bool` is `E_TYPE`; a sort without a key over an element with no native order is `E_COLLECTION_METHOD`. Legacy lambda key recognition infers a result type only to diagnose and migrate the callback under LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1; use a named key function. Named arguments on any other collection method are `E_CALL_ARGUMENT`.
- [LAMBDA-KEY-INFER-1] Legacy sort-key lambda recognition infers the key result from its ordinary checked body and the collection element type, including field, call, conversion and managed text expressions. Trial inference restores temporary nodes, locals, bindings, option facts and ownership state before checking the callback contract. A complete noncapturing key receives E_LAMBDA_REMOVED with a named-function repair; an untypable or unordered key retains its located type diagnostic. No inferred lambda becomes executable.
- [LIST-SORT-KEY-3] A keyed sort lowers to one key list sized to the list, filled by one pass that calls the key once per element, and one native `myl_native_list_sort_keyed` call. A numeric key becomes an order-preserving 64-bit word — the sign bit flipped, binary64 bits reflected with signed zeros merged and every NaN the largest word, all complemented for `reverse` — and when the words span fewer distinct values than there are elements, a stable counting sort gives each element, in input order, the next slot of its key; otherwise, when every word minus the smallest fits above the position bits, `(word - smallest) << bits | position` words are sorted by the integer engine in the key list's own storage; keys spread wider take two such sorts of 32 bits each. Text and record keys sort (key, position) entries with a stable merge. For `values.sort(...)` the elements then move once along the permutation's cycles through element-sized temporaries, prefetching a few steps ahead on each cycle, so the extra memory is the key list plus the sort's scratch and no second list of elements exists; for `sorted(values, ...)` they are gathered in order from the original list into a new list allocated at the list's length, so the copy is never built only to be permuted. The native sort calls no guest code and allocates nothing managed; scratch memory the host refuses is the allocation-failure trap, raised before any element moves.
- [SYNTAX-METHOD-KEYWORDS-1] A method call on an expression receiver, `holder.items.sort(key=f)`, records a keyword for each named argument exactly as the named-receiver form `items.sort(key=f)` does, with the same rule that positional arguments precede keyword arguments.

## Local-reasoning syntax foundation

- [SYNTAX-MUT-1] `mut name: T` declares a writable function or callback parameter, and `mut argument` marks caller storage. Callback parameter modes retain both mut capability and named labels. The parser records the marker; semantic checking validates writable targets and exact call-site positions.
- [SYNTAX-KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A bare `*` in a declared function's parameter list sets `FunctionSignature.keyword_only_from` to the zero-based index of the first parameter after it, which is also the positional parameter count, and is null when the list has no `*`. Only the positional group keeps the required-before-defaulted rule, so a required keyword-only parameter may follow a defaulted one. A `*` with no following parameter is `E_SYNTAX` at the `*` naming the missing parameter, a second `*` in one list is `E_SYNTAX` at that `*`, and a `*` in a callback type is `E_SYNTAX` because a callback slot is positional. Record field lists and lambdas gain nothing.
- [SYNTAX-TUPLE-1] Retained tuple migration-parser recognition builds a `tuple` node for a parenthesized comma list whose element nodes are in source order, and a trailing comma after two or more elements is accepted; a parenthesized expression with no comma stays that expression, so `(a)` is `a`. A bare comma list builds the same node after `return` and on both sides of a multi-target assignment, where the target list is a `tuple` of `name` nodes and the value is the right-hand `tuple`, reached through the same assignment node inside a discard that `rec.field = value` uses. One element is never a tuple: `(a,)`, `x, = pair` and a bare comma list with no `=` are `E_SYNTAX`.
- [SYNTAX-TUPLE-2] Retained tuple migration-parser recognition of `tuple[T1, ..., Tn]` in a type position appends one `TupleType` with the element spans to `TypeExpressions.tuples`, from the `tuple` keyword through the closing bracket. The parser accepts any comma count, including one, so the historical arity check is separate from parsing; normal checking reports E_TUPLE_REMOVED under TUPLE-REMOVED-1, and `tuple` stays an ordinary type name when no bracket follows it.
- [SYNTAX-CONDITIONAL-1] Retired `value_if_true if condition else value_if_false` syntax is recognized for diagnostics and migration as a `conditional` node and binds looser than every binary operator, with its condition parsed above `or` and its else arm at the lowest precedence, so `a or b if p else c` takes `a or b` as its then-arm and `a if p else b if q else c` puts a nested conditional in its else arm. A `kw_if` that begins a new logical line still starts an `if` statement, never a conditional.
- [SYNTAX-CONDITIONAL-2] The shared expression parser recognizes retired conditional syntax in call arguments, keyword values, collection elements, f-string holes, returns and assignments solely for diagnostics and migration. Checking reports E_CONDITIONAL_EXPRESSION_REMOVED; an edit is attached only when moving the selection into statements preserves order and execution frequency.
- [SYNTAX-FMT-1] The token formatter prints every new spelling through its ordinary spacing rules, so `selka fmt` returns them unchanged: one space around `mut` and around a bare `*`, no space before a comma and one after it in a tuple or a `tuple[...]` type, and one space around `if` and `else` in a conditional. A trailing comma before a closing delimiter is the one exception: canonical text drops it consistently, in tuples as in argument and field lists, which never changes a program's meaning because no one-element tuple is spellable.
- [MUT-PARAM-1] Declared `mut name: T` positions form the caller-update contract. Every corresponding argument requires `mut argument`, and markers on other positions reject with `E_MUTATION_MARKER`. Direct calls, generic calls and callbacks enforce the same contract; mutating methods require writable receiver roots.
- [MUT-PARAM-8] A missing or superfluous call-site `mut` marker does not end its function's check: the checker records the `E_MUTATION_MARKER` diagnostic with its fix, checks the call as if the marker were right, and fails the function once its body is done, so one `check` reports every marker a function needs rather than one per run. A `mut` on an argument that cannot be assigned through still ends the function's check at once.
- [MUT-PARAM-2] Ordinary parameter bindings are read-only; mutation through their roots rejects with `E_IMMUTABLE`. Copyable managed arguments are borrowed for the call; `.copy()` requests independent ownership. Affine arguments follow their ownership-transfer rules. Declared mut parameters may be unused and still retain caller-update capability. Forwarding mut storage requires a writable root. Match bindings are read-only; a borrowed copyable payload can be copied into a mut local, then explicitly stored back through a writable owner after the borrow ends.
- [MUT-PARAM-3] Mut parameters support scalars, text, bytes, collections, records and fixed arrays, including whole-value rebinding. A mut argument must name assignable caller storage: a writable variable, field or indexed slot rooted in one. Temporary constructors cannot be mut targets.
- [PY-ARRAY-MUT-1] `mut a: FixedArray[T, N]` permits element writes and whole-array rebinding through caller storage. Fields and indexed elements may be mut targets. Readonly managed inputs borrow; an explicit copy provides independent owned contents. Mut array callbacks use the ordinary native inout convention; external C ABI restrictions remain separate.
- [MUT-PARAM-4] Forwarding a caller parameter as `mut` requires that parameter to be declared `mut`. Writable calls retain mutation and ownership checks. A managed write that cannot be authorized by the declared mutation contract can still report `E_MUTATION_PERMISSION`; source effect ceilings are retired by EFFECTS-REMOVED-1. Read-only arguments preserve their independent value semantics.
- [MUT-OVERLAP-RECEIVER-1] An assignment or update receiver may not overlap a `mut` argument evaluated in that same statement: the same root and paths that may agree until either ends give E_MUTATION_OVERLAP at the mut argument with the receiver as related evidence. Make the call in its own statement first and use its saved result. Disjoint roots and paths, a call in a preceding statement, and read-only snapshots remain legal. A simple unconditional one-line update with no other effectful evaluation carries the exact structured split-call fix; conditional or effectful rewrites require the programmer to choose their ordering.
- [ORIGIN-WIDE-1] The checker's mutation and alias analysis names every parameter position of a function exactly, so a `mut` collection, a returned read-only collection or a returned string at any parameter position checks, builds and runs (a 17th such parameter used to panic the checker). A list, string, record or other aggregate parameter must be one of a function's first 64 parameters (`E_PARAMETER_POSITION` at a later one, advising to move it earlier or group parameters into a record); scalar parameters are never tracked and stay unlimited. An unattributed write publishes every parameter position as possibly written.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1] Mut arguments of a call must name disjoint caller places. Known same-place or enclosing-part overlap rejects with `E_MUTATION_OVERLAP`. Dynamic indices and keys are evaluated once in source order and compared before invocation; overlap traps before the callee runs.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-2] MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1 applies to direct, module-qualified, keyword, mixed, function-value and callback calls after binding arguments to parameters. Checked ownership also rejects a readonly borrow that conflicts with a mutable argument; an explicit independent copy or nonconflicting scalar read follows VALUE-SEMANTICS-5. Argument evaluation must not invalidate an earlier argument.
- [MUT-EXCLUSIVE-3] Static overlap diagnostics point at the later argument and relate the earlier one. Dynamic overlap guards compare the captured written indices or keys along common root paths, so equal valid positions naming one element trap; negative positions retain the bounds trap. Independent field paths remain legal.
- [MUT-PARAM-5] With `mut` supplying the footprint, the `mutates(...)` clause and the opt-in `&` call-argument marker are removed spellings: a clause is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` at `mutates` saying “mark each written parameter `mut name: T`”, `&` before an operand is `E_REMOVED_SPELLING` saying to write `mut value` (between two operands it is the set intersection, [SET-OPERATORS-1]), and the `--mutation-markers` switch is rejected on every command. The reference-typed `class` exemption and the advisory `W_MUTATES_MISSING` warning are gone with them: a compatibility-mode write through a class parameter needs its own `mut` like every other position, and the check is an error, not a warning.
- [MUT-PARAM-6] Signature surfaces render `mut name: T`, preserve mut positions and named labels in their public contract identity, and format call-site `mut argument` markers unchanged.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-1] A `named name: T` parameter must be supplied with its label. Positional use rejects with `E_KEYWORD_ONLY`; labeled operands may be written in any order and still evaluate once in their source order.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-2] Named mode belongs to each parameter contract, including callback types. Defaulted and required named parameters retain normal default and arity checking; generic and compile-time binding enforce the same labeled-call rule.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-3] Rendered signatures preserve named parameter labels, such as `func(host: Host, named code: int) -> int`; public interface identity includes those labels, while positional binder names do not affect callback identity.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-1] Conditional expressions are retired with E_CONDITIONAL_EXPRESSION_REMOVED. Recognition still requires a Boolean condition and agreeing arm types, using a contextual expected type when available and preserving integer widths without numeric widening. Invalid arm or condition types retain their located E_TYPE diagnostics before migration.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-2] Retired conditionals remain recognizable in nested expression positions so certain fixes can use typed mutable declarations and if/else assignments, or return from each branch. Option and Result constructors retain contextual arm types; generic repairs use source-level type parameters. Unsafe hoists, repeated conditions and short-circuit positions receive no speculative extraction.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-3] A certain conditional repair evaluates only the selected arm. Both original arms remain checked for type, definite initialization, ownership and pending Result obligations. Modern if/else statements preserve the same chosen-arm calls and traps; source conditional expressions never lower successfully.
- [CONDITIONAL-EXPR-4] Executable conditional-expression lowering is deleted. Retained typed and symbolic recognition supplies migration facts; the replacement is an ordinary if/else statement assigning a named definitely initialized mutable result or returning from each branch. Recognition-only temporary values never reach executable Core.
- [KEYWORD-ONLY-4] The `positional-literal` lint (report-only, `L_POSITIONAL_LITERAL`) reports the last operand a call of three or more arguments passes positionally as a `bool`, integer or `None` literal, at the callee: `` `True` is passed positionally to `draw`'s 4th parameter `filled`; name it: `draw(canvas, x=10, y=20, filled=True)` ``. The reported parameter is the one that operand binds to, an intrinsic or a call through a callback value is exempt because it has no parameter to name, and the repaired call comes from the shared call renderer with every operand from the first positional literal on written by name, so the paste is a call that binds.
- [TUPLE-1] Tuple type identity is a retired surface contract, superseded by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; tuple type spellings report E_TUPLE_REMOVED and nominal records retain replacement identity.
- [TUPLE-2] Tuple construction is retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; tuple values report E_TUPLE_REMOVED and certain tuple returns migrate to explicit named record fields.
- [TUPLE-3] Tuple destructuring is retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; certain swaps and destructured return consumers migrate through staged temporaries before ordinary writes.
- [PY-DESTRUCTURE-PLACE-1] Source tuple destructuring into elements and fields is retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1. Certain repairs stage every right-hand operand or result field before evaluating destination receivers and indices in original order, preserving managed snapshots and ordinary assignment checks.
- [TUPLE-4] Tuple collection iteration is retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; two-name enumerate, zip and dictionary items loops remain supported without materialized tuples.
- [TUPLE-5] Tuple operations and boundaries are retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; unsupported tuple consumers retain diagnostics and manual nominal-record guidance.
- [TUPLE-6] Tuple printing and structural comparison are retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; stored, printed and compared tuples have no automatic repair.
- [TUPLE-7] Legacy tuple spellings remain parseable for TUPLE-REMOVED-1 migration diagnostics; a normal check rejects them with their original source spans.
- [TUPLE-8] Generic tuple surfaces are retired by TUPLE-REMOVED-1; certain return migrations preserve abstract source binders independently of the first specialization, and unsupported generic tuple parameters remain manual.
- [OPTION-METHODS-1] `Option[T]` answers three intrinsic methods and nothing else: `unwrap_or(default: T) -> T`, `is_none() -> bool` and `is_some() -> bool`. They are reachable on both receiver spellings — a named local, whose call is checked where the collection receiver arm runs, and any other expression, whose method call is checked on the `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` dispatch path — and `unwrap_or`'s default is checked against the payload type with the ordinary expected-type rule, so `head(narrow).unwrap_or(7)` adopts a `u8` payload and a mismatched default reports `E_TYPE` with `` `unwrap_or` default must be `int` (the Option's payload), found str ``. All three are pure and allocate nothing. Presence tests lower to a tag test. Scalar `unwrap_or` lowers through exhaustive variant dispatch into one result slot. Aggregate `unwrap_or` uses an explicit internal borrowed selection: the receiver and default evaluate once in source order, the default is eager, and the selected payload borrows both possible input owners under checked ownership. Independent owned storage requires an explicit `.copy()`.
- [OPTION-METHODS-2] A generic body accepts the same three methods through the symbolic checker, so a template whose parameter is an `Option[T]` may call `unwrap_or`, `is_none` and `is_some` without an instantiation: the default is checked against the symbolic payload and the methods preserve symbolic payload types and allocate nothing. An `Option` carrying an owned value or a checked result still requires explicit `match`, exactly as a presence test does, and a receiver that has no such method reports `E_COLLECTION_METHOD` naming `unwrap_or(default)`, `is_none()` and `is_some()` rather than the collection vocabulary.
- [OPTION-METHODS-3] The `sentinel-int` lint reports an `int` used as a sentinel for the absent case: a function whose declared result is an integer and which returns a negative integer literal on some path, where a local bound from that call (or the call itself) is compared against a negative literal — or the `< 0`/`>= 0` presence guards — is reported at that comparison with `` `find_pin` returns -1 on one path and its result is tested against a negative literal here; return `Option[int]` and test `is None` or use `unwrap_or` ``, and a record field or a parameter compared against `-1` in two or more places is reported once with the same rewrite. The rule is `L_SENTINEL_INT`, report-only under `agent` and blocking under `strict` and `safety`; it is exempt by name for `find` and `find_any`, whose `-1` is their own contract.
- [MUT-PARAM-7] A `mut` argument marker inside a generic function body is transparent to the symbolic checker: the marked value is checked with the type it would have unmarked, so a template that forwards `mut` to a declared `mut` parameter checks and its concrete specializations re-check every call with the declared footprint and full origin analysis. The marker therefore has one spelling and one meaning in an ordinary body and in a template alike.

- [UNUSED-CAPABILITY-1] A capability parameter the body never reads is `E_UNUSED_CAPABILITY` at the parameter, replacing the `W_UNUSED_BINDING` it used to be: the message names the parameter and says the signature claims an effect the body cannot have (`remove the parameter or use it`). Reading it at all counts as use, including handing it to another call, so a frame that only forwards its `Host` keeps it; `Stream` and `Host` are covered like every other capability, and a `host def`/`extern def` declaration, which has no body, is exempt.

## Self-hosting oracle

- [SELFHOST-LEXER-DUMP-1] `selka dump-tokens <file.selka>` lexes exactly one file and writes a single-line `tokens-v1` JSON document to stdout with the compiler's own token stream: a `version` string, the `path`, a `count`, and one record per token in source order carrying the exact `@tagName` kind spelling, the half-open byte `start`/`end`, and 1-based `line`/`column`. It stops at the lexer — it never loads imports, parses, checks or lowers — and it is the stage-0 reference a differential self-host lexer is compared against. A lexical failure prints the ordinary diagnostics to stderr, writes no partial document, and exits 1.

## Contracts

- [CONTRACT-SYNTAX-1] `@requires(cond[, "message"])` and `@ensures(cond[, "message"])` attach to a `func` with a body, generic or not, and `@invariant(cond[, "message"])` attaches to a non-generic `record`. Each is repeatable and checked in source order; the message is a plain string literal. The clause on any other declaration (`class`, `union`, `enum`, `result`, a type alias, a constraint, a `const`, an `extern` or `host` def, a generic record) is `E_CONTRACT_TARGET`. A condition that is not `bool` is `E_TYPE`. The condition is part of the parsed declaration: the project merge relocates it with its module and the persisted frontend syntax cache stores it.
- [CONTRACT-REQUIRES-1] A `@requires` condition is checked at function entry, after the parameters are bound and before the body, in a scope holding only the parameters (plus module constants and functions). A false one traps `R_REQUIRES` located at the clause; the report names the function, the clause text and its message, says `precondition violated`, and names the caller that broke it — the next frame out — as `broken by the caller NAME at PATH:LINE:COLUMN`.
- [CONTRACT-ENSURES-1] An `@ensures` condition is checked at every normal return, including the implicit end of a procedure, in a scope holding only the parameters (a parameter's value at exit, so a `mut` parameter shows the body's writes), `result` (the returned value) and `old(...)`. A function returning `Result[T, E]` checks it only on an `Ok` return, with `result` the `T` payload; an `Err` return and a `?` that propagates one skip it. A false one traps `R_ENSURES` located at the clause. In a function with `@ensures`, `result` names the returned value, so a parameter named `result` is `E_CONTRACT_RESULT`, as is `result` in the postcondition of a function that returns no value; a body local named `result` is not visible to the clause and stays legal.
- [CONTRACT-OLD-1] `old(expr)` inside `@ensures` is evaluated once at entry, after the preconditions, in the parameter scope, and the postcondition reads the captured value. It is `E_CONTRACT_OLD` anywhere else — in `@requires`, in `@invariant`, in a body — nested inside another `old`, or with other than one argument. Under default value semantics, a captured collection value is an independent snapshot and `old(xs)` is accepted.
- [CONTRACT-PURE-1] A contract condition and every `old(...)` capture only observe. The checker rejects capability references, including named capability call receivers and nested capability projections, as `E_CONTRACT_EFFECT` ([CONTRACT-CAPABILITY-1]). It also rejects a `mut` call-site marker, assignment, a collection method that changes its receiver, `?`, and ownership transfer of move-only or result-bearing values. Conditions may allocate, and a list literal builds its own value. There is no transitive effect inference.
- [CONTRACT-INVARIANT-1] An `@invariant` condition, which sees only `self`, is checked wherever a value of its type is built or changed: every record construction (named, with defaults), every `replace(...)`, after every field write to a value of that type (`r.f = v`, `r.f += v`, a destructuring target, and a nested `outer.inner.f = v`, which checks `inner`'s type). Typed JSON construction checks the same record invariants before exposing a decoded value; a negative Cents value traps `R_INVARIANT` just as direct construction does. A plain copy is not re-checked. A false one traps `R_INVARIANT` at the construction or write that produced the value, naming the type, the clause text and its message.
- [CONTRACT-TRAP-1] A violated contract ends the program like any other trap, with exit status 1, in every build mode and backend — `run`, `selka test`, a Debug or ReleaseFast build, the fast frame-map protocol and `--release` (LLVM); no mode removes a contract. The runtime reports the contract's own code (`R_REQUIRES`, `R_ENSURES`, `R_INVARIANT`) where a plain `assert` reports `R_ASSERT`, the structured trap record carries the same text as its `operation`, a `--diagnostics=json` record adds a `contract` object with `kind`, `subject`, `clause`, the optional `message` and, for a precondition, the `caller` location, and `selka test` prints `CODE: text` beside the frames.
- [CONTRACT-TRAP-2] Lowering preserves assertion kind separately from message text and calls a dedicated native entry for each contract kind. An ordinary `assert` always traps `R_ASSERT`, even when its literal message starts with `R_REQUIRES: `, `R_ENSURES: ` or `R_INVARIANT: `; its full message is preserved in accounted and fast runtime protocols.
- [CONTRACT-INTERFACE-1] Contracts are part of the published interface. `selka list-interfaces` prints each exported declaration's clauses after its signature, the `--contracts` manifest carries them as `contracts`, `selka context` shows them above the signature, a declaration's `interface_sha256` covers their normalized text, so `api-diff` classifies a contract edit as a breaking interface change, and a `selka test` receipt's implementation hash folds in the text of every contract clause its body checks, including an invariant written on another declaration.
- [CONTRACT-CACHE-1] Every cache sees an edit to a contract clause: a function's semantic-cache region and dependency context start at its first attribute line and collect the names its clauses reference, a record's declaration fingerprint frames its `@invariant` lines, and the native object and metadata caches key on the emitted code and messages. Editing only one `@requires` makes the next `run` and `selka test` over the same caches trap under the new clause, with no stale body, object or receipt reused.
- [CONTRACT-CACHE-2] Invariant checks synthesized inside a function retain only spans inside that function's declaration region, including operator positions and generated locals. Unchanged bodies constructing invariant-carrying values can be reused after source relocation; edits to a referenced invariant still invalidate them.

## Value semantics (default, D9)



- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-1] Value semantics is the only model. The one-release legacy-sharing mode is retired: `--legacy-sharing` and `--value-semantics` are refused (NEW-CORE-S5-RETIRED-1), the front end has no mode switch and reads no `SELKA_VALUE_SEMANTICS`, and `"value_semantics"` in `selka.json` stays an optional boolean (a non-boolean or repeated one is `E_PROJECT_CONFIG`) whose `false` a build refuses as unsupported.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-2] Managed collections, text, bytes and aggregates containing owned values use checked ownership. Owning assignments, construction, stores and returns transfer ownership. Use `.copy()` when both independent values are needed; it duplicates copyable owned children. Ordinary parameters borrow readonly for the call; `mut` parameters borrow caller storage exclusively. Borrowed loop and match bindings cannot escape their owner without an explicit copy. Collection operations with explicit copying contracts retain those contracts (OWNED-LIST-COPYING-1).
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-14] Expression operands evaluate once, left to right. A borrowed operand must remain valid through its consumer; conflicting mutation or transfer requires an explicit copy made before the conflict, or staging independent values. Lowering does not implicitly snapshot a live borrow to permit a conflict.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-3] A declared `mut` parameter receives caller storage: scalar or whole-value rebinding and nested ordinary fields or collection elements update that storage. Its argument must be a writable variable, field or indexed element rooted in one. Function values preserve this capability through exact callback mut positions. Source lambdas report E_LAMBDA_REMOVED under PY-LAMBDA-1; named replacement functions retain the same callback mut contract. Readonly loop and match bindings cannot be mut targets.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-11] An explicit `mut` parameter can receive a whole writable variable or an interior field or element. Captured target indices evaluate once in written argument order, and whole-value rebinding writes back to that same target. Scalar-only records and fixed arrays follow the same caller-storage contract as other ordinary values.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-10] Under checked ownership, a `class` declaration is `E_VALUE_CLASS` even if unused: shared mutable identity must be expressed as a pool handle or other explicit id, while finite value trees use records or recursive unions. The builtin `Cell[T]` is `E_VALUE_CELL` both as a type in a signature or field and as a constructor, because it is a shared mutable slot; use a value record with `mut` and store-back, or a pool handle for identity. A user-declared type named `Cell` is ordinary and remains legal.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-4] A value copied into an explicit `mut` local is an independent writable snapshot: `mut copy = xs.copy(); copy.append(1)` does not change `xs`. Ordinary parameters, const bindings, loop binders and match payload binders are transitively readonly and reject writes with `E_IMMUTABLE`. Copying one into a mut local permits deliberate private updates. Updating an unbound returned temporary rejects with `E_ASSIGNMENT_TARGET`; bind it first to retain its updated value.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-5] An exclusive managed borrow may not overlap a live readonly borrow or another exclusive borrow of the same storage. A readonly scalar argument may observe an earlier mutable argument before the callee starts when evaluating it neither moves nor mutates the owner. An independent copy may be staged before an exclusive borrow. Calls preserve the source overlap rules in MUT-EXCLUSIVE-1.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-6] A declared mut parameter may remain unused or may be copied into private writable storage. Changes to that private snapshot stay private unless explicitly stored back through the parameter. For example, `mut row = rows[i].copy(); smooth(mut row)` leaves the caller row unchanged until `rows[i] = row`; retaining the mut parameter capability is legal.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-7] Under default value semantics `old(expr)` of a value whose writable storage is all collections (a `list`, `dict`, `set`, `byte_buffer` or a record of them) is accepted: the capture is a value of its own.
- [VALUE-SEMANTICS-8] All runnable documentation examples use default checked ownership. Parser, formatter and explicit error examples exercise only their stated stage; they do not claim successful ownership checking or execution.






- [COW-MUT-ARGUMENT-1] A nested mutable collection argument captures its selected writing place once. Accepted calls preserve source argument order and publish the returned child into the captured parent path. An explicit independent copy made before the mutable borrow preserves the prior value. Later argument evaluation may not invalidate an earlier borrow; field, key and index evaluation is never repeated.







- [COW-READ-RECEIVER-1] An explicit collection copy staged before an index or callback preserves the receiver contents and bounds used by the subsequent read. A later mutation of the original source cannot change that independent receiver. Borrowed receivers instead obey checked overlap and lifetime rules; lowering does not create an implicit snapshot to admit conflicting mutation.
- [D1-LIVE-PLACE] The following live-place lowering rules are subject to checked ownership; they do not permit overlapping mutation or transfer of an actively borrowed managed source. Iteration over a place rooted at a local or parameter sees element writes and mut-callee writes through that place, including record-field write-back and fixed-array places. Explicit nested loops retain the same live-source rule. Indices are evaluated once at entry; every indexed managed ancestor's length is checked exactly each step, including ancestors of immutable str/bytes. A live list or byte buffer sees growth and traps a decrease from its last observed length, including after earlier growth; dictionaries, sets and immutable str/bytes terminals trap any observed length change. Same-length str/bytes field replacement is visible; text length and indexing count Unicode scalars. Fixed-array terminal lengths are static. Direct rebinding of the root in the body selects an independent entry snapshot; non-place sources keep their entry behavior. Guards run before each element and after each continued or final iteration, while break exits without another guard.








- [MUT-REBIND-CALLER-1] Whole-variable rebinding of any ordinary mut parameter reaches its caller storage, including scalar, fixed-array, record and managed-collection values. Updates through nested targets and recursive forwarding retain this behavior, while readonly managed inputs remain protected by their borrows, and explicit independent copies preserve their values.













- [MUT-EXPRESSION-STACK-1] A transparent `mut` argument marker consumes exactly one checked target expression, regardless of whether that target lowers to an indexed read, a field or a synthesized block. Nested dictionary mutation arguments compose with later call arguments without checker stack underflow, while missing-marker and nonassignable-target diagnostics remain unchanged.

- [RECURSIVE-FILE-CONTRACT-1] Recursive immutable box admissibility detects builtin `File` and `TextFile` resource leaves through records, variants and nonempty fixed arrays, including declaration-order and generic wrappers, and rejects them with the existing `E_RECURSIVE_RECORD` rule before execution. Zero-length resource arrays contain no obligation; ordinary scoped file handling and acyclic resource/Result transfers keep their existing contracts.





















- [RC-ITERATION-HOLD-TRANSFER-1] An explicitly copied iteration source preserves its entry contents. A loop written as indexed reads from a mutable state observes each current writeback; independent field copies preserve their contents while record-field writeback remains visible to subsequent reads.



















## Compile-time file embedding

- [EMBED-1] `embed_bytes("path")` answers `bytes` and `embed_str("path")` answers `str`, both read from a project file while the program loads, so the data is immutable static content of the binary. They are prelude builtins that a binding, declaration or module name may shadow. Each takes exactly one plain string literal (a variable, `b"..."`, a second argument or a computed path is `E_EMBED_ARGUMENT`), and `embed_str` requires valid UTF-8 (`E_EMBED_UTF8` names the offending byte offset). A call needs no capability parameter and allocates no managed runtime value, so it is permitted under `@noalloc`, in generic bodies, and as the whole initializer of a module `const` whose type is `bytes` for `embed_bytes` and `str` for `embed_str`; reading that constant, locally or through an import, loads static data and allocates nothing. Every byte is kept, including NUL, and an empty file is an empty value. Native embedded objects are ordered by semantic symbol in both whole-program emission and module linking. Checked ownership preserves these results in debug and release builds.
- [EMBED-2] An embedded path is relative to the directory of the source file spelling the call and uses `/`. Absolute paths are `E_EMBED_ABSOLUTE`, an empty path or one holding NUL or a backslash is `E_EMBED_PATH`, and a path that leaves the project root through `..` or through a symbolic link is `E_EMBED_ESCAPE`, decided before the target is probed. The project root is the directory of the nearest `selka.json` above the entry, else the entry's own import directory. A missing file is `E_EMBED_MISSING`, a directory or other non-regular file `E_EMBED_DIRECTORY`, an unreadable file `E_EMBED_UNREADABLE`, a file over 16 MiB `E_EMBED_TOO_LARGE`, and a source that was not loaded from a directory `E_EMBED_UNAVAILABLE`; each is located at the string literal. A symbolic link that stays inside the root is followed.
- [EMBED-3] The loader finds every `embed_bytes("...")` and `embed_str("...")` call while it reads a module's header, reads each file once with the project provider, and records its outcome and BLAKE3 content digest on the module's source, so the checker only looks results up and a failed read is an ordinary diagnostic. That digest enters every key that covers the module: the configured identity the frontend verdict, in-memory verdict, run-manifest and emit-replay caches key on; the dependency context of each declaration whose region holds an embed, which keys checked bodies, check summaries, stand-in units and stored fragments; the implementation hash of a native test; and the test snapshot's resolved identity and materialized copy, where captured files sit at the paths their relative references need.
