- [NEW-CORE-S4-CACHE-1] The core's native driver keeps its own content-keyed stores. A unit's object is keyed by BLAKE3 over the store format, the optimization level, the target triple, the CPU flags and the resolved host CPU, the toolchain's content identity, the runtime's C declaration and layout surface, preprocessing directives and ABI/code-generation flags (function bodies remain in the runtime object's identity) and the unit's exact LLVM text. It lives in the per-user directory of NEW-CORE-S4-RUNTIME-CACHE-1 as `units-v1/ab/<key>.o` (`ab` is the first two hexadecimal key digits) beside a `.sum` record of its size and digest, so new worktrees and test directories share objects. A miss compiles beside its entry and is renamed into place after the toolchain revalidates. A hit starts no process and is served only when its bytes match the record, so a damaged entry is recompiled, never linked. Misses compile in parallel with `zig cc -c`, largest first, up to `SELKA_JOBS` or the CPUs of the process's affinity mask. `--link` C sources compile with `-MMD` into the work directory's `foreign-v1`, keyed by their path, spelling, working directory, bytes, settings and toolchain, and are served only while every header their last depfile listed keeps its recorded bytes; a file that changed during the compile pins nothing. The linked program, keyed by every object's bytes and the link settings, is kept in the work directory's `programs-v1` and copied to the output, so a build whose objects all match starts no process. Every `zig` process gets a fresh private local cache inside a staging directory removed when the build ends, and an unproved toolchain uses no shared or persistent store. Reads verify the sharded path first and then a legacy flat path. Verified unit hits refresh retention recency. Developer maintenance inventories managed objects, checksums and runtime source snapshots (including recognized partials) in a size ledger, migrates flat pairs by hardlink under shared namespace leases, and removes aliases or bounded LRU batches only while holding the exclusive peers of the builds' permanent leases. Busy leases and incomplete inventories report an unmet cap rather than authorizing deletion. Unsupported C surface syntax conservatively binds complete runtime source. An implementation-only function-body edit that preserves the extracted ABI surface and exact guest LLVM changes the runtime object and link identities while preserving guest unit keys; declarations, aggregate layout, macros, preprocessing directives (including inside bodies) and flags remain unit inputs.
- [RUNTIME-DIGEST-MEMO-1] The two digests of the ordinary runtime that depend on its C source alone, the declaration-surface digest of `core/runtime_abi.zig` and the BLAKE3 of the whole text, are recorded in the shared runtime directory as `runtime-digests-v1/COMPILER_IDENTITY.memo`: a magic line, both digests and a BLAKE3 checksum of the rest. They are a function of the embedded source and the scanner, which `compiler_identity` covers, so a valid record stands for them and a build reads 128 bytes instead of scanning and hashing 475 KB in a Debug compiler (about 90 ms). A missing, truncated, damaged or foreign record is recomputed and rewritten through a staging file renamed into place, and a failed write loses only the saving. The ABI digest takes the surface digest and, when no layout or sanitizer option is set, the implementation digest is the recorded BLAKE3, which is the value the full computation gives; a layout or sanitizer option computes both from the text. The counting runtime of `--counters` builds (the ordinary text with the counting hooks inserted, `core/count_runtime.zig`) has a record of its own, `runtime-digests-v1/COMPILER_IDENTITY-counted.memo`, with a different magic line so that neither variant's record stands for the other; the counting text is built only where an object has to be compiled from it, not to key a build. Every key derived from them is unchanged.
- [NATIVE-TARGET-ONCE-1] The digest of the native CPU and code-generation flags (`tool_identity.nativeTarget`) is detected once per process and remembered in a write-once cell owned by the process wiring (`wiring/native_target_memo.zig`): the machine and the process's loader cannot change while it runs, and detecting them reads the loader and libc files, about 3 ms in the Debug compiler, which every toolchain identification paid two to four times. The first digest stored wins and a reader sees all of it or nothing; the digest itself is computed as before. The cell is a file of the process wiring, not a variable of `tool_identity`, because only the wiring owns mutable global state.
- [FAST-SHA256-1] The SHA-256 the compiler spends on large inputs, the component artifacts it verifies on every `selka test` and `build` and the Zig toolchain closure it approves from a cold cache, runs through `src/fast_sha256.zig`, which has the standard library type's `init`, `update`, `final` and `hash`. An executable that links the separately optimized digest object (`selka_juice_sha256_init`, `_update`, `_final`, weak references) hashes through it, and any other binary uses the standard implementation, so every digest is the standard SHA-256 either way. In the Debug compiler the standard rounds run at about 15 MB/s, so a cold toolchain approval takes about 40 s against about 1 s through the object.
- [FAST-BLAKE3-1] The BLAKE3 the core spends on large inputs, the object and program bytes it verifies against their recorded digests on every link, the replay record and source digests, the stage-pack indexes, blocks and records, and the toolchain files it fingerprints, runs through `src/core/fast_blake3.zig`, which has the standard library type's unkeyed `init`, `update`, `final` and a one-call `hash`. It calls a separately compiled ReleaseFast object (`selka_fast_blake3_*`) that every executable compiling the wrapper links, so a digest is the standard BLAKE3 either way. In the Debug compiler the standard rounds run at about 30 MB/s, so verifying a linked program or a pack index was a fifth of a `selka` invocation's samples. Every other digest the core computes, the canonical forms of functions, the emission, lowering and optimization keys, the unit keys and the test-runner receipts included, goes through it as well, and a one-call digest into a shorter array or slice is the prefix of the 32-byte one, as the standard type's is. (A hasher kept as a struct field default is initialized by its constructor instead, since the object cannot run at compile time.)
- [NEW-CORE-S4-REPLAY-1] An unchanged `selka-core build` replays its last identical build: the work directory's `replay-v1` slot for the entry and settings (compiler identity, flags, working directory, entry and project configuration resolved afresh, toolchain identity, and the environment except the shell's `_`, `SHLVL` and `OLDPWD`) lists every module source, embedded file, generated interface file, `--link` source and depfile header the build read, with the digest of the bytes it used. When all of them, the toolchain and the program entry are unchanged, the build copies the recorded program to the output and repeats the recorded report without checking, lowering, emitting or starting a process. `--emit-llvm` builds neither replay nor record, and an unpinned `--link` input, an unresolved embed or an unproved toolchain records nothing. Replay records bind each present dependency's inode, size, modification time and change time, captured around a full hash that matched the build's bytes. An identical status reuses that digest without reading file contents; any status change rehashes the file, and missing or unstable inputs miss. Recording always rehashes before trusting a status. `--verify-cache` bypasses replay and still rebuilds or rejects corrupted and forged objects.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-VERIFY-CACHE-1] `selka-core build --verify-cache` also recompiles every unit the object store served and fails the build, naming the unit, when the fresh object's bytes differ from the cached one; such a build never replays. `--cache-report` adds one line with the units linked, reused, compiled and verified, whether the program was reused, and the processes started, or says that the build replayed.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-COLD-1] A first build on a machine compiles the core runtime object with `-g0` (the trap walker reads only `selka-core` compile units and the machine code is unchanged) and runs no `zig build-obj` for the number-text runtime, which ships prebuilt (NEW-CORE-S3-NUMBER-1): the processes of a cold build are its unit compiles, the runtime object and the link.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-LINK-1] A debug `selka-core` link with no `--link` C source passes `-fno-sanitize=undefined`, so Zig neither builds nor links its UBSan runtime: no core unit or runtime object calls it, and building it cost a fresh Zig cache about 10 s. A debug build with a `--link` source, which `zig cc -O0` may instrument, and every release link keep Zig's default; the flag is part of the linked program's store key.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TOOL-1] A build's toolchain identity covers the `zig` executable's content, each library root's own generation (entries added, removed or retargeted), the runtime and discovery inputs and the native CPU; it does not rescan every library file. A library tree's contents are reviewed once against the approved distribution, memoized by that identity, and an in-place edit inside an installed toolchain is not a build input.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-ASSERT-MESSAGE-1] An `assert condition, message` whose message is a computed `str` evaluates it only when the condition fails, writes it and a newline to stderr, then traps `R_ASSERT` at the condition with no message on the first line and an empty `SELKA-TRAP` operation, exactly as the old core does; `assert False, e` in a match arm does the same.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-STEPS-1] A core test's `--max-steps` budget (default 1,000,000) charges one step per executed block of an original program function, as the old core charges one per dispatch, so a test the old core finishes within its budget finishes within the core's; generated adapters are not charged.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-CAP-FIELD-1] A capability token (`Stream`, `Host`, a provider capability) may be a record field or payload in the new core: it is an `i64` with no count, so ownership and authority rules are unchanged; fixed arrays of them follow NEW-CORE-S5-CAP-COLLECTIONS-1, and the checker rejects lists and dictionaries of them (`E_LIST_CAPABILITY`) in both cores.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-FRAMES-1] `selka-core test --max-frames N` (also `--max-frames=N`, or `limits.test.max_frames` in `selka.json`) bounds the calls of original program functions one test trial may nest, 1,024 by default as in the old core: each call enters a frame on its first block and leaves it before every return, and the call past the budget traps `R_FRAME_LIMIT` with the old core's advice line (`R_STEP_LIMIT` and `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` end with theirs); generated adapters are not counted and programs built outside tests are not instrumented. The trial runs on a stack mapped for the whole budget (32 KiB per frame plus 8 MiB, one guard page), so recursion within the budget never depends on the native stack limit. A zero budget is a usage error, and a budget whose stack the byte ceiling cannot back is refused before anything is built with the old core's text and exit status 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-MEMORY-1] `selka-core test --max-memory SIZE` (bytes or a `K`/`M`/`G` size; else `SELKA_MEMORY_LIMIT`, else `limits.test.max_memory`, else 512 MiB; `0` removes it) bounds each test process: the runtime counts the chunks small blocks are carved from and every live large block, and an allocation past the ceiling traps `R_MEMORY_BUDGET`; the process's `RLIMIT_AS` is lowered (never raised) to the ceiling plus 256 MiB, and an allocation the OS then refuses reports `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` too. The ceiling and the frame budget are part of the test receipt key. Programs built outside tests carry no ceiling and pay nothing on the inline allocation paths.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TAKE-1] An owned managed field read and written back through a mut record transfers its selected storage through the update. Byte-buffer appends through mut record fields preserve output, range validation and negative-bound traps, and complete without leaked blocks. Repeated append operations retain capacity instead of copying the complete buffer on each call.
- [NEW-CORE-TAKE-READS-1] An exact fixed-array slot reconstruction may transfer the old slot through a matching writeback when the owner and captured normalized index agree, the taking read precedes replacement, and every path reaches that replacement or a trap. Earlier borrowed reads must end before transfer or begin after replacement; live aliases or unmatched indices cannot authorize a transfer. Source index expressions are evaluated once, and explicit independent copies preserve their values across the update.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-SITES-1] Traps, assertions, effect sites and error traces carry pointers to private immutable site records containing the original source path. Debug compile units and file tables preserve caller paths across separately compiled modules. Core clones retain their exact specialized callee and original call positions in sparse optimizer metadata outside the IR, composed through loop duplication, ownership edits and LLVM inline records. Ancestry deeper than 32 frames refuses expansion before changing IR.
- [PY-REPEAT-2] A contextual list constructor in repetition keeps its list element type independently of a runtime integer count: `[0] * width` and `width * [0]` typecheck in both cores, including a declared `list[u8]` result.
- [NEW-CORE-BUFFER-S4-1] Numeric byte-buffer append formats integers and binary64 values in bounded stack storage with the ordinary string spelling, then reserves once and copies the fragment once into the sole owner. No temporary string block or per-octet append loop is constructed; fact verification preserves length and capacity checks.
- [NEW-CORE-PLACE-S4-1] Accepted nested indexed assignments transfer the selected child owner through its update and replacement, preserving captured parent and index paths without repeating evaluation. The updated child is reattached exactly once; unrelated fields and elements retain their values. Source overlap checks and IR lifetime verification reject invalid ownership transfers.
- [NEW-CORE-INLINE-S4-1] Release builds also inline internal helpers with consuming mut parameters, at most 20 blocks and 100 instructions when they contain no calls, prints or trace operations, cloning predecessor order, loop metadata and trap spans. A mut return record used only through field projections is decomposed into SSA results before ownership analysis, preserving scalar and collection return values without an aggregate materialization or retain.
- [NEW-CORE-FILL-S4-1] After stack promotion, a heap list filled once per counted iteration from zero may reserve an initial prefix of `max(count, 0)`, capped at 4096 elements. Only the push that supplies that loop's backedge, whose receiver remains exclusively owned, uses the counted index and skips capacity checks within the prefix; longer fills and later appends retain ordinary growth. Verify builds assert the current length and room before each such store. Capacity hints do not change value semantics or loop evaluation.

## New core S4 projects

- [NEW-CORE-S4-PROJECT-1] Core project entries resolve nested module paths and standalone test files, discover the nearest strict selka.json, and enforce source budgets, import policy, embedded-asset roots and required generated interfaces before lowering.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-1] Core file and directory test runs share one worker budget from explicit jobs, SELKA_JOBS or available CPUs; empty selections exit 3 and small suites never start surplus workers.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-2] Directory test discovery includes mains, standalone test entries and orphan test libraries. Every test-declaring source must be accounted for by a checked root's report, from a child or from the run's own process (NEW-CORE-TEST-UNION-1); a missing program or inventory fails the run.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-3] Successful core test receipts key the exact reachable program (NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6), generation schema, settings, compiler/runtime/toolchain identity, native inputs, provider settings and the environment variables of NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-5, under `tests/receipts-v3` and `tests/manifests-v3`; v2 records are never read. Every path a key names is relative to the entry's root (RECEIPT-PATHS-1). Checksummed manifests replay only while all loaded source, embedded asset and configuration bytes remain unchanged and only in the root that wrote them. Fact verification and leak checking are keyed modes that reuse their own receipts (EXEC-RECEIPT-4); force executes trials; failures never become receipts. Behind this IR tier an execution tier (EXEC-RECEIPT-1) reuses a verdict by the linked program's bytes.
- [EXEC-RECEIPT-1] A test's execution receipt keys `core-test-exec-v3`, the runner identity (RUNNER-IDENTITY-1), the linked program's exact key (`driver.Outcome.program_key`: toolchain, target, every object including the runtime, the linker environment), the execution settings (mode, facts, code generation, seed, runs, budgets, the `SELKA_CORE_LEAKCHECK` value), the test's name, the cases, configuration and policy bytes and the keyed environment, and no compiler identity, cache planner or path. After a program is linked and before its first trial the runner looks up the whole-program receipt, then each test's; a hit is `cached` with reason `whole-exec-receipt-hit` or `exec-receipt-hit`, runs no trial and also saves the test's IR receipt, so a compiler edit that leaves the program's bytes unchanged reruns nothing. A pass saves both tiers; a forced run looks nothing up but still keys its tests, so its passes refresh their receipts. The JSON report counts `exec_receipts: {hits, saves}` and the text summary appends `, N exec-receipt hits` when there are any.
- [EXEC-RECEIPT-2] An edit to a runner source changes the runner identity and every execution key, as does any change to the program's bytes (an emitter or runtime edit); either misses.
- [EXEC-RECEIPT-3] A built program also keys all its consulted tests at once, over the sorted test names (`core-test-whole-exec-v3`): one receipt read answers every test, and adding, removing or renaming a test misses. The receipt is saved when every test it covers passed or was reused.
- [EXEC-RECEIPT-4] Plain, fact-verification (`--verify-facts`) and leak-checked (`SELKA_CORE_LEAKCHECK` non-zero) runs are all admitted and key three distinct settings in both tiers, so none hits another's receipt. Force, `--link` inputs, dynamic-loader overrides and an unproved toolchain still refuse; a missing cache planner refuses only the IR tier.
- [EXEC-SHARED-1] Execution-tier success receipts may use the native shared test store under their existing `core-test-exec-v3` and `core-test-whole-exec-v3` program-and-runner keys. Callable identities and observable error traces can share this tier because the compiled program bytes bind them. Native Host capabilities, live host effects, C extern declarations, external source/assets/settings and provider-overlay groups stay private with explicit refusal reasons. A whole-program shared receipt requires every consulted test to qualify and pass or reuse a success. Source, configuration, toolchain and planner identities are revalidated before shared lookup and publication; force invalidates old shared successes before trials; failed or incomplete execution publishes no success.

- [RECEIPT-PATHS-1] Receipt settings, manifest and IR keys name the entry, cases, configuration and policy relative to the entry's root (a single-file entry by its basename), and the IR key's source path is the root-relative entry path; two copies of a project at different absolute paths key alike. The trap-report path a test program embeds is unchanged.
- [ENTRY-PATH-1] A build names its entry in LLVM's `source_filename` and the entry unit's `DIFile` without the checkout's location: a header-less entry file by its basename (its path relative to its own directory, the name its receipts key), a module-headed entry by its module-root-relative path; a test program names its entry by basename. The same sources built or tested from two directories by the same relative spelling emit identical LLVM, link identical programs and report identical `cache.execution_key` values. Trap and trace text still reports the path the command was given.
- [RUNNER-IDENTITY-1] The build publishes, beside `compiler_identity`, a `runner_identity`: SHA-256 over the domain `selka-runner-identity-v2`, the Zig version, the optimize mode and each covered source's path and content digest (COMPILER-IDENTITY-TOKENS-1), in path order. It covers the production sources under `src` that build a test executable's driver, fork trials, apply budgets and judge outcomes: every `core/test_runner*` and `core/property_*` source, `core/test_builder.zig`, `core/test_shared_store.zig`, `core/run_budget.zig`, `core/trap_contract.zig`, `core/process_spawn.zig`, `core/native_receipt.zig`, `core/cli_run.zig`, `core/cli_args.zig`, `core/pipeline.zig`, `core/driver.zig`, `core/unit_cache.zig`, `core/target.zig`, `core/version.zig` and `memory_budget.zig`, plus the capability-eligibility traversal and body-completeness producers (`typed_walk.zig`, `typed.zig`, `type_identity.zig`, `sema.zig`, `sema_contracts.zig`, `entry_adapter.zig`, `semantic_entry.zig` and `semantic_cache.zig`), listed as `runner_inputs`. Test-only sources move neither identity; an edit to a front-end or emitter source outside that eligibility closure moves `compiler_identity` and leaves `runner_identity` unchanged, an edit to a runner source moves both, and `compiler_identity` keeps its inputs.
- [COMPILER-IDENTITY-TOKENS-1] `compiler_identity` (domain `selka-compiler-identity-v3`) and `runner_identity` digest each covered source by a content digest: for a Zig source (a path ending `.zig`), 128 bits over the sequence of its tokens as `std.zig.Tokenizer` reads them, each token's tag and spelling, leaving out comments, doc comments (`///`, `//!`) and every character between tokens; for any other file (the C runtime, the standard library's `.selka` sources, `.json`, `.zon`), 128 bits over its bytes. A comment, a doc comment or a change of layout in a Zig source therefore leaves both identities, and every cache keyed by them (build replay, front-end and semantic stores, stage packs, test execution receipts), as it was, while a change of any token, including text inside a string literal that looks like a comment, a line of a multi-line string literal, a number or an operator, moves them. The tree uses no `@src()`, no reflection over source text and no `@embedFile` of Zig, so two sources with the same tokens build compilers that differ only in the line numbers of Debug stack traces.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-5] A core test receipt keys only the environment variables that can change an outcome: the keyed class of the environment identity contract (ENV-IDENTITY-1), which covers every `MYL_` and `SELKA_` variable except the placement ones (cache and store locations such as `SELKA_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE`, job counts such as `SELKA_JOBS`, the parent tool ticket, the process-placement oracles `SELKA_TEST_ISOLATE` and `SELKA_TEST_CHILDREN`, and the telemetry directories `SELKA_TEST_TELEMETRY_DIR` and `SELKA_VERIFICATION_TELEMETRY_DIR`), the C compiler and Zig toolchain inputs (`CPATH`, `C_INCLUDE_PATH`, `CPLUS_INCLUDE_PATH`, `OBJC_INCLUDE_PATH`, `LIBRARY_PATH`, `COMPILER_PATH`, `GCC_EXEC_PREFIX`, `CCC_OVERRIDE_OPTIONS`, `SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH`, `ZERO_AR_DATE`, `ZIG`, `ZIG_LIB_DIR`, `ZIG_LIBC`) and `LD_*`, but never the host class (`PYTHON*`, the locale, `TZ`), which cannot change a guest outcome. A shell's `_` or `OLDPWD`, a terminal's variables or a wrapper such as `time` leave receipts valid; a test receives only `Stream` parameters, so it cannot read the environment, and dynamic-loader overrides still disable reuse. `SELKA_CORE_LEAKCHECK` selects a keyed mode rather than refusing reuse, and its value also enters the settings explicitly (EXEC-RECEIPT-4).
- [ENV-IDENTITY-1] One contract file, `src/environment_identity.zon`, sorts every environment variable into four classes for the compiler, the build graph and the verification coordinator alike: keyed (`NAME=VALUE` enters every identity, guest and host, and children receive it), host (`NAME=VALUE` enters host-tool identities only and children receive it: `PYTHON*`, `LANG`, `LANGUAGE`, `LC_*`, `TZ`, which change Python drivers and host tools but never a guest build or test), placement (children receive it, no identity contains it: homes, scratch, `PATH`, content-addressed store locations, job and slot settings, telemetry directories) and dropped (children never receive it and no identity contains it). Guest test receipts key only the keyed class; host tooling keys the keyed and host classes. An entry is an exact name or a prefix ending in `*`; an exact entry beats every prefix, the longest matching prefix beats a shorter one, and a name nothing matches is dropped, so `SELKA_JOBS` is placement although `SELKA_*` is keyed. The Zig classifier reads the file at compile time and rejects an empty, misplaced-`*` or duplicate entry across all four lists; the Python reader parses the same file with the same rules.
- [ENV-IDENTITY-2] The environment digest has a scope, guest (the keyed class) or host (the keyed and host classes), and is the lowercase hex SHA-256 of length-prefixed fields (a u64 little-endian length, then the bytes): the domain `environment-identity-v1`, then the name and value of every variable the scope takes in bytewise name order, then `tool:NAME` and the hex digest of every named tool in name order. Placement and dropped variables and input order never change either digest, and a host variable such as `PYTHONPATH` or `LANG` changes only the host digest; any keyed value or tool digest changes both. The Zig and Python implementations reproduce the same classification of a 200-variable golden fixture and the same guest and host digests.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6] A core test's receipt key hashes its program's Core IR before passes, every function, instruction, block, loop, type and string, with each span replaced by the file, line and column emission reads, and the entry path, rather than the emitted LLVM. Passes and emission are pure functions of those and the keyed settings, so looking up a cached test emits no code, and an edit that only moves code in other files keeps the key.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-QUEUE-1] A directory run starts its roots largest root file first (ties in path order) and runs them on a work queue: each of its workers starts the next root as soon as its last one ends, instead of waiting for a whole wave. Each child's `--jobs` is the run's budget divided by the roots that can still run beside it — those running, itself included, and those not yet started, at most the worker count — and never below one; when roots also run in the run's own process (NEW-CORE-TEST-UNION-1), each such root may use the whole budget and holds one of the budget's permits per test process, and a child holds its share of the permits while it runs, so the run never has more test processes than the budget. A test program's own test processes are likewise taken from a queue by its worker threads. Reports keep root order and test order whatever order the work finishes in.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-BATCH-1] A test executable's plain tests (every test but a property test, which keeps its own processes under PROPERTY-TEST-4) share processes. Each worker takes the next run of pending tests — the pending count over the worker count, rounded up — and one process answers them in table order, each in its own forked trial with fresh capability stand-ins, its own captured output and its own step, frame, memory and millisecond wall-clock budgets, so every verdict, reason and trace is the one the test gets alone. A test that ends its process without answering keeps the answers before it: its trial's timeout is its verdict (`timed out after N ms`), any other ending (a signal or an exit) reruns it alone for a verdict of its own, and the tests after it continue in a new process. `SELKA_TEST_ISOLATE=1` gives every plain test its own process, the differential oracle.
- [NEW-CORE-SPAWN-1] The core starts a child process — a test executable, a build tool, a directory run's child, the program `selka run` runs — without copying its own address space: a `clone(CLONE_VM | CLONE_VFORK)` child on a stack of its own takes the argv, environment and standard streams the caller names, resets every signal handler, restores the caller's signal mask and calls `execve`, searching `PATH` for a bare name. A program that cannot start fails with the error `std.process` gives (`FileNotFound`, `AccessDenied`, ...), and a run's timeout and output limits behave as `std.process.run`'s.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-UNION-1] A directory run runs its roots in its own process where they can share one check: a root that opens with its `module` header and declares no `main`, in a project whose configuration names no import policy and no interface files, with no providers in its cases file, in a run that selects by no git revision. Such a root first replays its recorded report as a child would; the roots of one project that miss are checked once together, as a synthetic entry importing each of them, and each root then selects, builds and runs its tests from its share of that program — the modules its own import graph reaches, which are the modules its own check would load — so its tests, receipts, report and exit status are the ones its child gives. Every other root, and every root of a project whose shared check fails, runs in a child process, whose own check reports its own diagnostics. `SELKA_TEST_CHILDREN=1` runs every root in a child, the differential oracle.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-SHARED-1] Explicit shared native test storage (`native-tests-v3`) is a second location for the private store's own root-relative IR keys (RECEIPT-PATHS-1), so it reuses only completed deterministic successes whose reachable code, logical source locations, exact inputs, compiler/runtime/toolchain and execution-context identities agree. Any test without live effects shares, under the legacy property policy, when its entry, cases, configuration, policy, sources and embedded assets lie inside its root; `--link` tests stay private. Each worktree checks its own current sources and reports its own diagnostics; literal path values and observable traces are never blindly remapped. Live effects and unproved portability remain fresh. Private manifests and mutable frontend state stay private. Versioned bounded receipts publish atomically under process locks; corrupt or missing receipts miss, invalid configured storage fails explicitly, and force bypasses shared reuse.
- [SHARED-PROGRAM-2] The shared store keys a linked program by `core-shared-program-v2`, its exact program key and the linker environment, without the compiler identity: the objects determine the bytes and the store's byte verification guards the rest.
- [COMPILER-SHARED-CACHE-1] Explicit shared compiler artifact storage transports immutable parsed syntax, qualified ordinary typed bodies and exact linked programs between compatible worktrees. Parser authority, compiler and serializer contracts, semantic source layout and context, and native object/tool/runtime/linker inputs determine admission; source literals and diagnostic paths are never blindly rewritten. Fresh declaration and policy checks, mutable indexes, rendered verdicts and materialized executables remain local. Bounded checksummed records publish atomically under per-key locks; an existing verified record is compared in place, so republishing identical bytes stages and renames nothing; differing valid outputs for one key are a logged miss (SHARED-STORE-3), and damaged or absent records miss. No-cache and verification requests suppress shared compiler participation; configured invalid storage fails before private replay.
- [NATIVE-CACHE-RETENTION-1] Shared native compiler artifacts and test successes use retention-aware namespaces isolated from older nonparticipating producers. Every write holds a shared process lease through staging, publication and publication-lock waits, and every work claim through its work; both take it without waiting (SHARED-STORE-5), while reads hold none. Collection acquires an exclusive nonblocking lease and skips an active store. Verified hits renew eviction recency without establishing validity. Configurable byte and entry budgets evict least recently used records; optional age limits use last verified access, with no age expiry by default. Opportunistic collection is throttled and explicit native collection previews unless applied. Bounded inventories preserve unknown files and report incomplete or unmet budgets, permanent coordination locks survive collection, and evicted or damaged results safely miss and refill. Policy changes are explicit and persistent; neither collection nor configuration requires Python or server admission.
- [SHARED-STORE-3] A shared compiler-artifact or native test-receipt publication that finds a verified record for its key holding different bytes is a miss, never an error: the existing record is kept, nothing is published, one line `mismatch <kind> <key> existing=<digest> attempted=<digest>` with no timestamp is appended to the namespace's `mismatches.log` under the key's publication lock, and the build or test run goes on with its own result. A linked-program mismatch is counted as `shared_mismatches` in `cache-report-v1` and named in the `--cache-report` text line when it is not zero. A damaged record is still replaced, and a record that fails verification on lookup or restore is a miss.
- [SHARED-STORE-4] The shared compiler-artifact store (`SELKA_COMPILER_SHARED_CACHE`) and the native test-receipt store (`--shared-cache-dir`, then `SELKA_TEST_SHARED_CACHE`) resolve through one policy: a non-empty setting names an explicit store, an empty one disables sharing, and an unset one names a per-user default (`$XDG_CACHE_HOME/selka/compiler-artifacts` when that is absolute, else `~/.cache/selka/compiler-artifacts`, and `native-tests` beside it) while the `default_sharing` switch is on, as enabled after the default-store qualification rollout. An explicit store that cannot be opened fails the command as before; a default store that cannot be created or opened prints one warning line and the command runs without sharing.
- [SHARED-STORE-5] Opening, looking up and restoring never wait on a sweep. Opening an existing namespace takes no lock (the exclusive `.lock` is taken only while the format marker is first created), and lookups and restores hold no lease, so they complete while a sweep holds the namespace and treat a record it evicted, truncated or removed as a miss. Publications and work claims take the shared lease without waiting: while a sweep holds the namespace they skip publication or run unclaimed. The throttled automatic sweep only tries its exclusive admission, so it never waits either.
- [SHARED-STORE-6] Several processes or threads may open one fresh shared compiler-artifact or native test-receipt store at once and publish into it immediately. Namespace creation tolerates directories a peer created, the format marker is staged and renamed into place only under the exclusive `.lock` after re-reading it, and an open that fails retries its creation a bounded number of times, re-reading what a peer published meanwhile, so only a store that stays unusable fails the command. Concurrent publications of one key's identical bytes store it once and otherwise find it present: none fails, none is counted or logged as a mismatch, and no staged partial file remains.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-WHOLE-1] A test program also keys all its ready tests at once, by joining their per-test keys (NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-1) and configured cases. When that key has a receipt, every one of them is `cached` with a single receipt read; the key's receipt is saved when every test it covers passed or was reused. Otherwise each test's own key decides, so a test whose own reachable program did not change stays `cached`. Tests that may call `Host`, a builder refusal and an unreadable result skip the whole-file key.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-1] A test program lowers all its ready tests once and derives each test's receipt key from that one lowering instead of lowering each test's program again: every function gets a canonical digest — callees by name and signature, strings by their bytes, types by structure (a cycle through a box by its depth), equality adapters by the type they compare and rendering adapters by their descriptors — and a test's key hashes its own root, the sorted digests of every function it reaches, its entry's inputs (streams, capability families, seed, parameter and result type names and result tags), the schema of the types its parameters and result reach when its entry generates or encodes values (types it cannot reach stay out), the settings of NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-3 and the entry path. Keys therefore change exactly when NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6's per-test program does, independent of where the shared lowering placed functions, types or strings. Lowering metadata the digests do not recognize falls back to per-test lowering; `SELKA_TEST_KEYS=per-test` forces it, as the oracle the derived keys are checked against.
- [NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-2] Neither a derived key (NEW-CORE-TEST-KEYS-1) nor a per-test program key (NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-6) hashes a loop's checker id, which counts loops program-wide and so moves with which modules the checker rechecked rather than loaded from its cache; a loop is keyed by its blocks. An edit that adds or removes a function elsewhere, or its revert, therefore no longer reruns tests whose code it did not touch.
- [NEW-CORE-S4-TEST-4] Declarative test cases accept explicit arguments, expectations, reasoned skips and exact provider overlays. Providers replace existing dependency paths, preserve the replaced module's own exported types and functions and the selected test signature with every type it reaches (other modules' exports are not compared, so a provider may import fewer modules) and retain original paths and embedded-asset roots. Invalid names, fields or traversal paths fail.

## New core S5

- [NEW-CORE-S5-RUNTIME-LAYOUT-1] The core runtime object compiles with `-falign-functions=64`, and its stack-floor signal stack is mapped when the program starts rather than reserved as a 256 KiB static array. Every runtime function starts on a cache line, so a change to one runtime function no longer moves the others' alignment, and no large `.bss` moves where the heap begins. Before this, unrelated runtime edits moved allocation- and text-heavy rows by 5–10% (S5-X: `packet_decode`, `csv_aggregate`, with identical instruction counts and more branch misses).
- [NEW-CORE-S5-COLD-INDEX-1] A list or fixed-array bounds trap that reports the negative index the program wrote (NEW-CORE-S5-UNITS-1) recovers it inside the trap's cold block (a list's written index is tested first and reported as written, NEW-CORE-F2-WRAP-1), so an in-bounds index costs no more than before: the in-bounds path carries only the comparison and the branch.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-VIEW-LEN-1] `len()` of a dictionary's `keys()` or `values()` view, or of any buffer operation typed `int`, is an `int` like `len(d)`: the IR's `u64` length is converted exactly, so the value can be stored, added or returned from `main`; it was an invalid-IR internal error before.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CAP-COLLECTIONS-1] A fixed array of capabilities (`[N]Stream`, `[N]Host`, `[N]Filesystem`, …, nested or inside a record or union payload) builds in the new core as the old core runs it: a `Stream` or `Host` element is an uncounted `i64` token and a provider capability element keeps its count, so indexing, element writes, iteration, `enumerate`, `len`, copies, `mut` parameters and results print, exit and release exactly as the old core's build does.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-RUNTIME-ABI-1] Calls to the core's C runtime helpers follow the same SysV rule as source externs: a `bool`, `u8` or `u16` argument is zero-extended and an `i8` or `i16` one sign-extended on the declaration and the call (`myl_core_text_case`, `myl_core_text_repr`, the trims, the sorts, `myl_core_str_from_bool` and the rest), so a Clang-compiled helper that reads the widened register never sees undefined upper bits.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-EXTERN-ABI-1] A source `extern def` argument of type `i8` or `i16` is passed sign-extended, and one of type `u8`, `u16` or `bool` zero-extended, as SysV x86-64 C callers do (LLVM `signext`/`zeroext` on the declaration and the call), so a C callee that reads the widened register sees the value; wider integers, `float` and results are unchanged. Aggregate (record, array, union, `str`, collection, capability) parameters and results and `mut` parameters stay rejected by the checker (`E_EXTERN_SIGNATURE`, `E_MUTATION_MARKER`), as in the old core.
- [LOCAL-STDLIB-SOURCE-1] A standard source served by the project provider keeps its project-relative path in diagnostics and module metadata; it receives compiler-internal intrinsic trust only when its bytes exactly match the embedded module.
- [CHECK-LIBRARY-3] A mainless `check` entry that imports modules is checked through the project library path, including with `--local-stdlib`; it checks every body and boundary without inventing a `main` or taking the executable path.
- [CLI-LOCAL-STDLIB-1] `selka build`, `run`, `check` and `test` accept `--local-stdlib`, which resolves a standard module name from a file beside the entry before the embedded copy; the switch is part of the build and test replay keys, so runs with and without it cannot replay across each other. A standard-named file named directly as the check entry is checked from its path even without the switch, not from the embedded module it shadows; the switch is only needed when another module imports it. Without it, an imported local standard-named source is ignored and a build or run warns which file it ignored. It is a development switch for editing `src/stdlib` without rebuilding the compiler, not a change to the published standard library.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CLI-1] `selka build` (and `build --run`), `run`, `run-project` and `test` run the new core (`src/core/cli.zig`), and `dump-ir`, `dump-typed` and `coverage` are `selka` developer commands. They take the old CLI's spellings: `--flag VALUE` or `--flag=VALUE`; `--qbe PATH` names a tool the new core never runs and is ignored; `--project` and `--whole-program` ask for nothing more (S5-D6); `--mode ReleaseFast` is `--release`. A build or run prints the front end's warnings to stderr before the program runs, as the old CLI did, and `--diagnostics=json` renders them, check errors, the new core's refusals and a trap as `diagnostic-v1` records (a trap's record carries its frames and the runtime's `trap_detail`).
- [NEW-CORE-S5-RUN-1] `selka run FILE.selka [options] [-- ARGS...]` builds the program with the new core and runs it: its exit status is `main`'s value `& 0xFF`, a trap exits 1, a check error 1, a usage or host failure 2, and a signal 128 plus its number. Every value after `--` is the program's. Grants (`--restrict`, `--allow-*`) and `--print-result` travel in `SELKA_OPTIONS`, which the runtime reads: a repeated or empty grant is a usage error, an ungranted capability under `--restrict` exits 2 before `main` runs, and `--print-result` prints `main`'s whole value on its own line at exit, in a built program too. `--max-steps N`, `--max-frames N`, `--timeout SECONDS` and `--max-memory SIZE` (each also `--flag=N`; else `limits.run` in `selka.json`; memory else `SELKA_MEMORY_LIMIT`, then 4 GiB; `0` removes the byte ceiling) are installed by `main` before anything else: steps and frames are charged as a test's (NEW-CORE-S4-STEPS-1, NEW-CORE-S4-FRAMES-1) and trap `R_STEP_LIMIT` and `R_FRAME_LIMIT`, the byte ceiling traps `R_MEMORY_BUDGET` and lowers `RLIMIT_AS` to it plus 256 MiB (NEW-CORE-S4-MEMORY-1), and the wall-clock limit ends the program with `R_TIMEOUT` and status 1. The program gets the caller's address space back from the compiler's own ceiling and, under a frame budget, a stack of 32 KiB per frame plus 8 MiB; a frame budget the byte ceiling cannot back is refused before building. A missing or directory entry is refused by name with status 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ONE-BINARY-1] `selka` is the one compiler binary (S5-D8): the separate `selka-core` executable and `zig build core` are gone, and the new core's own tests drive `selka`. A `selka build` that names one of the core's developer-only switches — `--sabotage-facts[=widening|length]`, `--generic-vectors`, `--trap-frames=MODE` — is answered by the core's own build command, as `selka-core build` answered it: its flag set (no `--run`), its source-path display and every build failure exit 1.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-RETIRED-1] The old back end's commands and flags are refused before anything runs, with status 2 and one line naming the retirement and what replaces it: `invoke`, `emit-qbe`, `emit-project-qbe`, `emit-runtime`, `dump-bytecode`, `compile-module`, `link-modules`, `native-test-graph`, `native-test-compile` and `bench-promote`; on any command, `--gc-runtime`, `--gc-stress`, `--rc-runtime`, `--legacy-sharing`, `--value-semantics`, `--library`, `--provider`, `--link-library`, `--library-path` and `--explain-ownership`; on `build` and `run`, `--backend`, `--llvm-opt`, `--lto`, `--target`, `--keep-symbols` and `--step-accounting`; on `test`, `--pure-skip`, `--diagnostics`, `--max-calls` and the `--bench*` flags (S5-D6). On `build`, `run` and `test`, `--explain-invalidation` (the old back end's stage fingerprints; `--report` says what the caches served, and on `test`, which refuses `--report`, `--json`) and `--timings=json` (the old phase profile) are refused as retired (`--friction-log` is accepted: see FRICTION-LOG-4), and on `test`, `--report` and `--timings`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CHECK-1] `selka check` of a project entry checks the merged program with the semantic cache, as the new core's front end does; it no longer reads or writes the old back end's per-module `compile-module` records, and its diagnostics are unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ARENA-1] `with arena():` is rejected with `E_ARENA_REMOVED`; use ordinary statements after removing the header and dedenting its body. The targeted error and certain repair remain available alongside independent semantic errors.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ARENA-2] Removed arena blocks have no replacement escape or confinement rules: migrating them to ordinary statements and unwrapping `name.escape(value)` preserves ordinary value semantics. Nested removals converge through `selka fix`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-ARENA-3] `E_ARENA_REMOVED` carries a structured fix, so `selka fix` migrates a file to a clean check: it deletes the `with arena() [as name]:` line (keeping a trailing `# comment` on it as a comment line at the block's indentation), dedents every non-blank body line, and the indented comment lines right after the body, by the body's one level, and rewrites each `name.escape(value)` of that block to `value` (to `(value)` unless `value` is a name, literal, call, field or index). A nested block is migrated in a later round. No fix is offered when the edit is not certain: a header not alone on its line, a tab in the indentation of the header or of a body line, or a triple-quoted string in the body. Body lines indented less than one level (comments, bracket continuations) are left as they are. The error and its fix are derived from syntax even when a body fails semantic checking, so a check that reuses cached bodies reports them exactly as a fresh one does. Disjoint, non-nested with scopes may reuse the arena name; nested same-name bindings remain ambiguous and receive no repair.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-HOST-1] `host def` is refused at check time (S5-D5): the declaration fails with `E_HOST_RETIRED` at its `host` word, saying host functions and their registered adapters were retired and to declare the function `extern def` and link its C implementation with `--link FILE.c`, so `check`, `run`, `build` and `test` stop there instead of a run failing with `HostMissingRegistration`. No `selka fix` is offered: the C side has to be written. `extern def` is unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-DOCTEST-1] `selka doctest` runs examples through the new `run`: a unit marked `legacy-sharing` or `gc-runtime` is skipped as documenting a retired mode, and a `value-semantics` unit runs without the retired switch, since value semantics is always on.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-NEGATIVE-INDEX-1] A list or fixed-array read or write with a computed negative index traps `R_LIST_BOUNDS` with the index the program wrote, in the text (`(index -5, length 2)`) and in the `trap_detail` record, in debug and release builds, as the old core reports it; an index past the end is reported unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-STREAM-WRITE-1] `stream.write(value)` answers what it wrote as `len` counts it, in the new core as in the old: the Unicode scalars of a `str`, the bytes of a `bytes` or `bytearray`; a write whose answer is discarded computes nothing more in a release build.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-EMBED-1] A header-less file `selka build`/`run` checks alone reads the files its `embed_bytes`/`embed_str` calls name, relative to its own directory and confined to its `selka.json` root, as the old CLI did; the build replays only while those files are unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-LABELS-1] The LLVM block that continues after a check is named from its block's own label and a fresh counter, not from the chain of labels before it, so a block with any number of checks (a thousand-term sum) builds in debug and release.
- [SELKA-START-1] `selka` starts with the allocator, I/O, arena and environment Zig's start code would give `main`, except that the Debug and ReleaseSafe allocator records no stack trace per allocation. It keeps Zig's leak, double-free and write-after-free checks. The first stack trace parsed and sorted the unwind tables of the whole executable: about 17 ms of every Debug `selka` start, two thirds of a cached `selka run`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-UNITS-1] The Zig unit tests build and run guest programs with the new core: `native_execute.run` writes a compilation's sources (one file, or a project's modules and the files they embed) into a content-addressed directory under the suite cache and runs `selka run --diagnostics=json --cache-report --print-result` on it with the run's budgets, grants, arguments, working directory and environment. The build's warnings end at the cache report and are not the program's stderr; a trap's record is restored to the runtime's text, frames and machine record with the directory removed from its paths, so a test sees the compilation's own paths; a program the front end accepts but the new core refuses fails the test with the refusal printed.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-UNITS-BATCH-1] A Zig unit test can run several guest programs as one batch: `native_execute.runAll`, or a `Batch` the test fills, checks every program and writes its sources in order, then runs the programs on up to `wiring.lanes()` threads and returns the runs in job order. The width is `SELKA_UNIT_JOBS`, else at most eight CPUs divided among the `SELKA_UNIT_SHARDS` shards `zig build test` runs at once, so a focused `test-units` run builds a test's programs in parallel while the gate's shards stay one program at a time. A rerun of a program under another budget shares its written sources, and a front-end error stops the batch before any program runs.
- [NATIVE-EXECUTE-CACHE-1] A Zig unit test's guest program builds and runs in a cache directory of its own, `build/IDENTITY` inside the suite cache, where IDENTITY is the content identity of the compilation's files (the name of its source tree), instead of one directory shared by every program of the suite. The build's stage stores index every record they hold when a process opens them, so a shared directory made each run cost more with every distinct program before it; the same files find the same directory and replay their last identical build.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-POLICY-1] An import the project's configured policy denies stops `selka build`, `run` and `test` with the policy's report and status 3, as the old CLI's policy gate did; a `selka.json` that does not load stops them with status 2, and other source failures keep status 1.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TEST-1] An exported `test_*` function whose result is neither `bool` nor a `Result` of one, and that no cases file gives an expected value, is a failed case (`test result type is not bool`; a property test's says `a property test returns bool or Result[bool, E]`) rather than an unsupported one, so the run exits 1, as the old runner reported it.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TEST-STATUS-1] `selka test` exits 3 when its selection is empty or every selected test was skipped by its cases file, as the old runner did: an all-skipped run is not a green run. An invalid cases file (`error: test cases: ...`: malformed JSON, an unknown test name or field) or an invalid provider fixture (`error: test providers: ...`, `InvalidTestProviders`, a fixture that does not declare) is a setup failure and exits 2, not 1; a directory run with any program that exits 2 exits 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-FANOUT-1] Without `--jobs` or `SELKA_JOBS`, `selka test` runs as many tests at once as there are CPUs, capped by how many per-test byte ceilings (each plus the 256 MiB address-space headroom) `MemAvailable` holds, at least one, as the old runner sized its pool; a directory run sizes its programs and the tests each runs from the same cap. An explicit width is not capped, and an unreadable `/proc/meminfo` leaves the CPU count.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TEST-USAGE-1] A `selka test` usage error says what is wrong on one `selka test:` line before the test usage, which lists every flag the runner takes: `invalid value 'V' for --FLAG` for a malformed or out-of-range value (`--jobs 0`, `--jobs=x`, `--property-runs 0`, `--seed x`, `--timeout-ms 0`, …), `unknown option '--X'`, `missing value for '--FLAG'`, `unexpected argument 'X'` for a second entry, and `--affected and --against REF go together`. The status stays 2.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TRACE-1] A trap lists at most its innermost sixteen frames, its site's line included, in a program and in a test, as the old core did; a test trial, which counts the frames it entered, then adds `  N frames omitted`. A budget's trap (`R_STEP_LIMIT`, `R_FRAME_LIMIT`, `R_MEMORY_BUDGET`) names no site (a budget is charged anywhere, and the last I/O call's site may be stale) and lists its frames from the innermost program frame; in a test it writes the trap record like any trap, so the runner reports it as `failed NAME: CODE`, the frames without the compiler's `__test_trial_*` wrapper, the omitted count and, after a blank line, the runtime's advice, instead of `NativeTestProcessFailed`.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-DEBUG-RELEASE-1] `selka build FILE.selka -o OUT --release` (or `--mode ReleaseFast`) drops every `debug(...)` line in lowering, its arguments unevaluated, as the old core's ReleaseFast build did (DEBUG-LINE-1); a debug build, `run`, `run-project`, `build --run` and `test` keep them in every mode. The choice is part of the build's replay key.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-AFFECTED-1] `selka test ENTRY --affected --against REF` runs only the tests, of those `--filter` selects, whose Core IR build reaches a declaration changed since the git revision `REF`, with the changes derived under the project root as AFFECTED-TEST-2 derives them: a function the build reaches is a changed function, a generic specialization of one, or a function of a module changed outside its functions and constants. A changed name of a module the program loads that no checked `def` carries (a constant, which the front end folds into its readers, or a deleted function) keeps every test, as does a test the new core cannot build; a change in a module the program does not load selects nothing. It prints `affected: N selected, M skipped against REF` and one `skipped NAME: no changed declaration is reachable` line per dropped test on stderr, and an empty selection exits 3. `--against` without `--affected`, or the reverse, is a usage error (status 2), and a `REF` git cannot resolve exits 2 saying `--against names a revision git cannot resolve`. Such a run neither replays nor records a whole run's report, while each selected test still replays its own receipt, and a directory run passes both switches to every program it runs.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-NO-CACHE-1] `--no-cache` on `build`, `build --run`, `run`, `run-project` and `test` builds through an empty private cache (`no-cache-PID` under the cache directory, or under a run's own directory), removed when the command ends: nothing is read from or published to the build replay, program, foreign-object, front-end or test-receipt caches. The per-user store of content-keyed runtime and unit objects (`SELKA_CORE_RUNTIME_CACHE`) is still used, as the old core's `--no-cache` kept its native object cache. Its status, output and diagnostics are those of the same command with caches.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-REPORT-1] `--report` on `build`, `build --run`, `run` and `run-project` prints the new core's cache report as one JSON line on stderr, in place of the retired `work-report-v1` and `qbe-build-v1` reports (S5-D6): `{"version":"cache-report-v1","program":P,"units":N,"reused":N,"compiled":N,"verified":N,"processes":N}`, the units linked, served by the object store, compiled and verified (`--verify-cache`) and the tool processes started, with `program` `linked`, `reused` (the program store served the executable) or `replayed` (the last identical build was replayed; every count is 0). `--cache-report` prints the same as one `selka-core: cache:` text line. Builds with stage measurements also include `stages` (`stage-report-v3`): frontend rejection categories, raw bodies materialized versus left unloaded, final optimized bodies loaded, intermediate checkpoint records decoded (`optimizer.restores.checkpoints`), complete LLVM keys constructed, LLVM bytes materialized into emission output buffers, deferred native handles, and inclusive stage times. `optimizer.restores.final` replaces the former flat `final_restores` count. `nanoseconds.frontend_phases` lists opt-in frontend phase names, nanosecond durations and inclusive markers; nested intervals are not additive. These distinguish body processing from compact metadata visits and actual native compilations.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-TIMINGS-1] `--timings` on `run`, `run-project` and `build --run` prints `timings: native_ns=N` on stderr once the program exits: the awake-clock nanoseconds from starting the program's process to its exit, the build excluded. It changes neither the status, the output nor a trap's report; given twice or on `build -o` it is a usage error.
- [NEW-CORE-S5-CONTRACT-1] A broken contract clause's trap names it as the old core did: `error[R_REQUIRES]: precondition violated: MESSAGE`, `R_ENSURES` `postcondition violated`, `R_INVARIANT` `invariant violated`; a failed `assert` keeps `native execution failed`. A broken precondition's first line also names the caller that broke it, `; broken by the caller FUNCTION at PATH:LINE:COLUMN`, the first of the frames listed below it.

- [FRONTEND-COST-1] `selka cost ENTRY.selka [FUNCTION] [--json]` reports static Core costs per reachable emitted function and their totals: kept/proved checks, in-place updates, potential dynamic allocation sites, direct destruction and stack lists, inlining and vector plans. Totals share the `build --stats` counters. Cost and `build --stats` collect LLVM optimization remarks from identical emitted units; LLVM vectorization is unknown without positive optimization remarks; `--llvm-remarks FILE.opt.yaml` reads successful LLVM loop-vectorization remarks separately from Core vector plans. Allocation classification includes runtime scratch, counts unresolved function values and foreign calls conservatively, and is shared with the build-time allocation promise. `--debug`, `--no-facts` and `--verify-facts` select the corresponding build pipeline.

- [FRONTEND-INTERFACE-COST-1] `selka list-interfaces ENTRY.selka --cost` preserves the default declaration listing and appends the same release per-function cost report and totals as `selka cost ENTRY.selka`. The option requires checked executable bodies, fails before printing a partial listing, and rejects duplicate `--cost` or combination with the JSON `--contracts` option. Ordinary listings remain unchanged. Main help lists the cost command and the optional interface costs.

## New core run-time counters

- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-1] `--counters` on `selka build`, `build --run` and `run` makes a counting build: the program prints, returns and traps exactly as the ordinary build does, and at exit — or when a trap ends it — writes one `selka-counters-v1` JSON document to the path in `SELKA_COUNTERS` (else `selka-counters.json` in its working directory). The document holds `totals` (calls, block allocations and frees with bytes, list growths, `realloc` growths and moves, list, dictionary, buffer and text copies with bytes, dictionary, buffer and text growths, out-of-line text appends, byte pushes and byte appends, peak and final live block bytes), per-kind site sums (`kinds`), one row per emitted function that did anything (`functions`, the runtime's events charged to the emitted function running when they happened) and one row per executed site (`sites`: emitting function, source function, path, line, column, kind, the Core operation that emitted it, count, bytes, extra). Site kinds count list, dictionary, buffer, text and large aggregate copies; list pushes; small aggregate element loads and stores; and bounds, overflow, division, shift and other checks. Copy sites carry bytes, list pushes also carry bytes moved by growth, and check sites carry execution counts. Reference-count and uniqueness fields and site kinds are absent.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-2] A counting build compiles the counting runtime: the ordinary runtime source, unchanged in every ordinary build, with `runtime/core_count.h` inserted after its includes, a hook inserted at each anchored place (allocation, free, list growth and copy, dictionary copy and growth, buffer copy, growth and push, text copy, growth and append, exit and trap exit), and `runtime/core_count.c` appended. An anchor that is missing or occurs twice fails the counting build with an error instead of leaving a path uncounted.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-3] Without `--counters` the emitted LLVM text, the runtime object and the executable are byte-identical to a build of the compiler without counting support. With it, every emitted function has a record that its prologue makes current and every return restores, every counted site is a record in section `myl_count_sites` kept by `@llvm.used`, list allocations and frees go through the runtime so they are counted, and the build links the counting runtime.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-4] `--counters` is accepted once per command line (a repeat is a usage error) and is part of the build's replay key, so a counting build never replays an ordinary build's executable or the reverse.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-5] `selka cost ENTRY.selka --counters FILE.json` prints the static cost report unchanged and then a counters section from one counting run's `selka-counters-v1` document: per reported function its calls, copies and bytes copied (records, lists, dictionaries, buffers, text), bytes moved by growth and managed allocations with bytes, then their total; with `--json` the same rows follow the static members as `counters`. A function the run never entered has a zero row. A missing or unreadable file, or a document that is not `selka-counters-v1`, exits 2; `--counters` given twice or without a file is a usage error.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-6] A counting build also counts every conditional trap the emitted code executes, one site per check: `bounds_check` (list, text and byte index checks, a pop's empty-list and index checks, substring bounds), `overflow_check` (integer arithmetic, negation and `MIN // -1`), `division_check` (a zero divisor), `shift_check` (a shift amount) and `other_check` (casts, float conversions, fact verification and every other conditional trap); `count` is the check's executions, and `kinds` sums them. A check the core's passes removed is never counted, a check LLVM removes later still is, and an unconditional trap is no check. The program's output, status and traps are those of the ordinary build, and without `--counters` the emitted text is unchanged.
- [NEW-CORE-COUNTERS-7] `selka cost ENTRY.selka --counters FILE.json` adds, per reported function and in the total, the checks it executed by kind (`checks: bounds N, overflow N, division N, shift N, other N` at the end of each text line, a `checks` object with `bounds`, `overflow`, `division`, `shift` and `other` beside each JSON row's `run`).

## New core copy elimination: lists

- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-1] A store into a field of a list element (`xs[i].f = v`, `xs[i].a.b = v`), which the builder lowers as a read of the whole element, a record update and a write of the whole element, becomes one `list_set_field` when nothing else uses the element read or the intermediate records and only instructions that cannot trap, write or have an effect lie between the read and the write: checked ownership updates the sole owner in place and directly destroys replaced managed storage, the value is stored at the field's offset inside the element, and the field it replaces is destroyed after the store; the rest of the element is never read or copied. The store traps where the element read would have, with the same index, length and source position. An element read nothing uses whose bounds check the next access of the same element repeats, with only such instructions between, is dropped, the access taking its position; any other unused element read emits only its bounds check. An independent value needed after the store is made with explicit `.copy()` in checked ownership.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-2] A whole-element store (`xs[i] = v`) of a managed element reads the old element's managed parts where they are — a record, array or union over four words part by part (a union's parts for every managed case, each released only under the case its tag names), anything of at most four words as a value — stores the new value, and then releases those parts, instead of copying the whole old element out first. A field store (NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-1) releases the field it replaces the same way.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-3] Mutating an owned field does not copy its containing list. An explicit copy made for a writeback temporary remains an independent source-level copy; lowering adds no intermediate container copies to subsequent field stores.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-4] A managed part needed across mutation of its owner requires an explicit independent `.copy()`; an invalidated borrow is refused. A selected object or wide record copy duplicates its owned children without copying the containing object table. Cleanup directly destroys those independent children, and the structural oracle distinguishes source-required copies from added container copies.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-5] A slice used only as the source of a counted loop (`for b in xs[i:j]`) is never built when the builder's endpoint clamps prove both endpoints within `[0, len(xs)]` and nothing between the slice and the loop, or in the loop, updates, stores, returns, joins or passes `xs` to a call: the loop counts from the clamped start while below the clamped end and reads `xs` at that index, so it reads exactly the elements the slice held, in bounds, and `break`, `continue` and `return` leave it as before. A slice that is still built (one kept past the loop, measured, or whose source the loop can change) is allocated with room for exactly `max(end - start, 0)` elements, so filling it never grows it.
- [NEW-CORE-CE-LISTS-6] `buffer.append_range(data, start, end)` and `append_utf8(text)` compile to the range test's comparisons (`0 <= start <= end <= len(data)`, the method's result) and a `buffer_append` update: checked ownership updates the sole buffer owner directly, and the emitted code copies the octets in place and raises the length when the range is non-empty, valid and fits the buffer's room, calling the runtime's checked append only to grow the buffer or to append nothing. The appended octets, the result, the growth policy and a trap while growing (reported at the append) are those of the runtime call.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-1] `effects.analyze` summarizes every function's effect on each parameter as two sets of field paths (record field chains from the parameter; the empty path is the whole value): the paths the function may read, whose values its result, its other parameters' final values, its traps or its output may depend on, and, for a `mut` parameter, the paths its final value may differ in from the value passed. Summaries are transitive over direct calls and a least fixed point over recursion and mutual recursion; indirect, `extern` and runtime calls read and write everything they are given. A function whose declared result is a `bool` beside `mut` parameters also gets the writes of the returns that may answer `True` and of those that may answer `False`, and a function that returns a call's whole result answers as that call does.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-2] Memory homes interfere per field: a record update writes its changed field, and a mutable call result written back into its argument home writes the fields identified by the callee effect summary. Accepted owned programs preserve nested fields, mutable writeback results and trap behavior through calls and loops, with no block live at exit. A live borrow still forbids mutation of its owner.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-3] Before ownership, a whole-element store `list_set(xs, k, v)` whose element nothing can read before a later store of the same element overwrites it is removed: its list is followed forward through the records it is put back into, phis (the element's index crossing with the record that holds it) and calls whose effect summary neither reads it nor, for a normalized negative index, may change the list's length, until a store of the same element ends it on every path. An element read, a return, a call that may read the list, an indirect, `extern` or runtime call and anything else that lets the list escape keep the store; a trap does not, since it ends the process (test trials are forked). When only some successors of the store's block observe it, the store and its write-backs move to those successors, reading the record field again there so ownership takes it over, and the others go without. The removed store's bounds check stays at its place as an element read nothing uses, so an index past the list traps where it did. A dispatch loop that stores its frame at the end of a step and again after the next step's first read keeps the end-of-step store only on its return path.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-4] A whole-element store `list_set(xs, k, v)` of a record `v` that was read from element `k` of the same list, or last stored there, stores only the fields that may have changed since, one `list_set_field` each (none: the bounds check alone), with no whole-element copy and no ownership transfer or destruction of unchanged parts. The proof walks `v` back through record updates (a nested update counts as its inner field) and `mut` calls (their effect summaries) to that read or store, and the element back through the list's stores, records and calls that do not write it; at a phi it takes each predecessor in turn with the branch conditions that lead to that edge, so a call answering `True` contributes only the writes of its returns that may answer `True`. An element index is its integer before normalization from the end, from a field of a record or a value, plus a constant; a normalized index names the same element only while the list's length cannot have changed. Lox's dispatch loop stores only `frame.ip` per instruction; a list that grows or is replaced, an error path that resets the frame count, and an indirect read keep the whole store where the proof fails.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-5] A field write-back preserves updates to sibling fields and the field’s owned contents. When the ownership proof transfers a field through intervening sibling updates, the destination is updated without a whole-record copy; no borrowed alias may require the old field after transfer.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-EFFECTS-6] A record or union construction used once by a later whole-element store may be built directly in the destination after bounds checks. Operand storage remains live until construction, replaced managed fields are directly destroyed, and explicit independent copies retain their values.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-1] A value of more than four words that a `ret` stores inside the returned value — a field or `Ok`/`Err` payload of a construction returned through `sret`, the declared result next to `mut` values, or the value a view of it lies in — is built at that place in the caller's destination `%ret` (`emit_homes.zig`), so `return Ok(token)`, `return Ok(make_token(...))` and `r = next_in(src, mut cur); ...; return r` store nothing: the place must fit inside `%ret` (bytes of in-out final fields, which the caller reads where it passed them, may be used), share no byte with another value in `%ret` that is live when it is written, is read by its defining instruction or meets it on a phi's edge, and no store of any `ret` may overwrite it before that `ret` reads it.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-2] A `ret` whose stores into `%ret` would overwrite a value that lives in `%ret` (built there for another return) before reading it — `return Pair(x=w.y, y=w.x)` where `w` is returned whole on another path — builds its value in a temporary and copies it to `%ret`, so the swapped halves are the old ones.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-3] Results built in `%ret` behave exactly as copies would: early returns of other shapes, `Err` paths of a `?`-style match and of `?` itself (`first = next(mut tok)?` then `return Ok(first)`), a callee's payload returned under another error type, snapshots of a `mut` cursor, swaps and duplicated halves next to a `mut` record, values rebuilt while an earlier one is read, results carried around loops, and a trap between building a result in `%ret` and returning it print the same at debug, release and `--verify-facts`, with no block live at exit.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-4] A list element of more than four words read with `list_get` or `list_get_field` may be lent from its slot when every use follows it in the same block and is a readonly memory argument to an internal call or a register-sized field, payload or tag read. Intervening instructions and calls must preserve list storage; a call with an owning or consuming managed parameter prevents lending. `is_free(objs[i])` can therefore pass the element itself. For a scalar-only aggregate such as the five-integer `Big` record in the regression, `grow(mut h, h.items[0])` and a read whose slot is overwritten before its use preserve the earlier value with a temporary copy. Overlapping borrowed managed arguments are rejected; for copyable values, stage an independent `.copy()` before the mutating call.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-5] Sharing a by-value argument's memory with the callee's `sret` destination (`Planner.shareable`: an internal function with no in-out parameter, nothing living in `%ret` before a `ret`, and no `ret` that reads a byte of the parameter after one of its own stores wrote it) is off (`emit_homes.steps.share_sret = false`): such a callee would be defined with neither the parameter nor `%ret` `noalias`, and the rows' `record_pass` (`s = advance(s, x)` over a twelve-field record) ran 15-19% slower than the one copy per call it saves. Every function keeps `noalias` on `%ret` and its parameters, and a loop's `s = advance(s, x)` copies the call's result once. R3's sole-pointer noalias prototype likewise regressed record_pass instructions (1.207x) and is retained only in experiment history.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-6] An explicit field copy may be mutated while calls read the original record before write-back. Argument evaluation order is preserved, reads before write-back see the original field, and publishing the copied field preserves its updated contents.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-RESULTS-7] A container update of a list element's block-holding field can take that field with a transferred `list_get_field` and publish its returned owner with `list_set_field`, without copying the whole element or its untouched fields. The read retains the element bounds check and source position. The checked ownership proof must establish the transfer; explicit independent copies retain their own values, and conflicting live borrows are rejected.
- [NEW-CORE-CE2-MERGE-1] Per-field interference narrows what a call writes in an argument's home to the callee's write summary only for a `mut` (owned) parameter. A by-value argument that shares the call's `sret` result home (`s = rotate(s, j)`) is overwritten whole by the result, so every value still reading that home — the argument itself, a copy kept from it — keeps its own home.

## New core runtime parity: aliasing and overflow facts (F1)

- [NEW-CORE-F1-TBAA-1] A release or debug build tags each scalar a list operation loads or stores in a list's element data (`list_get`, `list_set`, `list_get_field`, `list_set_field`, `list_push`, and the old element a store reads to release it) with a TBAA type of its LLVM storage type — `myl.data.double`, `.i64`, `.i32`, `.i16`, `.i8` (bytes and booleans) or `.ptr` — each a child of `myl.data`. A union's tag word and payload (any element, field or path that passes through a union), whole-value copies (`llvm.memcpy`, untagged) and every access that is not a list element (homes, boxes, return slots, dictionary entries, stack slots) keep the generic `myl.data`, which aliases every child. A list's element type is fixed for its block's lifetime, so the bytes at one offset of an element are only ever accessed as one scalar type or generically; answers are unchanged in every mode.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-TBAA-2] The inline free of a small block (`myl_core_free`) starts its free-list push with a compiler barrier (`asm sideeffect "", "~{memory}"`): a freed block may come back from the inline allocator as a list of another element type in the same function, and no load or store may move across the free. Frees and allocations that call the runtime are opaque calls and order every access already.
- [NEW-CORE-F1-STOREBACK-1] A whole-element store `list_set(xs, k, v)`, or a `list_set_field` of a block-pointer field reached without crossing a union, that puts back a block pointer a transferred read of the same element (same list value, index and field) took out — `c[i][j] = v` stores row `c[i]` back — follows `v` to that read through in-place updates (`list_set`, `list_set_field`, `list_pop`), copies, growth and replacements of the block and phis (a loop that carries it included). When every path is an in-place update, the slot already holds `v`'s pointer and nothing is stored; otherwise the store runs only when `v`'s pointer differs from the read's, which is when an explicit copy, growth or a replacement moved it.
